\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phantom{+}\frac{6{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(a)}{a^{2}}+\frac{8\pi G\rho(a)}{a}-4\pi G\rho^{\prime}(a)\;,

(2.34)

where
{\cal M}:={\cal M}(a)
is the total mass of the fluid body, which remains constant. Higher-order derivatives of the potential
U
at the surface
r=a
can be computed by using Eq. (
2.30
). Notably, the higher-order surface derivatives for
n\geq 3
are independent of the parameter
\mathsf{m}
– only the surface values of the potential
U
, its first derivative
U^{\prime}
, and second derivative
U^{\prime\prime}
exhibit explicit dependence on
\mathsf{m}
.

To characterize the fluid’s response to rotational perturbations, we introduce a variable on the base manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, analogous to Eq. (
2.5
), but defined in terms of the auxiliary density
\sigma
of the reference configuration. This variable is denoted by:

\displaystyle{\upbeta}(r):=\frac{\sigma(r)}{\bar{\sigma}(r)}\;,

(2.35)

where the average value of
\sigma
over the volume of radius
r
is given by:

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}(r)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{r^{3}}\int_{0}^{r}\sigma(s)s^{2}ds=\bar{\rho}(r)-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\bar{\rho}(a)\;.

(2.36)

The first derivative
U^{\prime}(r)
of the effective gravitational potential
U
, the auxiliary density
\sigma(r)
, and the variable
\upbeta(r)
are related through the identity:

\displaystyle 4\pi G\frac{\sigma}{U^{\prime}}=-\frac{3\upbeta}{r}\;,

(2.37)

which mirrors the structure of Eq. (
2.7
).
Furthermore, the variables
\upalpha
, defined in Eq. (
2.5
), and
\upbeta(r)
are interconnected via the series expansion:

\displaystyle\upbeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\upalpha+(\upalpha-1)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\frac{\bar{\rho}(a)}{\bar{\rho}(r)}\right]^{n}\;.

(2.38)

The variable
\upbeta
plays a dual role in the analysis of rotational deformations of fluid bodies. It serves both as a physical parameter quantifying the fluid’s response to rotational perturbations and as a mathematical construct that simplifies the governing equations. Unlike earlier formulations that relied on the parameter
\upalpha
, which required explicit retention of all terms in the expansion series (as shown in Eq. (
2.38
)), the use of
\upbeta
significantly reduces algebraic complexity and enhances the tractability of higher-order perturbative calculations.

2.4
Mathematical Formulation of Hydrostatic Equilibrium in the Reference Configuration

The reference configuration of a rotating fluid plays a central role in the computation of rotational deformations via perturbation theory. The defining parameters of this configuration – such as the radius, internal density distribution, and gravitational field – are fundamentally governed by the fluid’s equation of state, which dictates the balance between pressure and gravity under hydrostatic equilibrium. In general, the equation of state expresses pressure as a function of density
\rho
, temperature
T
, and other thermodynamic variables
[
71
]
. However, in this analysis, we restrict our attention to a barotropic equation of state, wherein pressure depends solely on density, i.e.,
p=p(\rho)
. This assumption is consistent with the condition of rigid rotation in the fluid
[
13
]
.

We further assume that the fluid density
\rho
in the reference configuration is inhomogeneous and varies only with the radial coordinate, such that
\rho:=\rho(r)
. Given the barotropic assumption, the pressure likewise becomes a function of the radial coordinate alone,
p=p(r)
. The density is permitted to vary continuously throughout the fluid interior and is assumed to possess non-zero derivatives at the surface, reflecting a physically realistic boundary behavior consistent with the equation of state. The unperturbed reference state is characterized by an isotropic stress tensor,

\displaystyle t_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-p\delta_{ij}\;,

(2.39)

where
\delta_{ij}={\rm diag}(1,1,1)
denotes the identity matrix and
p
is the pressure.

The fluid in the reference configuration satisfies the equation of hydrostatic equilibrium
[
2
]
:

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{i}U+\partial_{j}t^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(2.40)

where
U
denotes the effective gravitational potential, as defined in Eqs. (
2.21
)–(
2.25
). Since all quantities in Eq. (
2.40
) depend solely on the radial coordinate
r
, the equation simplifies to:

\displaystyle\rho U^{\prime}-p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(2.41)

where, here and throughout the paper, a prime denotes differentiation with respect to
r
, e.g.,
U^{\prime}=dU/dr
.

For a fluid obeying a polytropic equation of state,
p=\mathsf{K}_{0}\rho^{1+1/n}
, where
\mathsf{K}_{0}
and
n
(the polytropic index) are constants, Eq. (
2.41
) is typically reformulated into the Lane-Emden equation
[
72
]
. This is achieved by introducing a dimensionless function
\Theta=\Theta(r)
such that
\rho=\rho_{0}\Theta^{n}
, where
\rho_{0}
is the central density. Substituting this into the polytropic equation of state yields:

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{0}\Theta^{n+1}\;,

(2.42)

with
p_{0}=\rho_{0}\mathsf{K}_{0}
. Combining Eqs. (
2.23
), (
2.41
), and (
2.42
) leads to a second-order ordinary differential equation for
\Theta
, known as the Lane-Emden equation
[
13
,
14
]
. This equation is solved subject to the boundary conditions
\Theta(0)=1
and
\Theta^{\prime}(0)=0
. An additional boundary condition,
\Theta(a)=0
, ensures that the density vanishes at the surface
r=a
, thereby defining the radius of the fluid body. The resulting solutions for
\Theta
provide the radial profiles of pressure and density and are referred to as polytropes of index
n

[
13
,
14
]
.

In the subsequent analysis, we assume that the solution to the hydrostatic equilibrium equations for the reference configuration – describing the radial profiles
\rho=\rho(r)
,
p=p(r)
, and
U=U(r)
– is known exactly, either through analytical expressions or high-precision numerical integration. Accordingly, all required radial derivatives of these functions, which are essential for the perturbative treatment developed in later sections, are also assumed to be accurately determined.

3
Linear Perturbations of the Base Manifold

A distinctive feature of this study is the application of the formalism of the Lie group of fluid diffeomorphisms to develop a nonlinear extension of Clairaut’s theory of the figure for rotating fluid bodies. The Lie group of fluid diffeomorphisms acts on the base manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, which represents the reference configuration of the fluid. Rotation induces displacements of fluid elements from their positions in this reference configuration, resulting in a deformation of the base manifold. These diffeomorphisms encapsulate both the magnitude and direction of the deformation, making them a powerful tool for analyzing changes in the shape of the rotating body through Lie group techniques. This approach, inspired by the foundational ideas of V. Arnold
[
73
,
74
]
, is systematically developed in the subsequent sections of the paper.

3.1
Fluid Diffeomorphisms and Their Lie Algebra

We consider a stationary, rotating fluid body described in a rigidly rotating Cartesian coordinate system
{\bm{x}}=(x^{i})
that is comoving with the fluid. In this frame, the fluid velocity vanishes. The unperturbed state of the fluid is defined by incorporating the radial component (
2.15
) of the centrifugal potential
W_{R}
into the reference configuration, which occupies a spherically symmetric volume
\mathcal{V}
of radius
a
. The union of all interior and boundary points of this volume constitutes the base manifold
\mathfrak{M}
:
{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{V}\cup\partial\mathcal{V}
.

The non-radial, quadrupole component (
2.16
) of the centrifugal potential
W_{Q}
induces a physical deformation of the volume
\mathcal{V}
, transforming it into a spheroidal configuration. This chapter focuses on the regime of slow rotation, where the deformation remains small and can be treated within the linearized framework of Clairaut’s theory, which neglects nonlinear effects. The perturbations considered here arise from the stationary rotation of an astrophysical fluid body (e.g., a gaseous planet or star) and are therefore time-independent. Consequently, all functions in this and subsequent sections depend solely on the spatial coordinates
{\bm{x}}
, and time derivatives are excluded from the analysis.

The perturbing potential
W_{Q}
displaces each fluid element (or fluid parcel
[
75
]
) from its equilibrium position
{\bm{x}}=x^{i}
to a perturbed position described by

\displaystyle w^{i}=x^{i}+\xi^{i}\;,

(3.1)

where
x^{i}
are the Lagrangian coordinates of the fluid parcel
[
76
]
,
w^{i}
are the corresponding Eulerian coordinates, and
\xi^{i}=\xi^{i}({\bm{x}})
is the infinitesimal Lagrangian displacement vector – also referred to as an infinitesimal fluid diffeomorphism
[
24
,
13
]
. The displacement magnitude is proportional to the perturbation
W_{Q}
, i.e.,
|{\bm{\xi}}|\sim\mathsf{m}
, where
\mathsf{m}
is the rotational parameter defined in (
2.14
). The precise magnitude and direction of the displacement vector
{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi^{i})
are not known a priori and must be determined by solving the governing differential equations, which will be addressed in later sections. The collection of all such vectors
{\bm{\xi}}
at each point
{\bm{x}}\in\mathfrak{M}
forms the tangent space
T_{{\bm{x}}}\mathfrak{M}
to the base manifold.

This tangent space can be endowed with the structure of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
of infinitesimal diffeomorphisms, which provides a geometric framework for analyzing perturbations in the fluid and gravitational fields. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
is defined by equipping the tangent space with a Lie bracket
[{\bm{\xi}},{\bm{\eta}}]
for any two vector fields
{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{\eta}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
, given by the Lie derivative:
[{\bm{\xi}},{\bm{\eta}}]:={\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\eta}}=-{\mathcal{L}}_{\bm{\eta}}{\bm{\xi}}
. This bracket satisfies the Jacobi identity, a fundamental property of Lie algebras
[
73
]
. The collection of all tangent spaces
T_{\bm{x}}\mathfrak{M}
forms the tangent (vector) bundle
T\mathfrak{M}
.

The corresponding collection of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
over the manifold defines a (tangent) Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}

[
77
]
. The Lie bracket structure on
\mathfrak{M}
is preserved via the anchor map, which, in the case of the tangent algebroid, is defined by the Leibniz rule
[
77
]
:

\displaystyle[{\bm{\xi}},f{\bm{\eta}}]

\displaystyle={\bm{\xi}}(f){\bm{\eta}}+f[{\bm{\xi}},{\bm{\eta}}]\;,

(3.2)

where
f\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
is a smooth scalar function, and
{\bm{\xi}}(f)=\xi^{i}\partial_{i}f
denotes the action of the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
on
f
.

3.2
Level Surfaces: Key to the Geometry of Rotating Fluid Bodies

In a rotating fluid body, three distinct types of three-dimensional surfaces are commonly recognized. Surfaces on which the total gravitational potential
V
remains constant are termed equipotential or level surfaces. Surfaces of constant fluid density
\rho
are referred to as
isopycnal
surfaces, while those of constant pressure
p
are known as
isobaric
surfaces. It is well established that, under hydrostatic equilibrium and assuming the angular velocity of the fluid’s steady rotation is independent of the vertical coordinate
z
, these three surfaces coincide. In such a configuration, each surface simultaneously maintains constant values of gravitational potential, density, and pressure
[
13
]
. For brevity, we shall refer to these coinciding surfaces collectively as the
level surface
.

Level surfaces are fundamental to the analysis of both the internal structure and the overall shape of a rotating fluid body
[
2
,
78
]
. The collection of these surfaces exhibits several important geometric and physical characteristics that are essential for understanding the equilibrium configuration of such bodies
[
1
]
:

Continuity
– Level surfaces are continuous and free of any breaks or discontinuities.

Convexity
-- Each level surface is convex at every point
1
1

1

This condition may be violated in configurations involving rapid rotation. In the present analysis, we assume that the angular velocity is sufficiently small to preserve the convexity of the level surfaces.
. That is, in a neighborhood of any point on the surface, the local curvature is non-negative, implying that the surface either curves outward or remains locally planar.

Non-intersection
– Level surfaces do not intersect and are nested concentrically, resembling the pattern of tree rings in a cross-section of a trunk.

Smooth curvature
– The local curvature of the surfaces varies smoothly, except possibly at the outer boundary where abrupt changes in matter density may occur.

Centering
– The geometric center of the entire family of level surfaces coincides with the center of mass of the fluid body.

The boundary of a fluid body is defined by the condition of vanishing pressure:
p({\bm{x}})\left|{}_{\rm surface}\right.=0
. In fluid bodies lacking a boundary layer, this condition also implies that the surface density vanishes
[
13
]
:
\rho({\bm{x}})\left|{}_{\rm surface}\right.=0
. For a fluid in hydrostatic equilibrium, the surfaces of constant pressure and density coincide with equipotential surfaces of the gravitational field. As a result, the boundary of the fluid body also corresponds to an equipotential surface. Therefore, the shape of a fluid body in hydrostatic equilibrium is governed by the structure of the gravitational field’s level surfaces within the interior, along with the boundary conditions imposed on the gravitational potential and its first derivatives at the surface of the rotating body
[
2
]
.

The central objective of the theory of hydrostatic perturbations in rotating fluid bodies is to establish the governing principles and equations that describe the geometry of perturbed level surfaces within the body’s interior. These perturbations arise in response to the centrifugal force and depend on the fluid density
\rho
, the equation of state
p=p(\rho)
, and the angular velocity of rotation
\omega
.
Before delving into the details of this perturbation theory, it is essential to clarify the relationship between fluid diffeomorphisms –representing the displacement of individual fluid parcels – and the diffeomorphisms associated with the deformation of level surfaces induced by rotation. In the reference configuration of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, each level surface comprises fluid parcels sharing identical values of density, pressure, and gravitational potential. The quadrupole component of the centrifugal potential,
W_{Q}
, displaces a fluid parcel from its original position
{\bm{x}}
to a new location
{\bm{w}}
, as described by Eq. (
3.1
). However, the surface formed by the new positions of these displaced parcels may not constitute a level surface, since the density may vary differently in different radial directions. This implies that the fluid diffeomorphism
\xi^{i}
alone cannot, in general, capture the deformation of level surfaces in a compressible fluid.

To address this issue, we introduce a new infinitesimal (geometric) diffeomorphism, denoted by
\zeta^{i}=\zeta^{i}({\bm{x}})
, which represents the displacement of a level surface element initially located at the point
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
to a new position:

\displaystyle y^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{i}+\zeta^{i}\;.

(3.3)

In the linearized framework, this expression characterizes the geometry of the perturbed level surfaces, thereby describing the deformation of the fluid body relative to the spherical level surfaces in the reference configuration. The alteration in the geometry of a level surface is captured by the vector field
\zeta^{i}({\bm{x}})
, whose magnitude is proportional to the rotational parameter
\mathsf{m}
.

As will be demonstrated below, the fluid diffeomorphism
\xi^{i}
is related to the geometric diffeomorphism
\zeta^{i}
via a first-order partial differential equation – see Eq. (
3.45
). Importantly, the identity
\zeta^{i}=\xi^{i}
holds only in the special case of an incompressible fluid, where the displacement field is divergence-free, i.e.,
\theta:=\partial_{i}\xi^{i}=0
. Both types of diffeomorphisms play fundamental roles in perturbation theory and will be employed as appropriate, depending on the specific characteristics of the problem under investigation.

3.3
Hydrostatic Perturbations

The external perturbation
W_{Q}
induces variations in all relevant physical quantities, including the density, pressure, and gravitational potential of the body. These variations can be categorized into two types, corresponding to the fluid diffeomorphism (
3.1
).

The
Lagrangian
perturbation of the density, denoted
\Delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, characterizes the change in density experienced by a fluid element as it is displaced from its original position
\xi^{i}
to a new position
w^{i}=x^{i}+\xi^{i}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{w}})-\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(3.4)

where
\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{w}})
and
\rho({\bm{x}})
represent the perturbed and unperturbed densities of the same fluid element, evaluated at positions
{\bm{w}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{x}}
, respectively. In contrast, the
Eulerian
perturbation,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, describes the change in density at a fixed point on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})-\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(3.5)

where both
\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})
and
\rho({\bm{x}})
are evaluated at the same spatial location
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
. The underlying principle of both Lagrangian and Eulerian perturbations is that the variation in density, governed by the infinitesimal displacement vector
{\bm{\xi}}
, must satisfy the fluid continuity equation. This equation can be expressed in either Lagrangian coordinates
x^{i}
, which track the position of a fluid parcel in the unperturbed configuration, or Eulerian coordinates
w^{i}
, which describe the perturbed configuration of the base manifold
[
13
]
. In the subsequent analysis, we adopt the Lagrangian coordinate framework
x^{i}
for our mathematical formulation. This choice aligns with the methodology of Lie groups and offers distinct advantages in the treatment of nonlinear perturbations, which will be discussed in detail in the following sections.

We consider a barotropic fluid, in which the pressure
p
depends solely on the density
\rho
via an equation of state of the form
p=p(\rho)
. By applying the chain rule to this functional relationship, the infinitesimal Eulerian variation of pressure is given by:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}p

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})-p({\bm{x}})=\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(3.6)

where
p^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}):=p\left[\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})\right]
denotes the perturbed pressure evaluated at the same spatial point
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
in the fluid’s reference configuration, and
p({\bm{x}}):=p\left[\rho({\bm{x}})\right]
is the corresponding unperturbed value.

The infinitesimal variations of density and pressure are closely associated with the Lie derivative
[
79
,
80
]
along the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
, and are expressed as:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}p

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(3.8)

where
\partial_{i}:=\partial/\partial x^{i}
denotes partial differentiation. Here, the Lie derivative of the fluid density is defined as
[
80
]
:

\displaystyle\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\partial_{i}\left(\xi^{i}\rho\right)=-\xi^{i}\partial_{i}\rho-\rho\partial_{i}\xi^{i}\;.

(3.9)

The expression for the Lie derivative of the fluid density reflects the fact that
\rho
is not a conventional scalar field, as it incorporates the infinitesimal volume element of a fluid parcel, which depends on the divergence of the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
, defined as
\theta:=\partial_{i}\xi^{i}
. Consequently, as the parcel is advected along
{\bm{\xi}}
, both its particle content and volume vary. The Lie derivative of
\rho
thus captures both the directional derivative of the density and the divergence of
{\bm{\xi}}
. This formulation is consistent with the fluid continuity equation
[
24
,
13
]
. Notably, the present analysis assumes a compressible fluid, implying
\theta\neq 0
.

The variation of pressure,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}p
, can be reformulated by introducing the adiabatic bulk modulus
[
81
]
, defined as:

\displaystyle\uplambda

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\rho\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\right)_{S}\;,

(3.10)

where the partial derivative
\left(\partial p/\partial\rho\right)_{S}
is evaluated at constant entropy
S
. Since we consider a stationary rotational configuration without heat exchange, the entropy remains constant throughout the fluid. Consequently, the subscript
S
may be omitted in all subsequent thermodynamic derivatives.
Utilizing Eqs. (
3.7
) and (
3.10
), the Eulerian variation of pressure becomes:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}p=-\xi^{i}\partial_{i}p-\uplambda\,\partial_{i}\xi^{i}\;.

(3.11)

The deformation of the stress tensor (
2.39
), in the most general case, receives contributions from variations in pressure
p
, the metric tensor
\delta_{ij}
, and changes in the thermodynamic state of matter due to the displacement of material elements from their equilibrium positions
[
81
]
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}t_{ij}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle t^{\dagger}_{ij}({\bm{x}})-t_{ij}({\bm{x}})=\left(-\xi^{p}\partial_{k}p-\uplambda u_{kk}\right)\delta_{ij}-2(\upmu-p)u_{ij}\;,

(3.12)

where
\upmu
is the shear modulus, and the strain tensor is defined as

\displaystyle u_{ij}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{i}\xi_{j}+\partial_{j}\xi_{i}\right)\;.

(3.13)

Here,
\uplambda
denotes the bulk modulus, characterizing resistance to compression, while
\upmu
quantifies resistance to shear deformation. In the general theory of elasticity
[
81
,
82
]
, the strain tensor may include nonlinear terms quadratic in the derivatives of the displacement vector
\xi^{i}
. However, within the linearized regime, such terms are neglected. This linearization effectively combines the contributions of
p
and
\upmu
in the stress tensor, leading to the definition of an
effective shear modulus
:

\displaystyle\upmu_{\rm eff}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\upmu-p\;,

(3.14)

which vanishes in the case of a static fluid, i.e.,
\upmu_{\rm eff}=0
. Consequently, for a rigidly rotating fluid, the deformation of the stress tensor (
2.39
) reduces to a purely volumetric (bulk) response:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}t_{ij}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(-\xi^{p}\partial_{k}p-\uplambda u_{kk}\right)\delta_{ij}=\delta_{\bm{\xi}}p\,\delta_{ij}\;,

(3.15)

which is directly proportional to the Lie derivative of the fluid pressure.

It is important to emphasize that the variation of the stress tensor in a solid body or a viscous fluid does not, in general, coincide with the Lie derivative of the unperturbed stress tensor, as expressed in Eq. (
3.15
). This discrepancy arises because, in these more general media, the stress tensor depends explicitly on the effective shear modulus (
\upmu_{\rm eff}\neq 0
) or on the viscosity coefficient
[
81
,
13
]
. While this issue is absent in the case of an ideal (inviscid) fluid, it can manifest in viscous fluids, particularly under differential rotation.

However, when a viscous fluid undergoes rigid-body rotation, the viscosity-dependent terms vanish, and the variation of the stress tensor remains consistent with Eq. (
3.15
)
[
13
]
. Therefore, the formalism developed here is applicable not only to ideal fluids but also to viscous fluids, provided the rotation is rigid. In such cases, contributions from viscosity and effective shear modulus are absent, regardless of the fluid’s internal constitution.

3.4
Gravitational Perturbations

The unperturbed gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}:={\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}})
of the fluid body satisfies the Poisson equation (
2.1
), whose solution is given by Eq. (
2.2
) as an integral over the volume
{\mathcal{V}}
of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
. In the perturbed configuration, the gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}:={\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})
satisfies the modified Poisson equation:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}=-4\pi G\rho^{\dagger}\;,

(3.16)

where
\rho^{\dagger}
denotes the perturbed density of the fluid, now occupying a deformed volume
{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}
due to the influence of the quadrupole component of the centrifugal potential
W_{Q}
. A particular solution to Eq. (
3.16
) is given by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}}\frac{\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{y}}^{\prime})d^{3}y^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}|}\;,

(3.17)

where the integration is performed over coordinates
{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Although the perturbed potential
{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}
is evaluated at the same spatial point
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
as the unperturbed potential
{\mathcal{U}}
, a direct comparison between Eqs. (
2.2
) and (
3.17
) is not immediately possible due to the difference in integration domains. To facilitate this comparison, the integration volume
{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}
in Eq. (
3.17
) must be mapped back to the unperturbed spherical volume
{\mathcal{V}}
of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
using the pullback diffeomorphism.

Recall that a barotropic, rotating fluid in hydrostatic equilibrium occupies a volume whose boundary coincides with a level surface of constant density, pressure, and gravitational potential
[
1
,
13
]
. In the presence of rotational perturbations, the level surfaces of the gravitational potential are described by the position vector
{\bm{y}}=(y^{i})
, defined by Eq. (
3.3
) as
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}}
, where
{\bm{x}}
denotes a point on the unperturbed level surface, and
{\bm{\zeta}}=(\zeta^{i})
is an infinitesimal displacement vector belonging to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
. The vector
{\bm{\zeta}}
can be determined, for example, by requiring that the total potential remains constant on the perturbed surface:
U^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}})+W_{Q}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}})={\rm const}
.

This approach is employed in the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory of level surfaces in rotating bodies
[
2
]
, which assumes that
{\bm{\zeta}}
has only a radial component, i.e.,
{\bm{\zeta}}=\zeta{\bm{n}}
, where
{\bm{n}}
is a unit radial vector and
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
is a scalar function defined on
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
. This assumption arises from interpreting Eq. (
3.3
) in spherical coordinates
(r,\theta,\varphi)
as an infinitesimal transformation of a level surface from a sphere of radius
r
to a more general surface
R=r+\zeta(r,\theta,\varphi)
, clearly indicating that
{\bm{\zeta}}
is purely radial. In contrast, we adopt a more general method for computing the components of the deformation vector
\zeta^{i}
, which is outlined in detail in Section
3.7
.

At this stage, it is useful to recall that the vector field
{\bm{\zeta}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
introduced in Eq. (
3.3
) is distinct from the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
defined in Eq. (
3.1
). The vector
{\bm{\xi}}
represents the displacement of a fluid parcel, described by the transformation
{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{w}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{\xi}}
, whereas
{\bm{\zeta}}
characterizes the displacement of a point on a level surface, given by
{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}}
. By construction, the displacement
{\bm{\xi}}
ensures mass conservation for the fluid element under external perturbations. In contrast,
{\bm{\zeta}}
preserves the values of fluid density, pressure, and gravitational potential on the perturbed level surface, maintaining them equal to those in the reference configuration – see Section
3.7
for further discussion. These two vector fields coincide only under the assumptions of fluid incompressibility and volume conservation, which are not adopted in the present analysis.

Building on these considerations, we adopt the pullback transformation of the level surfaces,
{\bm{y}}^{\prime}={\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}
, as defined in Eq. (
3.3
) and applied at the point
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
. In our formulation, the infinitesimal displacement vector
{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}
is not restricted to being radial; rather, it may be any smooth vector field that transforms the level surfaces. This leads to the following expression for the perturbed potential:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-({\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime})|}\left(1+\frac{\partial\zeta^{\prime i}}{\partial x^{\prime i}}\right)d^{3}x^{\prime}\;,

(3.18)

where
\zeta^{\prime i}:=\zeta^{i}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
, and all terms quadratic in
{\bm{\zeta}}
have been omitted.

All subsequent calculations are performed to linear order in the displacement vector
{\bm{\zeta}}
. Expanding the integrand in Eq. (
3.18
) in a Taylor series about the point
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
and retaining only terms linear in
\zeta^{i}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}+G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\prime i}}\left[\frac{\rho({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\zeta^{i}(x^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\right]d^{3}x^{\prime}\;,

(3.19)

where, in the second integral, the difference between the perturbed and unperturbed fluid densities has been neglected, as it is of order
{\cal O}(\zeta)
and the integrand already contains the infinitesimal displacement
{\bm{\zeta}}
. The divergence in the second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
3.19
) can be converted into a surface integral via Gauss’s theorem, yielding:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}+G\oint\limits_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\frac{\rho({\bm{a}})\zeta^{i}({\bm{a}})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{a}}|}d^{2}S_{i}\;,

(3.20)

where
d^{2}S_{i}:=d^{2}S_{i}({\bm{a}})
denotes the oriented surface element at the point
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}={\bm{a}}
, and
\mathbb{S}^{2}
indicates that the integration is performed over the boundary
\partial{\mathcal{V}}
of the volume
{\mathcal{V}}
occupied by the fluid in the unperturbed reference configuration, which is a sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
of radius
a=|{\bm{a}}|
.

Expression (
3.20
) represents the same perturbed potential
{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}
as given in Eq. (
3.16
), but now expressed as the sum of a volume integral and a surface integral. The volume integral in Eq. (
3.20
) is evaluated over the unperturbed, spherically symmetric volume
{\mathcal{V}}
of the reference configuration, where all integration points lie on the base manifold, i.e.,
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
. This allows for a direct comparison with the unperturbed potential
{\mathcal{U}}
, since both integrals are now computed over the same spatial domain. The surface integral in Eq. (
3.20
) corresponds to a solution of the Laplace equation, with the fluid density
\rho({\bm{a}})
evaluated on the spherical boundary
\partial{\mathcal{V}}=\mathbb{S}^{2}
of the unperturbed configuration. If the surface density vanishes – as is the case for certain idealized models such as polytropic stars – this surface term becomes zero. However, in more general scenarios where the fluid possesses a non-zero surface density layer, the surface integral cannot be neglected and must be included in the analysis. The evaluation of this surface contribution can be deferred to the solution of the boundary conditions that match the interior and exterior solutions of the perturbed gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}
at the spherical boundary
r=a
of the reference configuration.

In general, the variation of the gravitational potential,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}
, induced by the external perturbation of the centrifugal potential
W_{Q}
, can be defined as the difference between the perturbed and unperturbed volume integrals:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}:=G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}-G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\rho({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}=G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\rho^{\dagger}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})-\rho({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}d^{3}x^{\prime}=G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{\delta_{{\bm{\xi}}}\rho({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}d^{3}x^{\prime}\;,

(3.21)

demonstrating that the variation
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}
is a functional of the Eulerian density perturbation
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, as introduced in Eq. (
3.5
).
The definition in Eq. (
3.21
) is fully consistent with the expression in Eq. (
3.20
). Specifically, the final integral in Eq. (
3.21
) represents a particular solution to the Poisson equation:

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(3.22)

where
\Delta:=\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}
denotes the Laplace operator. The general solution to Eq. (
3.22
) is defined up to a solution of the homogeneous Laplace equation, which corresponds to the surface integral in Eq. (
3.20
). The value of this surface term will be determined later by applying the appropriate boundary conditions that match the interior and exterior solutions of the perturbed gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}
at the spherical boundary
r=a
of the reference configuration.

It is important to emphasize that the diffeomorphism defined in Eq. (
3.1
) is induced by the external centrifugal potential
W_{Q}
and applies exclusively to the coordinates of the fluid parcels. Consequently, the Eulerian variation operator
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}
does not act on the external perturbation itself
[
24
]
. This implies that the Eulerian variations of the external potentials vanish:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}W_{R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\qquad,\qquad\delta_{\bm{\xi}}W_{Q}=0\;.

(3.23)

The physical interpretation of Eq. (
3.23
) is that the fluid’s perturbations do not exert any back-reaction on the external potential
W=W_{R}+W_{Q}
. As a result, the Eulerian variation of the effective potential
U
, as defined in Eq. (
2.21
), coincides with the variation of the gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}\;.

(3.24)

This property will be utilized later in the development of the nonlinear perturbation theory describing the shape of a rotating fluid body.

3.5
Coupling of Density Variation with Gravitational Perturbation

It is essential to elucidate the relationship between the Eulerian variation of density,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, and the corresponding variation in the effective gravitational potential,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U
. This relationship has already been partially established through the integral equation (
3.21
), which is mathematically equivalent to the Poisson differential equation (
3.22
). In the context of a barotropic equation of state, the coupling between the density and gravitational field perturbations becomes more direct, governed by a linear algebraic relation. This relation represents the first integral of the perturbed hydrostatic equilibrium equation for a rotating fluid. We derive this fundamental equation in this section.

To this end, we consider the linearized perturbation of the hydrostatic equilibrium equation (
2.40
), induced by the quadrupole component
W_{Q}
of the centrifugal potential. This perturbation is obtained by taking the Eulerian variation of the equilibrium equation and equating it to the external force arising from
W_{Q}
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\left(\rho\partial_{i}U+\partial_{j}t^{ij}\right)=-\rho\partial_{i}W_{Q}\;.

(3.25)

The variation of the product
\rho\partial_{i}U
follows the Leibniz rule. When applying this rule, it is important to note that the operations of taking the Eulerian variation and partial differentiation commute, regardless of the geometric nature of the quantity involved
[
80
]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\partial_{i}\;.

(3.26)

Applying the Leibniz rule and incorporating Eqs. (
3.11
), (
3.15
), (
3.23
), and (
3.26
), we recast Eq. (
3.25
) into the following form:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\partial_{i}U+\rho\partial_{i}(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U+W_{Q})+\partial_{i}(\xi^{j}\partial_{j}p)+\partial_{i}\left(\uplambda\,\partial_{j}\xi^{j}\right)=0\;,

(3.27)

where the gradients of
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U
and
W_{Q}
have been combined into a single term for clarity.

We now take into account that the functions
\rho=\rho(r)
,
p=p(r)
, and
U=U(r)
in Eq. (
3.27
) correspond to the unperturbed configuration of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
and depend solely on the radial coordinate
r
. This allows us to express all partial derivatives of these functions in terms of derivatives with respect to
r
. Specifically, we use the identity
\partial_{i}U=U^{\prime}n^{i}
, where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate, and
n^{i}=x^{i}/r
is the radial unit vector. With this substitution, the terms in Eq. (
3.27
) can be grouped accordingly, and the equation takes the following form:

\displaystyle\left(U^{\prime}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho-\rho^{\prime}{\cal K}\right)n^{i}+\partial_{i}\left(\rho{\cal K}+\xi^{j}\partial_{j}p+\uplambda\,\partial_{j}\xi^{j}\right)=0\;,

(3.28)

where, once again, the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
r
. We have also introduced the notation

\displaystyle{\cal K}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U({\bm{x}})+W_{Q}({\bm{x}})\;,

(3.29)

to represent the total gravitational perturbation at a point
{\bm{x}}
on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
.

It is noteworthy that the terms involving pressure and the bulk modulus in Eq. (
3.27
) can be expressed in terms of the Eulerian variation of density. Specifically, we have:

\displaystyle\xi^{j}\partial_{j}p+\uplambda\,\partial_{j}\xi^{j}=\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\xi^{j}\partial_{j}\rho+\uplambda\,\partial_{j}\xi^{j}=\frac{\uplambda}{\rho}\left[\xi^{j}\partial_{j}\rho+\rho\partial_{j}\xi^{j}\right]=-\frac{\uplambda}{\rho}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;.

(3.30)

The right-hand side of Eq. (
3.30
) can be further simplified using the Adams-Williamson equation, which describes hydrostatic equilibrium in spherically symmetric configurations
[
2
]
:

\displaystyle\rho U^{\prime}-\frac{\uplambda}{\rho}\rho^{\prime}=0\;.

(3.31)

This equation is equivalent, in the case of a barotropic fluid, to the standard hydrostatic equilibrium condition (
2.41
). This equivalence can be demonstrated by invoking Eq. (
3.10
), which yields:

\displaystyle\frac{\uplambda}{\rho}\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\rho^{\prime}=p^{\prime}\;,

(3.32)

reducing Eq. (
3.31
) to
\rho U^{\prime}-p^{\prime}=0
, which is precisely the form of Eq. (
2.41
) for a self-gravitating fluid sphere in hydrostatic equilibrium.

By incorporating Eq. (
3.31
), we can rewrite Eq. (
3.30
) in the following form:

\displaystyle\xi^{j}\partial_{j}p+\uplambda\,\partial_{j}\xi^{j}=-\frac{\rho}{\rho^{\prime}}\,U^{\prime}\,\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;.

(3.33)

Substituting this expression into Eq. (
3.28
) simplifies the equation to:

\displaystyle\left[\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho-{\cal K}\right]\rho^{\prime}n^{i}-\partial_{i}\left[\rho\left(\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho-{\cal K}\right)\right]=0\;.

(3.34)

Taking the partial derivative in the second term and canceling like terms yields:

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{i}\left[{\cal K}-\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]=0\;,

(3.35)

which represents the gradient form of the perturbed hydrostatic equilibrium equation (
3.27
), describing the interplay between variations in matter variables and the gravitational field. This equation can be integrated, leading to the first integral:

\displaystyle{\cal K}-\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm const.}

(3.36)

The constant of integration must be zero, since in the absence of the external perturbation
W_{Q}
, the left-hand side of Eq. (
3.36
) vanishes identically.

Thus, the first integral (
3.36
) of the variational form of the hydrostatic equilibrium equation (
3.25
) establishes a direct relationship between the first Eulerian variation of fluid density and the Eulerian variation of the gravitational potential:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho=\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}{\cal K}\;.

(3.37)

This fundamental result was originally derived by M.S. Molodensky
[
61
]
. It is important to emphasize that Eq. (
3.37
) is valid exclusively for bodies composed of stationary, rotating fluids without differential rotation. This restriction arises from the absence of an effective shear modulus in fluids, i.e.,
\upmu_{\rm eff}=0
. In cases involving differentially rotating viscous fluids or solid bodies, Eq. (
3.37
) must be appropriately modified to incorporate additional terms accounting for bulk viscosity or shear elasticity.

3.6
Molodensky’s Equation for Gravitational Perturbation

We have established that the variation in the gravitational field satisfies the Poisson equation (
3.22
), where the variation in density appears on the right-hand side as the source term for the perturbation in the gravitational potential. Additionally, Eq. (
3.37
) provides a linear relationship between the density variation and the gravitational field, enabling us to reformulate Eq. (
3.22
) into a Helmholtz-type equation that governs only the variation in the gravitational field.

To proceed, we observe that
W_{Q}
satisfies the Laplace equation (
2.13
), allowing Eq. (
3.22
) to be equivalently expressed as:

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho-\Delta W_{Q}\;.

(3.38)

By combining the Laplacian terms and applying the definition (
3.29
) for the total perturbation
{\cal K}
of the gravitational field induced by the body’s rotation, we recast Eq. (
3.38
) into the form:

\displaystyle\Delta{\cal K}+4\pi G\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(3.39)

This equation can be further simplified by substituting the expression for the density perturbation
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, given in terms of
{\cal K}
via Eq. (
3.37
). This substitution transforms Eq. (
3.39
) into the homogeneous Helmholtz equation for the gravitational field perturbation
{\cal K}={\cal K}({\bm{x}})
:

\displaystyle\Delta{\cal K}+\kappa^{2}{\cal K}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(3.40)

where the coefficient

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{4\pi G\rho^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}}\;,

(3.41)

is a function of the radial coordinate,
\kappa=\kappa(r)
, defined by the internal density distribution
\rho=\rho(r)
and the effective gravitational potential
U=U(r)
. Here, the prime denotes differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate. The dimensionality of
\kappa
is
[\kappa]=(\rm{length})^{-1}
.

Equation (
3.40
), known as the Molodensky equation for gravitational field perturbations
[
61
]
, forms the foundation of Molodensky’s theory of the Earth’s figure. This theory aims to determine the Earth’s shape and gravity field based on measurements of gravitational anomalies at the topographic surface
[
83
]
. It is widely applied in geodesy for computing gravitational anomalies and determining heights above the (quasi)geoid
[
84
]
, and its relevance has recently been explored in planetary science
[
85
]
.

Although it is possible to analyze the boundary conditions associated with the linearized theory to fully resolve the perturbations in density, pressure, and gravitational field, this approach proves insufficient for modeling the shape and gravitational field of rapidly rotating massive planets and stars
[
2
,
51
,
54
]
. These celestial bodies require the inclusion of non-linear perturbations due to their high rotational velocities
[
48
,
49
,
9
]
. Therefore, we will not pursue the technical elaboration of the linearized theory further. Instead, we will focus on the fundamental principles and key results of the non-linear theory, which constitutes the primary objective of this study.

3.7
Exploring Infinitesimal Distortions of Level Surfaces: The Clairaut Equation

The level surface of the effective gravitational potential, denoted by
U=U({\bm{x}})
, is defined as the set of points where the potential assumes a constant value. Owing to the spherical symmetry of the unperturbed potential,
U({\bm{x}})=U(r)
, these level surfaces form a family of concentric spheres. Each sphere can be uniquely identified by a continuous parameter, most naturally chosen as the radial coordinate
r
, which varies from
r=0
at the center of the body to
r=a
at the spherical boundary of the reference configuration.

The introduction of the rotational quadrupole perturbation
W_{Q}
modifies the gravitational potential from
U
to

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})+{\cal K}({\bm{x}})\;,

(3.42)

where
U({\bm{x}})
and
{\cal K}({\bm{x}})
are defined in Eqs. (
2.21
) and (
3.29
), respectively. This modification manifests in two distinct ways: first, through the direct addition of the perturbation potential
W_{Q}
to the effective gravitational potential
U
at each point
{\bm{x}}
; and second, through the induced variation in the body’s potential,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U
, resulting from the density perturbation
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
caused by the centrifugal effects associated with
W_{Q}
.

The coordinates of points on the level surfaces of the perturbed gravitational potential
U^{\dagger}
are denoted by the vector
{\bm{y}}=(y^{i})
, as defined in Eq. (
3.3
). To determine the translation vector
{\bm{\zeta}}
appearing in this expression, we derive the functional equation governing the deformed level surface. This equation follows from Eq. (
3.37
), which encapsulates the condition of hydrostatic equilibrium under perturbations and relates the variation
{\cal K}
in the gravitational field to the perturbation in the fluid’s density.

This equation can be recast in the following form:

\displaystyle{\cal K}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\zeta({\bm{x}})U^{\prime}(r)=-\zeta^{i}({\bm{x}})\partial_{i}U(r)\;,

(3.43)

where the displacement vector

\displaystyle\zeta^{i}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{i}\zeta({\bm{x}})+\zeta^{i}_{\perp}\;,

(3.44)

is expressed as the sum of two components. The first term,
n^{i}\zeta({\bm{x}})
is purely radial, while the second term,
\zeta^{i}_{\perp}
, is tangential to the radial direction and satisfies
n^{i}\zeta^{i}_{\perp}=0
. The scalar function
\zeta
is given by:

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{\rho}=\xi+\frac{\rho}{\rho^{\prime}}\theta\;,

(3.45)

where
\theta=\partial_{i}\xi^{i}
denotes the divergence of the fluid diffeomorphism vector field
\xi^{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
.

The equation of hydrostatic equilibrium (
3.43
) imposes no constraint on the tangential component
\zeta^{i}_{\perp}
which remains undetermined. This reflects a gauge freedom in the choice of the vector field
\zeta^{i}
. It is convenient to fix this gauge by setting
\zeta^{i}_{\perp}=0
, corresponding to the adoption of the radial gauge, which is also used to restrict the freedom in the fluid diffeomorphism
{\bm{\xi}}
. Since the magnitude of the fluid diffeomorphism scales with the rotation parameter,
\xi\sim\mathsf{m}
, the corresponding magnitude of
\zeta
also scales as
\zeta\sim\mathsf{m}
as follows from Eq. (
3.45
). Here,
\mathsf{m}
denotes the parameter characterizing the rotational speed of the body, as defined in Eq. (
2.14
).

By substituting Eq. (
3.43
) into Eq. (
3.42
) and applying the Taylor expansion of the potential
U({\bm{x}})
, Eq. (
3.42
) can be reformulated to express the perturbed potential in terms of a shifted argument of the effective gravitational potential:

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})+{\cal K}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})-\zeta^{i}\partial_{i}U(r)=U({\bm{x}}-{\bm{\zeta}})+{\cal O}\left(\zeta^{2}\right)\;,

(3.46)

where
{\bm{\zeta}}=(\zeta^{i})
and
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
represents the radial displacement of the original (spherical) level surface due to the perturbation induced by the gravitational field
{\cal K}
. This displacement vector is the same infinitesimal translation introduced earlier in Eq. (
3.3
). Its connection to the generator
{\bm{\xi}}
is established in Eq. (
3.45
), which reveals that the difference between
{\bm{\zeta}}
and
{\bm{\xi}}
arises from the divergence
\theta=\partial_{i}\xi^{i}
of the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
.

Equation (
3.46
) can be equivalently expressed in terms of a push-forward transformation as:

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}})+{\cal K}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})\;,

(3.47)

where we have used the fact that, under the linear approximation,
{\cal K}({\bm{x}})={\cal K}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{\zeta}})
. This formulation replaces, in the linear regime, the condition given in Eq. (
1.7
), which was originally employed in the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory to determine the distortion
{\bm{\zeta}}
of the level surfaces.

The infinitesimal radial displacement
\zeta
of the level surface in our framework can be determined via two distinct approaches. The first method utilizes Eq. (
3.43
), which yields the direct expression:

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\cal K}}{U^{\prime}}\;.

(3.48)

This formulation for
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
is applicable when the effective potential
U=U(r)
is known from the solution of the reference configuration, and the perturbing potential
{\cal K}={\cal K}({\bm{x}})
is available from the solution of the Molodensky equation (
3.40
). In the context of geodesy, Eq. (
3.48
) is recognized as Bruns’ theorem
[
78
,
86
]
.

In cases where the perturbing potential
{\cal K}
is not explicitly known, an alternative approach can be employed to determine the radial shift
\zeta
without relying on Eq. (
3.48
). This method involves deriving a differential equation for
\zeta
in which
{\cal K}
does not appear explicitly. Such an equation can be obtained directly from the Molodensky equation (
3.40
), which governs the gravitational perturbation
{\cal K}
.

By substituting the expression for
{\cal K}
from Eq. (
3.43
) into the Molodensky equation (
3.40
) and carrying out the differentiation, we obtain the following differential equation for the radial displacement
\zeta
:

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta-\frac{4\zeta^{\prime}}{r}-8\pi G\sigma\frac{\zeta^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}+\frac{2\zeta}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(3.49)

where Eqs. (
2.28
) and (
2.29
) have been used to eliminate the second and third derivatives of the potential
U
, respectively. Eq. (
3.49
) has been also derived by S. Molodensky
[
61
]
.

This equation can be further simplified by introducing the shape function
s=\zeta/r
, which admits a decomposition into a series of Legendre polynomials:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}s_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;.

(3.50)

Substituting this expansion into Eq. (
3.49
) yields the governing equation for the spectral harmonics
s_{l}
of the shape function:

\displaystyle s_{l}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(s^{\prime}_{l}+\frac{s_{l}}{r}\right)-\frac{l(l+1)s_{l}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(3.51)

where Eq. (
2.37
) has been applied, and the function
\upbeta=\upbeta(r)
is defined in Eq. (
2.35
).

Equation (
3.51
) is, in fact, the classical Clairaut equation
[
10
,
2
]
governing the spectral harmonics
s_{l}
(
l\geq 2
) of the shape function
s=s(r,\theta)
. Solving this equation with appropriate boundary conditions on
s_{l}
and its radial derivative
s^{\prime}_{l}=ds_{l}/dr
determines the geometric configuration of the level surfaces. The derivation of the Clairaut equation (
3.51
) within the linearized approximation – based on the small parameter
\mathsf{m}
characterizing the body’s rotational speed – is attributed to S.M. Molodensky
[
61
]
.

The next step in our analysis is to extend this linearized Clairaut theory to higher-order approximations and ultimately to formulate a fully non-linear theory describing the figure of a rotating fluid body.

4
Nonlinear Hydrostatic Perturbations

The previous section presented a linearized theory of rotational perturbations in a fluid body, formulated in terms of infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of fluid parcels and level surfaces, along with first-order Eulerian variations in fluid density, pressure, and the gravitational field. These quantities were defined on the tangent bundle
T{\mathfrak{M}}
of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
. While this linear theory is adequate for modeling the rotational deformation of slowly rotating celestial bodies – where perturbations are of the same order as the small parameter
\mathsf{m}
– it becomes insufficient for rapidly rotating bodies such as Jupiter, where the deformation reaches a significant magnitude, approximately 10% of the planetary radius
[
70
,
9
]
. In such cases, the perturbation theory must incorporate higher-order effects in
\mathsf{m}
to accurately describe small-scale variations in the gravitational field and to compute large (finite rather than infinitesimal) deformations of the body’s shape.

This section develops a non-linear theory of hydrostatic perturbations in the density distribution of a rotating fluid body. It begins with a formulation of finite fluid diffeomorphisms and their associated Lie group structure, and proceeds to derive the finite, non-linear Eulerian perturbations in density, pressure, and the gravitational field.

4.1
Fluid Diffeomorphisms: From Lie Algebra to Lie Group

The advanced theory of non-linear hydrostatic perturbations necessitates a generalization of the infinitesimal Eulerian variations in a fluid’s density, pressure, and gravitational field to their finite counterparts. This extension enables a more comprehensive analytical treatment of finite deformations within the fluid body, accommodating large-scale perturbations and yielding a more accurate mathematical representation of fluid behavior under the extreme conditions characteristic of planetary and stellar interiors. By incorporating finite variations, the theoretical framework can effectively capture the influence of external forces on the intrinsic displacements of fluid parcels. This leads to the development of enhanced analytical models and more precise numerical simulations, particularly in the context of hydrostatic studies concerning the internal structure, equilibrium shape, and gravitational field of rotating planets and stars.

The vector flow
{\bm{x}}_{\tau}
is generated by the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
. This flow induces a finite diffeomorphism, denoted by
\phi_{\tau}
, which maps the unperturbed base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
to the perturbed manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
, i.e.,
\phi_{\tau}:{\mathfrak{M}}\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
. The base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
is compact and has a volume
{\mathcal{V}}={\rm Vol}({\mathfrak{M}})
, including its boundary. Correspondingly, the perturbed manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
has volume
{\rm Vol}({\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau})
. It is important to note that, in the general case of a compressible fluid, the volume
{\rm Vol}({\mathfrak{M}})
of the base manifold may differ from the volume
{\rm Vol}({\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau})
of the perturbed manifold due to the deformation induced by the flow, such as rotational motion.

The parameter
\tau
in the vector flow parameterizes the strength of the external centrifugal perturbation
W_{Q}
through a linear mapping:
W_{Q}\to\tau W_{Q}
. This is equivalent to introducing a
\tau
-dependent rotational parameter:
\mathsf{m}\to\tau\mathsf{m}
, where
\mathsf{m}
is fixed and defined in Eq. (
2.14
). When
\tau=0
, the quadrupole centrifugal perturbation is absent, corresponding to the undisturbed reference configuration. When
\tau=1
, the full quadrupole perturbation of interest,
W_{Q}
, is present. Intermediate values
0<\tau<1
allow us to consider a continuous family of quadrupole centrifugal perturbations, interpolating from
W_{Q}=0
(at
\tau=0
) to the full perturbation
W_{Q}
(at
\tau=1
).

Additionally,
\tau
parameterizes the rotationally induced perturbation of the gravitational field of the reference configuration, denoted by
U_{\tau}=U_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
(see Eq. (
5.23
)). This perturbation includes both linear and non-linear terms in
\tau
and, as shown later in Eq. (
5.24
), is expressed using an exponential mapping.

It is important to emphasize that
\tau
should not be interpreted as time. Instead, we work within the Lagrangian coordinate system
{\bm{x}}
, which rigidly co-rotates with the fluid. In this frame, all functions defined on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
depend solely on the spatial coordinates
{\bm{x}}
, and thus all time derivatives vanish identically. Within this framework,
\tau
serves as a homotopy parameter
[
87
]
, interpolating between two continuous functions: the unperturbed gravitational potential
U({\bm{x}})
of the reference configuration (see Section
2
), and the fully perturbed gravitational potential
V({\bm{x}}):=U({\bm{x}})+K({\bm{x}})
. Here,
K({\bm{x}})
encapsulates the total perturbation, including both the gravitational field variation and the quadrupole centrifugal potential, as detailed later in Eq. (
6.13
). This homotopy defines a smooth transition from the unperturbed base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}={\mathfrak{M}}_{0}
to the perturbed manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
. It is represented by a continuous function
H(\tau,{\bm{x}}):{\mathfrak{M}}\times[0,1]\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
, mapping the product space
{\mathfrak{M}}\times[0,1]
into
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
, such that
H(0,{\bm{x}})=U({\bm{x}})
and
H(1,{\bm{x}})=V({\bm{x}})
for all
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
.

The parameter
\tau
thus serves as a valuable organizational tool in the perturbation analysis. In the previous section, we implicitly set
\tau=1
, omitting it from the notation, as we were working within the framework of linear perturbation theory – where distinguishing between different orders of
\tau
is unnecessary. However, this distinction becomes crucial in the context of non-linear theory, where higher-order contributions must be systematically tracked and analyzed.

Each vector flow is a smooth map:
{\bm{x}}\rightarrow{\bm{x}}_{\tau}=\phi_{\tau}{\bm{x}}
, generated by a vector field
\xi^{i}=\xi^{i}({\bm{x}})
, which is tangent to the integral curve of the flow at the point
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
. This vector field defines the entire congruence associated with the deformation of the fluid body. The flow map is governed by the ordinary differential equation:

\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}_{\tau}}{d\tau}=\xi^{i}\left({\bm{x}}_{\tau}\right)\;,

(4.1)

with the initial condition
{\bm{x}}_{0}={\bm{x}}
. Under standard regularity assumptions, this initial value problem admits a unique solution. Assuming the vector field is analytic over the entire base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, Eq. (
4.1
) can be solved locally around the initial point
{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}_{0}
using a Taylor expansion:

\displaystyle x^{i}_{\tau}=x^{i}+\tau{\dot{x}}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\tau^{2}\ddot{x}^{i}+\ldots\;,

(4.2)

where the derivatives are computed using Eq. (
4.1
), yielding
\dot{x}^{i}=\xi^{i}({\bm{x}})
,
\ddot{x}^{i}=\xi^{j}({\bm{x}})\partial_{j}\xi^{i}({\bm{x}})
, and so on. This leads to the exponential map:

\displaystyle x^{i}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau L_{\bm{\xi}}\right)x^{i}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}L_{\bm{\xi}}^{n}x^{i}\;,

(4.3)

where
L_{\bm{\xi}}:=\xi^{i}\partial_{i}
is the directional derivative along the tangent vector
{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi^{i})
taken at the intiial point
{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}_{0}
. All evaluations of
\xi^{i}
and its derivatives are taken at the initial point
x^{i}=x^{i}_{0}
. The flow map
{\bm{x}}\mapsto{\bm{x}}_{\tau}=\phi_{\tau}{\bm{x}}
thus extends the elements
{\bm{\xi}}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
to a one-parameter Lie group
{\mathfrak{G}}_{\tau}
. The collection of all such one-parameter subgroups over the base manifold forms the full Lie group
{\mathfrak{G}}=\cup{\mathfrak{G}}_{\tau}
of diffeomorphisms on
{\mathfrak{M}}
.

Equation (
4.3
) can be interpreted as describing a finite translation of a fluid parcel from its unperturbed position
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
to a new position
{\bm{w}}:={\bm{x}}_{\tau}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
:

\displaystyle w^{i}=x^{i}+{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}^{i}({\bm{x}})\;,

(4.4)

where the vector
{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{X}}}_{\tau}=({\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}^{i})
represents the finite displacement of the fluid parcel and is given by the exponential map:

\displaystyle{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}^{i}({\bm{x}})=\left[\exp\left(\tau L_{\bm{\xi}}\right)-1\right]x^{i}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}L_{\bm{\xi}}^{n}x^{i}\;.

(4.5)

This expression can be recast using the identity
\xi^{i}=L_{\bm{\xi}}x^{i}
, yielding:

\displaystyle{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}^{i}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}L_{\bm{\xi}}^{n}\xi^{i}\;.

(4.6)

The vector
{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}^{i}\in{\mathfrak{G}}_{\tau}
generalizes the infinitesimal translation
\xi^{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\bm{x}}
introduced in Eq. (
3.1
) to the domain of a finite size. Here, the coordinates
w^{i}\in{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
represent the Eulerian position of the fluid element, while
x^{i}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
are its Lagrangian coordinates on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
.

The collection of all independent vector fields
{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi^{i})
forms a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
associated with the diffeomorphism group on
{\mathfrak{M}}
. The structure of this Lie algebra is fully characterized by the Lie derivative operator
\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}
, which defines the Lie bracket between any two vector fields. The vector field
{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{X}}}_{\tau}
is an element of the Lie group associated with the Lie algebra of vector fields
{\bm{\xi}}
via the exponential map (
4.5
)
[
74
]
. This exponential map (
4.3
) extends the Lie bracket structure across the entire base manifold.

4.2
Hydrostatic Perturbations as an Exponential Map

The finite Lagrangian variation of the fluid density, denoted by
\rho
, is defined as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\tau}\rho:=\rho_{\tau}\left({\bm{x}}_{\tau}\right)-\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(4.7)

where
\rho({\bm{x}})
represents the unperturbed density of a fluid parcel located at the point
{\bm{x}}
on the reference manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, and
\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}_{\tau})
denotes the perturbed density of the same fluid parcel, now located at the point
{\bm{x}}_{\tau}
on the deformed manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
due to a perturbation.

In general,
\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}_{\tau})\neq\rho({\bm{x}}_{\tau})
, as the fluid is compressible and its density may vary along the flow induced by the perturbation. The definition in (
4.7
) inherently involves quantities defined on two distinct manifolds,
{\mathfrak{M}}
and
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
, which complicates analytical treatment – particularly because the geometric structure of the deformed manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
is typically unknown a priori. In light of these complications, Lagrangian variations such as (
4.7
) are not utilized in this manuscript in the formulation of the theory of figures for rotating fluid bodies.

From a mathematical standpoint, it is more convenient to work with the finite Eulerian variation of the density, denoted by

\displaystyle\varrho_{\tau}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})-\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(4.8)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}\left(\phi^{-1}_{\tau}{\bm{x}}_{\tau}\right)\;,

(4.9)

represents the pullback of the perturbed density
\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}_{\tau})
from the point
{\bm{x}}_{\tau}\in{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
to the corresponding point
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
. This pullback is defined via the inverse diffeomorphism
{\bm{x}}_{\tau}\to{\bm{x}}=\phi^{-1}_{\tau}{\bm{x}}_{\tau}
, which is obtained from Eq. (
4.3
) as
{\bm{x}}=\exp(-\tau L_{\bm{\xi}}){\bm{x}}_{\tau}
. The Eulerian variation (
4.8
) is thus entirely defined on the reference manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, thereby circumventing the need to work with coordinates
{\bm{x}}_{\tau}
on the deformed manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
. This makes it more suitable for analytical developments, particularly in contexts where the geometry of
{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
is not explicitly known.

A finite Eulerian perturbation of a fluid attribute (such as density or pressure) is defined by extending the concept of the linear Eulerian variation,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}
, into the nonlinear regime induced by the finite displacement vector field given in Eq. (
4.5
). According to Eq. (
3.7
), the linearized variation of the density,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, corresponds to the Lie derivative of
\rho
along the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
, thereby revealing the underlying structure of the Lie algebra generated by
{\bm{\xi}}
.

To construct a nonlinear extension, the Lie algebra is promoted to its associated Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}\mapsto\mathfrak{G}
, via the exponential map
[
80
]
, defined by

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}=1+\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2!}\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}+\frac{\tau^{3}}{3!}\delta^{3}_{\bm{\xi}}+...\;.

(4.10)

Since the first-order Eulerian variation commutes with partial derivatives with respect to spatial coordinates, as shown in Eq. (
3.26
), the exponential map inherits this commutation property:

\displaystyle\exp\left({\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)\partial_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\exp\left({\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)\;.

(4.11)

Using the exponential map, the pullback of the density
\rho
to the reference configuration is defined as

\displaystyle\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)\rho({\bm{x}})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho({\bm{x}})\;.

(4.12)

This definition ensures that the perturbed density satisfies the continuity equation along the integral curve
{\bm{x}}_{\tau}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})}{\partial\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\;,

(4.13)

where the variational operator
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho_{\tau}
is interpreted as the Lie derivative,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho_{\tau}=\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho_{\tau}=-\partial_{i}(\xi^{i}\rho_{\tau})
, with the minus sign reflecting the pullback transformation. Equation (
4.13
) expresses the conservation of mass for a fluid parcel as it is transported along the flow field.

The finite Eulerian variation of the density, as defined in Eq. (
4.8
), can thus be expressed via the exponential map as

\displaystyle\varrho_{\tau}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\bigl[\exp\left(\tau{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)-1\bigr]\rho=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;.

(4.14)

This formulation generalizes the linear perturbation theory expressed in Eq. (
3.7
) to the nonlinear regime. In particular, the linear Eulerian variation is recovered as the leading-order term:
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho=\lim\limits_{\tau\to 0}\partial_{\tau}\varrho_{\tau}
.

The finite Eulerian perturbation of pressure is defined via the equation of state as

\displaystyle p_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle p(\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}))=p\left(e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\rho({\bm{x}})\right)\;.

(4.15)

By the equivariance property of the Lie group of diffeomorphisms (as discussed in Appendix
A
), the composition of the equation of state with the exponential map satisfies:
p\left(e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\rho({\bm{x}})\right)=e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}p(\rho({\bm{x}}))
. This implies that the finite perturbation of pressure is also governed by the exponential map:

\displaystyle p_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)p({\bm{x}})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}p({\bm{x}})\;.

(4.16)

The higher-order variations
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}^{n}p
can be computed recursively using the equation of state
p=p(\rho)
and successive applications of the chain rule for composite functions. For instance:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}p}{\partial\rho^{2}}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)^{2}+\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(4.18)

and so on. For general higher-order variations appearing in the expansion (
4.16
), Faà di Bruno’s formula
[
88
]
is employed to systematically compute the derivatives of composite functions. This procedure is further elaborated in Section
5.4
.

4.3
Gauge Freedom in Fluid Diffeomorphisms

4.3.1
Gauge Freedom in Lie Algebra Generators

The perturbation in density governs corresponding perturbations in both pressure and the gravitational field of the fluid body. Typically, an infinitesimal density perturbation of the fluid, denoted by
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, is attributed to an infinitesimal displacement
{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathcal{G}
of the fluid element, which transports it from the point
{\bm{x}}
to the point
{\bm{w}}
, as described in Eq. (
3.1
).

However, this interpretation warrants careful consideration. In the macroscopic framework of continuum mechanics, where the fluid is modeled as a continuous medium, it is possible for the same physical density perturbation,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, to arise from different infinitesimal displacements of fluid elements. This phenomenon, known as
gauge freedom
, is well recognized in cosmological studies of the large-scale structure of the universe
[
89
,
90
]
, and was also identified in the Newtonian perturbation theory of rotating fluid bodies by Friedman and Schutz
[
91
]
.

Gauge freedom is characterized by a transformation of the generator of the Lie algebra:

\displaystyle{\bm{\xi}}\mapsto{\bm{\chi}}={\bm{\xi}}+{\bm{\eta}}\;,

(4.19)

where the gauge vector field
{\bm{\eta}}=(\eta^{i})
is chosen to preserve the density perturbation:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\chi}}\rho=\delta_{{\bm{\xi}}+{\bm{\eta}}}\rho=\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho+\delta_{\bm{\eta}}\rho=\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;.

(4.20)

Since the density perturbation is defined as the Lie derivative, the gauge invariance condition expressed in Eq. (
4.20
) requires:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\eta}}\rho=\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\rho=-{\bm{\nabla}}\cdot(\rho{\bm{\eta}})=0\;,

(4.21)

where
{\bm{\nabla}}=(\partial_{i})
denotes the gradient operator.

This differential condition can be addressed using Helmholtz’s theorem, which states that any vector field
\rho{\bm{\eta}}
satisfying Eq. (
4.21
) can be decomposed into the sum of a curl-free component,
{\bm{\nabla}}\Phi
, and a divergence-free component,
{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{A}}

[
92
]
:

\displaystyle\rho{\bm{\eta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\nabla}}\Phi+{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{A}}\;,

(4.22)

where
{\bm{A}}={\bm{A}}({\bm{x}})
is an arbitrary smooth vector field, and
\Phi=\Phi({\bm{x}})
is a non-singular harmonic function satisfying the Laplace equation,
\Delta\Phi=0
.

Friedman and Schutz
[
91
]
referred to the gauge transformation in Eq. (
4.19
), with the gauge field given by Eq. (
4.22
), as “trivial”. However, it is important to note that the original formulation of the “trivial” gauge transformation by Friedman and Schutz omits the gauge degree of freedom associated with the curl-free scalar field
\Phi
in Eq. (
4.22
). This degree of freedom presents only in case of hydrostatic perturbations.

An illustrative example of a gauge diffeomorphism is an infinitesimal rigid rotation of the fluid’s reference configuration by a constant angle
{\bm{\alpha}}
about a fixed axis. The corresponding gauge vector field is given by:

\displaystyle\eta^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left({\bm{\alpha}}\times{\bm{x}}\right)^{i}\;.

(4.23)

Evaluating the density variation along this vector field yields:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\eta}}\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\rho=-{\bm{\nabla}}\cdot\left[\rho\left({\bm{\alpha}}\times{\bm{x}}\right)\right]=-{\bm{\nabla}}\rho\cdot\left({\bm{\alpha}}\times{\bm{x}}\right)+\rho{\bm{\alpha}}\cdot\left({\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{x}}\right)\;.

(4.24)

Assuming the density depends solely on the radial coordinate, i.e.,
\rho=\rho(r)
, we have
{\bm{\nabla}}\rho=\rho^{\prime}{\bm{n}}
, where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
r
, and
{\bm{n}}={\bm{x}}/r
is the unit radial vector. Under this assumption, the first term on the right-hand side vanishes:
{\bm{\nabla}}\rho\cdot\left({\bm{\alpha}}\times{\bm{x}}\right)=0
, since the radial gradient is orthogonal to the tangential vector
{\bm{\alpha}}\times{\bm{x}}
. The second term also vanishes because
{\bm{\nabla}}\times{\bm{x}}=0
. This result implies that a rigid rotation of a fluid sphere about a fixed axis in space does not induce any perturbation in the fluid density. Physically, this reflects the invariance of the density distribution under such a symmetry transformation.

4.3.2
Gauge Freedom in Exponential Map of a Lie Group

The gauge freedom in choosing generators of the Lie algebra naturally extends to gauge invariance of the Lie group of diffeomorphisms on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
. The exponential map generates the total variation of density
\varrho_{\tau}
, as given in Eq. (
4.14
). Let us consider a more general gauge transformation:

\displaystyle{\bm{\xi}}\mapsto{\bm{\chi}}={\bm{\xi}}+{\bm{\upsilon}}\;,

(4.25)

where
{\bm{\upsilon}}
must ensure the gauge invariance of the finite Eulerian vartiation of density,
\varrho_{\tau}
. This condition is satisfied if and only if:

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\pounds_{{\bm{\xi}}+{\bm{\upsilon}}}\right)\rho({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\right)\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(4.26)

where
{\bm{\upsilon}}
has yet to be determined. It is always possible (in the region of existence of the exponential map) to choose the gauge vector field
{\bm{\upsilon}}
in a form suitable for the application of the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula
[
93
]
:

\displaystyle{\bm{\upsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\eta}}+\frac{\tau}{2}\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\eta}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{12}\pounds^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\eta}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{12}\pounds^{2}_{\bm{\eta}}{\bm{\xi}}-\frac{\tau^{3}}{24}\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\pounds^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}{\bm{\eta}}+\ldots\;,

(4.27)

where the residual terms are well-known and consist of the commutators (Lie derivatives) of the vector fields
{\bm{\eta}}
and
{\bm{\xi}}

[
93
]
.

Using the BCH formula
[
93
]
for the exponential function of the sum of two non-commuting vector fields, we can rewrite the left-hand side of Eq. (
4.26
) as follows:

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\pounds_{{\bm{\xi}}+{\bm{\upsilon}}}\right)\rho({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\right)\exp\left(\tau\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\right)\rho({\bm{x}})\;.

(4.28)

The product of the two exponents on the right-hand side of Eq. (
4.28
) must be equal to the exponent on the right-hand side of Eq. (
4.26
), which imposes the following condition on the gauge field
{\bm{\eta}}
:

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\right)\rho({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho({\bm{x}})\;.

(4.29)

This condition aligns with the gauge requirement
\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\rho({\bm{x}})=0
, imposed on the vector field
{\bm{\eta}}
within the Lie algebra space. It provides the same solution for the gauge vector field
{\bm{\eta}}
as shown in Eq. (
4.22
). With this understanding, and knowing the generator
{\bm{\xi}}
, we can determine the generalized gauge vector field
{\bm{\upsilon}}
using Eq. (
4.27
).

4.3.3
The Radial Gauge

The gauge freedom for the generators
{\bm{\xi}}
of the Lie group
{\mathfrak{G}}
is constrained by the condition
\pounds_{\bm{\eta}}\rho=0
, which is imposed on the three otherwise free components of the gauge vector field
{\bm{\eta}}
. This implies that any two components of the gauge vector
{\bm{\eta}}
can be chosen arbitrarily. Consequently, any linear diffeomorphism
{\bm{\xi}}
in a stationary rotating fluid possesses two nonphysical degrees of freedom associated with the Lie-invariance of the fluid density
\rho
along the gauge vector field
{\bm{\eta}}
, as defined by Eq. (
4.22
). This freedom is particularly advantageous for theoretical calculations, as it permits the elimination of two coordinate components of the diffeomorphism
{\bm{\xi}}
that lack physical significance. For example, the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
can be chosen coinciding with coordinate
x=x^{1}
of the rigidly rotating coordinate system:
{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi,0,0)
, where
\xi=\xi({\bm{x}})
. This choice will be implemented in Section
5.2
.

A particularly advantageous choice for our analysis is the radial gauge, in which the generator of the Lie algebra is constrained to be purely radial, taking the form
{\bm{\xi}}={\bm{n}}\xi({\bm{x}})
or
{\bm{\zeta}}={\bm{n}}\zeta({\bm{x}})
, where
{\bm{n}}
denotes the unit radial vector. This gauge naturally extends the local generators of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
to the global vector flows of the Lie group
{\mathfrak{G}}
across the entire base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, as illustrated previously via the exponential map. The radial gauge proves especially useful in deriving the differential equations governing gravitational perturbations and the equilibrium shape of a rotating fluid body, as will be discussed in subsequent sections.

The presence of gauge freedom implies that, in the context of hydrostatic perturbations, an identical variation in fluid density within the perturbed configuration can be produced through multiple distinct permutations of fluid parcels from their original positions in the unperturbed reference state. These permutations are governed by the gauge vector field
{\bm{\eta}}
, as defined in Eq. (
4.22
). Although such rearrangements can be implemented in various ways, they do not alter the geometry of the level surfaces and can always be interpreted as resulting from displacements of fluid parcels exclusively along radial directions from their initial positions.

5
Nonlinear Perturbations of Gravitational Field

5.1
Gravitational Potential and Level Surfaces

The quadrupole centrifugal potential
\tau W_{Q}
generates a vector flow
\phi_{\tau}
, which perturbs the fluid density by an amount
\varrho_{\tau}
, defined via the exponential map (
4.14
). This perturbation modifies the total density on the base manifold, such that
\rho({\bm{x}})\to\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})=\rho({\bm{x}})+\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
, with
