\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
given explicitly in Eq. (
4.12
). The gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
corresponding to the perturbed density
\rho_{\tau}
satisfies the Poisson equation:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\;.

(5.1)

The rotation-induced change in density at each point on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
is accompanied by a geometric deformation of the manifold itself,
{\mathfrak{M}}\to{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
, and a rearrangement of fluid parcels. This rearrangement alters both the shape of the reference configuration and, in the case of a compressible fluid, its volume
{\mathcal{V}}
. Importantly, the vector flow generator
\xi^{i}
, which defines the fluid displacement, does not fully determine the deformation of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
. Likewise, the finite diffeomorphism
{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}^{i}
alone does not uniquely define the shape of the rotating body.

The infinitesimal change in the volume occupied by a fluid parcel is characterized by the divergence of the vector flow,
\theta=\partial_{i}\xi^{i}
, which represents the algebraic part of the operator for the infinitesimal Eulerian variation of density,
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho=\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
. Since the fluid is assumed to be compressible, we have
\theta\neq 0
. Therefore, the rearrangement of fluid parcels due to rotational perturbation must account for both the geometric deformation of the fluid volume and the change in its numerical value.

The physical deformation of the base manifold is thus associated with a reconfiguration of level surfaces – those of constant density, pressure, and gravitational potential (see Section
3.2
). To describe the infinitesimal deformation of these surfaces, we introduce a generator
{\bm{\zeta}}=(\zeta^{i})
and adopt the radial gauge
\zeta^{i}=n^{i}\zeta({\bm{x}})
, as detailed in Section
3.2
. In the reference configuration, each level surface on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
is spherically symmetric and labeled by the radial distance
r\in[0,a]
measured from the center of mass which coincides with the origin of the coordinate system
{\bm{x}}
.

Under a large rotational deformation induced by the potential
\tau W_{Q}
, each point
{\bm{x}}
on an unperturbed spherical level surface is displaced to a new position with coordinates:

\displaystyle y^{i}_{\tau}=x^{i}+X^{i}_{\tau}\;,

(5.2)

where
X^{i}_{\tau}=X^{i}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
describes the finite displacement of the level surface. The displaced points
y^{i}_{\tau}
span a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with volume
{\cal V}_{\tau}
, whose boundary
\partial{\cal V}_{\tau}
defines a physical realization of the perturbed base manifold, denoted by
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}=\{y^{i}_{\tau}\in{\cal V}_{\tau}\cup\partial{\cal V}_{\tau}\}
.

In general, for a compressible fluid, the transformations (
5.2
) and (
4.4
) differ, implying that
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}\neq{\mathfrak{M}}_{\tau}
. The two representations of the perturbed base manifold coincide only in the special case of an incompressible fluid – a scenario that is typically inadequate for modeling stars or giant planets in the solar system.

The vector field
X^{i}_{\tau}=X^{i}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
, characterizes the finite displacement of level surfaces within space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, accompanied by a physical deformation of the fluid body’s initially spherical configuration. Mathematically,
X^{i}_{\tau}
is interpreted as an element of the Lie group
\mathfrak{G}
associated with the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
. Accordingly, we postulate that this displacement field is defined via the exponential map with generator
{\bm{\zeta}}
applied to the identity element
{\bm{x}}
, yielding the transformed position
{\bm{y}}_{\tau}
. This leads to the expression:

\displaystyle X^{i}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[\exp\left(\tau L_{\bm{\zeta}}\right)-1\bigr]x^{i}=\tau\zeta^{i}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2!}\zeta^{j}\partial_{j}\zeta^{i}+\frac{\tau^{3}}{3!}\zeta^{k}\partial_{k}\left(\zeta^{j}\partial_{j}\zeta^{i}\right)+\ldots\;.

(5.3)

Here, the linear operator
L_{\bm{\zeta}}
is defined as
L_{\bm{\zeta}}:=\zeta^{i}\partial_{i}
. The partial derivative of the generator is given by:
\partial_{j}\zeta^{i}=\partial_{j}(\zeta n^{i})=n^{i}\partial_{j}\zeta+(\zeta/r){\cal P}_{ij}
, where
{\cal P}_{ij}=\delta_{ij}-n_{i}n_{j}
denotes the projection operator onto the plane orthogonal to the unit radial vector
n^{i}
. Substituting this expression into the expansion above demonstrates that if the generator
\zeta^{i}
is purely radial, then the resulting vector field
X_{\tau}^{i}
is also radial, i.e.,
X_{\tau}^{i}=X_{\tau}n^{i}
, with
X_{\tau}=X_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
being a scalar function of position.

We refer to the radial displacement of the level surface,
X_{\tau}=X_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
, as the
height function
. This function is related to the infinitesimal generator of radial translation of level surfaces,
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
, through the push-forward exponential map, as a direct consequence of Eq. (
5.3
):

\displaystyle X_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[\exp\left(\tau L_{\bm{\zeta}}\right)-1\bigr]r=\tau\zeta+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2!}\zeta\partial_{r}\zeta+\frac{\tau^{3}}{3!}\zeta\partial_{r}\left(\zeta\partial_{r}\zeta\right)+\ldots\;.

(5.4)

In the general case of a compressible fluid,
\zeta({\bm{x}})\neq\xi({\bm{x}})
, and the displacement
X^{i}_{\tau}\neq{\mathscr{X}}^{i}_{\tau}
. These quantities coincide only in the incompressible fluid case. At this stage, explicit expressions for
\zeta
and
X_{\tau}
are not required for the discussion of subsequent transformations of the gravitational potential. The generator
\zeta
is obtained by solving the Clairaut equation (
3.49
), while the differential equation governing the height function
X_{\tau}
will be derived in Section
6.3.2
.

A particular solution to Eq. (
5.1
), expressed in Cartesian coordinates and regular at infinity, is given by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau}}\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{y}}^{\prime})d^{3}y^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{y}}^{\prime}|}\;,

(5.5)

where
{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau}
denotes the volume occupied by the fluid body deformed under the influence of the rotational potential
\tau W_{Q}
. Here,
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
represents the Cartesian coordinates of the field point, and
{\bm{y}}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
are the Cartesian coordinates of the integration points.

Evaluating the integral in Eq. (
5.5
) constitutes a central task in Newtonian gravitational theory. This computation becomes tractable when both the perturbed density
\rho_{\tau}
and the deformed volume
{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau}
are known. These quantities can be determined if the diffeomorphism
X_{\tau}
is known – a determination that lies at the heart of the theory of rotating figures (TOF)
[
2
,
54
]
.

To solve this problem, it is necessary to decompose the perturbed Newtonian potential
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
into two components: a volume integral over the unperturbed domain
{\mathcal{V}}
of the reference configuration, involving the perturbed density
\rho_{\tau}
, and a surface integral over the boundary
\partial{\mathcal{V}}
, accounting for the density of surface layers. For infinitesimally small perturbations, this decomposition –
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}={\mathcal{U}}^{\dagger}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2})
– was discussed in Section
3.4
and is presented in Eq. (
3.20
).

A similar decomposition can be extended to cases involving strong gravitational perturbations and significant deformations of the spherical reference volume
{\mathcal{V}}
. This requires isolating the finite Eulerian perturbation of the density,
\varrho_{\tau}
(as defined in Eq. (
4.8
)), within the integrand of Eq. (
5.5
), in a manner consistent with the finite Eulerian variation of the Poisson equation (
5.1
).

However, the integration domain
{\cal V}_{\tau}
in Eq. (
5.5
) corresponds to the perturbed configuration, mapped from the reference volume
{\mathcal{V}}
via the diffeomorphism
{\mathcal{V}}\to{\cal V}_{\tau}
, as defined by Eq. (
5.2
). Consequently, the Eulerian variation of the gravitational potential cannot be obtained by directly subtracting the unperturbed potential
{\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}})
(see Eq. (
2.2
)) from
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
, since the respective integrals are defined over different manifolds: the base manifold
\mathfrak{M}
for
{\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}})
and the perturbed manifold
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
for
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
.

To address this, and following the approach used in the linearized perturbation theory of Section
3.4
, we apply a pullback diffeomorphism to transform the integration in Eq. (
5.5
) from manifold
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
back to the base manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. This reduces the integration domain to the spherical reference volume
{\mathcal{V}}
, enabling the decomposition of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}
into volume and surface integrals.

5.2
Pull Back Transformation of Gravitational Potential

The volume of integration
{\cal V}_{\tau}
in Eq. (
5.5
) consists of a continuous set of stratified level surfaces which do not overlap and can be naturally considered as representing three-dimensional coordinate system on the perturbed based manifold
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
with Cartesian coordinates
{\bm{y}}_{\tau}
. On the other hand, diffeomorphism (
5.2
) can be considered as a coordinate transformation between the coordinates of the base
{\mathfrak{M}}
and perturbed
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
manifolds. We identify the coordinates
{\bm{y}}^{\prime}
in the integral (
5.5
) with the coordinates
{\bm{y}}^{\prime}_{\tau}
defined by the set of the level surfaces

\displaystyle y^{\prime i}:=y^{\prime i}_{\tau}=x^{\prime i}+X^{\prime i}_{\tau}\;,

(5.6)

where
X^{\prime i}_{\tau}=X^{i}_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
is given by Eq. (
5.3
) after replacing the generator
{\bm{\zeta}}\to{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}:={\bm{\zeta}}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
and
L_{\bm{\zeta}}\to L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}:=\zeta^{i}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\partial/\partial x^{\prime i}
. The transformation (
5.6
) brings the deformed volume of integration
{\cal V}_{\tau}
back to the spherical volume
{\mathcal{V}}
of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
and transform the integral (
5.5
) to the following form, explicitly depending on the coordinates
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
of the unperturbed base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{\cal V}\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{X}}^{\prime}_{\tau})}{|{\bm{x}}-\left({\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{X}}^{\prime}_{\tau}\right)|}{\rm det}\left[\frac{\partial y^{\prime i}}{\partial x^{\prime j}}\right]d^{3}x^{\prime}\;,

(5.7)

where the integration is over the coordinates
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
.

Now, using Appendix Eq. (
A.1
), we write the ratio of two functions in the above integral in the form of the exponential mapping:

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{X}}^{\prime}_{\tau})}{|{\bm{x}}-\left({\bm{x}}^{\prime}+{\bm{X}}^{\prime}_{\tau}\right)|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\right)\left[\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\right]\;,

(5.8)

where the operator
L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}=\zeta^{\prime i}\partial/\partial x^{\prime i}
. The Jacobian of the coordinate transformation in the integral of Eq. (
5.7
) is

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left[\frac{\partial y^{\prime i}}{\partial x^{\prime j}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left[\delta_{ij}+A_{ij}\right]=\exp\left[-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n}A_{i_{1}i_{2}}A_{i_{2}i_{3}}\ldots A_{i_{n}i_{1}}\right]\;,

(5.9)

where the repeated indices imply Einstein’s summation over three coordinates, and the matrix

\displaystyle A_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X^{\prime i}_{\tau}}{\partial x^{\prime j}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\prime j}}\left[L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}^{n-1}\zeta^{\prime i}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right]\;.

(5.10)

Directly computing the matrix
A_{ij}
in the coordinates
x^{\prime i}
requires the introduction of multivariate tensorial Bell polynomials
[
94
]
, extending those introduced in Appendix Eq. (
A.5
). However, calculating the determinant (
5.9
) with these multivariate polynomials is a formidable task. Fortunately, there is an alternative, more elegant approach utilizing the invariance of the determinant under coordinate transformations. We choose the orientation of the coordinates such that at the tangent space of the point
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
, the generator
{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}
of the Lie group is aligned along, say, the
x^{\prime 1}
axis, that is
\zeta^{\prime i}=(\zeta^{\prime 1},0,0)
, where
\zeta^{\prime 1}=\zeta^{\prime 1}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
. In such a coordinate system, the matrix
A_{ij}
has only three components different from zero, which significantly simplifies the calculation of the determinant (
5.9
). The result is:

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left[\frac{\partial y^{\prime i}}{\partial x^{\prime j}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}1+A_{11}&A_{12}&A_{13}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right|=1+A_{11}\;.

Thus, the calculation of the determinant is reduced to the calculation of a single element
A_{11}
of the matrix
A_{ij}
. Direct calculation of
A_{11}
relies upon the application of the Faà di Bruno formula
[
88
]
for the derivatives of a composite function and results in:

\displaystyle A_{11}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\prime 1}}\left[L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}^{n-1}\zeta^{\prime 1}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}{\bf B}_{n}\left(\theta^{\prime},L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\theta^{\prime},L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}^{2}\theta^{\prime},...,L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}^{n-1}\theta^{\prime}\right)\;,

(5.15)

where
\theta^{\prime}:=\partial\zeta^{\prime 1}/\partial x^{\prime 1}
is the divergence of the vector field
\zeta^{\prime i}
, and the multi-argument function

\displaystyle{\bf B}_{n}\left(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=1}^{n}{\bf B}_{n,p}\left(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n-p+1}\right)\;,

(5.16)

is the complete Bell polynomial
[
95
]
. Using the generating function of the complete Bell polynomials
[
95
]
, the final result for the determinant (
5.2
) is:

\displaystyle{\rm det}\left[\frac{\partial y^{\prime i}}{\partial x^{\prime j}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left[\frac{\exp\bigl(\tau L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)-1}{L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}}\theta^{\prime}\right]\;.

(5.17)

The value of the determinant does not depend on the choice of the coordinate system. Therefore, the formula (
5.17
) for the determinant remains exactly the same in the original coordinates, with
\theta^{\prime}=\partial\zeta^{\prime i}/\partial x^{\prime i}
representing the divergence of the vector flow with the generator
{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}={\bm{\zeta}}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
at the point
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
.

Now, we substitute Eqs. (
5.8
) and (
5.17
) into integral in the right hand-side of Eq. (
5.7
). It yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{\cal V}\exp\left[\frac{\exp\bigl(\tau L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)-1}{L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}}\theta^{\prime}\right]\exp\bigl(\tau L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)\Biggl[\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\Biggr]d^{3}x^{\prime}\;.

(5.18)

The product of two exponential functions in the integrand of (
5.18
) cannot be immediately simplified because the arguments of the exponents are elements of the Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{g}\mapsto{\mathfrak{M}}
, and they do not commute. Therefore, we need to employ the BCH formula
[
93
]
, which provides a method to express the product of two non-commuting exponential operators. We have found that the most optimal approach is to use the BCH formula in its Zassenhaus form
[
96
]
. By computing the commutators of the arguments of the exponentials in Eq. (
5.18
) as prescribed by the Zassenhaus formula
[
96
]
, we find out that the product of the two exponential operators in Eq. (
5.18
) reduces to the exponential Lie derivative:

\displaystyle\exp\left[\frac{\exp\bigl(\tau L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)-1}{L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}}\theta^{\prime}\right]\exp\bigl(\tau L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\bigl(-\tau\pounds_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)\;,

(5.19)

where the action of the operator of the Lie derivative is understood as
\pounds_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})=-\partial(\zeta^{\prime i}f({\bm{x}}^{\prime}))/\partial x^{\prime i}=-\left(L_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}+\theta^{\prime}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})\right)f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
for any scalar density
f({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
of weight +1
[
97
,
80
]
.
Using Eq. (
5.19
) we write Eq. (
5.18
) for the perturbed potential in a more simple form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{\cal V}\exp\bigl(-\tau\pounds_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)\Biggl[\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\Biggr]d^{3}x^{\prime}

(5.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{\cal V}\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}d^{3}x^{\prime}+G\int\limits_{\cal V}\Bigl[\exp\bigl(-\tau\pounds_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)-1\Bigr]\Biggl[\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\Biggr]d^{3}x^{\prime}\;.

Subsequent calculations are conducted in the radial gauge in which
{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}=\zeta^{\prime}{\bm{n}}^{\prime}
,
X^{\prime}_{\tau}=X^{\prime}_{\tau}{\bm{n}}^{\prime}
, and
X^{\prime}_{\tau}=X_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
, where
{\bm{n}}^{\prime}
is a unit vector in the direction of vector
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}=r^{\prime}{\bm{n}}^{\prime}
. We shall also use spherical coordinates for calculations. It turns out that the integrand with the exponential map on the right-hand side of formula (
5.20
) can be expressed in terms of the finite translation
X_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})
as follows:

\displaystyle\Bigl[\exp\bigl(-\tau\pounds_{{\bm{\zeta}}^{\prime}}\bigr)-1\Bigr]\Biggl[\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{\prime 2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r^{\prime}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial r^{\prime n}}\Biggl[\frac{r^{\prime 2}\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}\Biggr]\;,

(5.21)

where we have used the equivariance formula (
A.1
) and its Taylor expansion analogue in Eq. (
A.11
).

Substituting Eq. (
5.21
) into the right-hand side of Eq. (
5.20
) and integrating the term with the total divergence in spherical coordinates yields:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{\cal V}\frac{\rho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}+G\oint\limits_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}d^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}}_{a})\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}_{\tau}({\bm{a}})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\left[\frac{a^{2}\rho_{\tau}({\bm{a}})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{a}}|}\right]\;,

(5.22)

where functions
X_{\tau}({\bm{a}})
and
\rho_{\tau}({\bm{a}})
are evaluated on the surface of the reference configuration, a sphere of radius
r=a
. Here,
X_{\tau}({\bm{a}})\equiv X_{\tau}(a,\theta_{a},\varphi_{a})
,
\rho_{\tau}({\bm{a}})\equiv\rho_{\tau}(a,\theta_{a},\varphi_{a})
,
{\bm{a}}=a{\bm{n}}_{a}
, and
{\bm{n}}_{a}=(\sin\theta_{a}\cos\varphi_{a},\sin\theta_{a}\sin\varphi_{a},\cos\theta_{a})
is the unit vector in the direction of the integration point on the unit sphere. The solid angle element is
d^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}}_{a})=\sin\theta_{a}d\theta_{a}d\varphi_{a}
, and the surface integral is over the unit sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{2}:\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;,\;|{\bm{x}}|=1\}
. The correctness of Eq. (
5.22
) can alternatively be verified using only the Taylor series, without invoking the exponential operator or the Lie series. This verification of Eq. (
5.22
) is presented in Appendix
B
.

Equations (
5.5
) and (
5.22
) yield the same result for the perturbed gravitational potential
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
both inside and outside the fluid volume. However, Eq. (
5.22
) offers a distinct mathematical advantage for calculations, as it involves integration over a known, spherically symmetric domain
{\mathcal{V}}
of the fluid’s reference configuration. The surface integral in Eq. (
5.22
) naturally accounts for the gravitational field contribution from the fluid contained within the domain between the deformed volume
{\cal V}_{\tau}
and the spherical volume
{\mathcal{V}}
. From the perspective of differential equations, the surface integral in Eq. (
5.22
) solves the homogeneous Laplace equation and is crucial for formulating boundary conditions to match solutions for the gravitational potential inside and outside the spherical surface of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
.

Hubbard
[
98
]
observed that the perturbed potential could be presented as a superposition of contributions from volume and surface integrals in the specific case of a polytrope with a unit index. Eq. (
5.22
) extends this observation to rotating fluid bodies with an arbitrary barotropic equation of state.

5.3
Poisson Equation for Total Gravitational Perturbation

Let’s revisit the effective potential
U
defined in Eq. (
2.21
) and examine its perturbation. We define the perturbed effective potential
U_{\tau}
as a map
\phi_{\tau}:{\mathcal{U}}\to U_{\tau}
, given by:

\displaystyle U_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}+W_{R}\;.

(5.23)

According to Eq. (
3.23
), the Eulerian variation of the potential
W_{R}
is zero. Thus, the perturbed effective potential
U_{\tau}
is expressed by the exponential mapping:

\displaystyle U_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\right)U({\bm{x}})\;,

(5.24)

where
U({\bm{x}})
is given by Eq. (
2.21
).
This mapping can be represented in the form of the integral accounting for the Eulerian variation of the fluid density
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
within the volume
{\mathcal{V}}
and the surface integral accounting for the variation caused by the deformation of the volume
\phi_{\tau}:{\mathcal{V}}\mapsto{\mathcal{V}}_{\tau}
. More explicitly, we use Eq. (
2.2
) for
{\mathcal{U}}
and Eq. (
5.22
) for
{\mathcal{U}}_{\tau}
, to represent the finite Eulerian variation of the effective potential
U
as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\exp\left(\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\right)-1\right]U({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int\limits_{\cal V}\frac{\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}+G\oint\limits_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}d^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}}_{a})\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}_{\tau}({\bm{a}})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\left[\frac{a^{2}\rho_{\tau}({\bm{a}})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{a}}|}\right]\;,

(5.25)

where the perturbation of the density
\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
is given by the exponential mapping (
4.14
).

To denote the overall value of the gravitational field at point
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we use:

\displaystyle V_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})+K_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\;.

(5.26)

Here,
K_{\tau}
represents the perturbation of the gravitational field, comprising the perturbation of the effective potential
U
of the body and the external quadrupole perturbation
W_{Q}
. It is defined as:

\displaystyle K_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left[\exp\left(\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\right)-1\right]U({\bm{x}})+\tau W_{Q}({\bm{x}})=\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}})+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}{\mathcal{U}}({\bm{x}})+\ldots+\tau W_{Q}({\bm{x}})\;.

(5.27)

Applying the Laplace operator to both sides of Eq. (
5.27
) and considering Eqs. (
2.2
), (
2.13
), (
5.22
), along with the Green’s function of the Laplace equation, we find that the perturbation
K_{\tau}
satisfies the following Poisson equation:

\displaystyle\Delta K_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\left(\varrho_{\tau}+\nu_{\tau}\right)\;,

(5.28)

where
\nu_{\tau}=\nu_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
denotes the density of the surface layer localized on the spherical shell of radius
a
. This surface density is given by:

\displaystyle\nu_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}_{\tau}({\bm{a}})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\left[\frac{a^{2}}{r^{2}}\rho_{\tau}({\bm{a}})\delta(r-a)\right]\;.

(5.29)

where
\delta(r-a)
denotes the Dirac delta function. We emphasize that functions standing in the right-hand side of the Poisson equation (
5.28
) are defined on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
corresponding to the reference configuration.

The emergence of a surface layer density in the Poisson equation (
5.28
) is a purely mathematical artifact resulting from the transformation of the integration domain from the perturbed physical volume
{\cal V}_{\tau}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
to the reference volume
{\mathcal{V}}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
. Specifically, this artifact arises when the integral in Eq. (
5.5
) is reformulated by changing coordinates from
{\bm{y}}
on the perturbed manifold
\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
back to
{\bm{x}}
on the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
. Such a surface term does not appear in the original formulation of the Poisson equation, where the density is supported over the actual perturbed volume
{\cal V}_{\tau}
of the fluid body. Crucially, the contribution of the surface layer originates primarily from the radial derivatives of the fluid density at the boundary of the fluid body. These contributions are particularly significant in determining the figure of a rotating fluid body, where surface gradients play a central role in shaping the equilibrium configuration.

To determine the perturbation of the gravitational field
K_{\tau}
by solving the Poisson equation (
5.28
), we utilize the Green function of the Laplace equation convoluted with the volume density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
, integrating it over the unperturbed, spherically symmetric volume
{\mathcal{V}}
of the fluid body’s reference configuration (calculated in the previous step). The contribution of the surface-layer density (
5.29
) is derived from a solution of the Laplace equation and must be incorporated into the boundary conditions imposed on the perturbation
K_{\tau}
in both the internal and external regions of the spherical volume
{\mathcal{V}}
. The boundary value problem is detailed in Section
8
.

The results in this section clarify and reconcile various methods used by researchers to solve for the gravitational field perturbation
K_{\tau}
. Our approach, utilizing the Lie group of diffeomorphisms, shows that the integration domain in the Poisson equation (
5.28
) for
K_{\tau}
can be reduced to the spherically symmetric volume
{\mathcal{V}}
of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
using the pullback transformation of coordinates of the level surfaces. This method is valid as long as the infinite power series defining the perturbations are convergent. Determining the precise convergence domain of these series is a separate mathematical problem not addressed in this paper.

5.4
Nonlinear Interaction between Fluid Density and Gravitational Field Perturbations

In Section
3.6
, we demonstrated that, under the linear approximation, the Poisson equation (
3.39
) for the gravitational field perturbation
{\cal K}
can be transformed into the Helmholtz equation (
3.40
) for the same perturbation. This transformation is made possible by the linear coupling between the infinitesimal perturbations of the density and the gravitational field, as expressed in Eq. (
3.37
). Extending this result to the nonlinear regime of perturbation theory, the underlying coupling mechanism remains valid; however, the corresponding coupling equation becomes highly nonlinear. In this section, we derive the nonlinear coupling equation that establishes the mathematical relationship between the finite gravitational perturbation
K_{\tau}
and the finite density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
.

To begin, we restate Eq. (
3.37
) in a form more suitable for further calculations. Using Eqs. (
3.10
) and (
3.31
), we express the ratio
U^{\prime}/\rho^{\prime}
of the radial derivatives of the effective potential
U
and the fluid density
\rho
as:

\displaystyle\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\;.

(5.30)

Here, the fluid density
\rho
is treated as an independent radial variable, with
U
and
p
considered as functions of
\rho
, i.e.,
U=U(\rho)
and
p=p(\rho)
. Substituting Eq. (
5.30
) into Eq. (
3.37
) transforms it into:

\displaystyle\delta_{\bm{\xi}}U+W_{Q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal A}\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;,

(5.31)

where we use a definition of
{\cal K}
from Eq. (
3.29
) and introduce the shorthand notation:

\displaystyle{\cal A}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\;,

(5.32)

for the pressure derivative with respect to the fluid density
\rho
. Naturally,
{\cal A}={\cal A}(\rho)
is a function of density, and its explicit form is determined by the barotropic equation of state,
p=p(\rho)
.

Next, we apply the exponential operator to both sides of Eq. (
5.31
) and account for the vanishing Eulerian variation of the external perturbation
W_{Q}
(see Eq. (
3.23
)). Consequently, applying the exponential operator to both sides of Eq. (
5.31
) yields:

\displaystyle K_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}-1}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]\;,

(5.33)

where the gravitational perturbation
K_{\tau}
is defined in Eq. (
5.27
). Equation (
5.33
) establishes a functional relationship between
K_{\tau}
and the infinitesimal variation of the fluid density
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
.

We can further develop the right-hand side of Eq. (
5.33
) using the Taylor series expansion of the exponential operator:

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}-1}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\,\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]\;,

(5.34)

where
\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]
indicates that the Eulerian variation operator
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}
is applied
n
times to the product
{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
. This expansion allows us to systematically account for higher-order perturbations, facilitating further transformations and analysis of the coupling equation.

In the next step, we use the fact that the Eulerian variation of fluid density is a linear operator of the variational derivative
[
80
]
. This allows us to employ the binomial Leibniz rule for derivatives
[
99
]
to compute the
n
-th order variation of the product of two functions:

\displaystyle\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\left[\delta_{\bm{\xi}}^{n-k}{\cal A}(\rho)\right]\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\;.

(5.35)

Substituting Eq. (
5.35
) into Eq. (
5.34
) yields:

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}-1}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+k+1}}{n+k+1}\frac{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}^{n}{\cal A}(\rho)}{n!}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}\;.

(5.36)

The function
{\cal A}(\rho)
defined in (
5.32
) is composite, and computing its
n
-th order variation is laborious. This variation can be computed using the Faà di Bruno formula in Riordan’s form:
[
88
]
,

\displaystyle\delta^{n}_{\bm{\xi}}{\cal A}(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{n}{\bf B}_{n,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n-p+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)\,\partial^{p}_{\rho}{\cal A}(\rho)\;,

(5.37)

where
\partial_{\rho}=\partial/\partial\rho
, and
{\bf B}_{n,p}\left(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{n-p+1}\right)
is the incomplete Bell polynomial
[
95
]
with arguments
x_{j}\equiv\delta^{j}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, similar to that defined in Eq. (
A.5
) for the scalar case. Substituting Eq. (
5.37
) into Eq. (
5.36
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}-1}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{p=0}^{n}\frac{\tau^{n+k+1}}{n+k+1}\frac{\partial^{p}_{\rho}{\cal A}(\rho)}{n!}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}{\bf B}_{n,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n-p+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)\;.

(5.38)

This formula, along with Eq. (
5.33
), defines the overall perturbation of the gravitational potential as:

\displaystyle K_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+p+k+1}}{n+k+p+1}\frac{\partial^{p}_{\rho}{\cal A}(\rho)}{(n+p)!}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}{\bf B}_{n+p,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)\;,

(5.39)

where we have exchanged the order of summation compared to (
5.38
).

The right-hand side of Eq. (
5.39
) can be simplified and expressed solely in terms of the total variation (
4.14
) of density
\varrho_{\tau}
. To achieve this, consider the sum in (
5.39
) for a fixed value of the index
p
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+p+k+1}}{(n+k+p+1)}\frac{{\bf B}_{n+p,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)}{(n+p)!}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}=

(5.40)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\Biggl[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\bf B}_{n+p,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)}{(n+p)!}\Biggr]\Biggl[\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+p+k+1}}{n+k+p+1}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}\Biggr]\;.

To facilitate the calculation of the product of two sums on the right-hand side of Eq. (
5.40
), we express the sum with respect to the index
k
as an integral:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+p+k+1}}{n+k+p+1}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}=\Biggl[\int_{-\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}^{0}\left(-\frac{t}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)^{n+p}e^{-t}dt\Biggr]\rho\;.

(5.41)

Assuming that the operations of summation and integration commute (because the series are assumed to be convergent), we use the definition of the generating function for the Bell polynomials
[
95
]
to obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{{\bf B}_{n+p,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)}{(n+p)!}\left(-\frac{t}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)^{n+p}\,=\,\sum_{n=p}^{\infty}\frac{{\bf B}_{n,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n-p+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)}{n!}\left(-\frac{t}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)^{n}\,=

(5.42)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\frac{1}{p!}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{t}{\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}\right)^{j}\frac{\delta^{j}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{j!}\right]^{p}=\frac{1}{p!}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(-t\right)^{j}}{j!}\rho\right]^{p}=\frac{1}{p!}\left[\left(e^{-t}-1\right)\rho\right]^{p}\;.

What remains is to perform the integral with respect to
t
, which yields the sum on the left-hand side of Eq. (
5.40
) in the following form:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+p+k+1}}{(n+k+p+1)}\frac{{\bf B}_{n+p,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)}{(n+p)!}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p!}\left\{\int_{-\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}^{0}\left[\left(e^{-t}-1\right)\rho\right]^{p}e^{-t}dt\right\}\rho

(5.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(p+1)!}\Bigl[\left(e^{\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}}}-1\right)\rho\Bigr]^{p+1}=\frac{\varrho_{\tau}^{p+1}}{(p+1)!}\;,

where we have used the definition of
\varrho_{\tau}
given in Eq. (
4.14
) to obtain the final term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
5.43
).

Finally, we perform the summation with respect to the index
p
in the triple sum in (
5.39
) and obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\tau^{n+p+k+1}}{n+k+p+1}\frac{\partial^{p}_{\rho}{\cal A}(\rho)}{(n+p)!}\frac{\delta^{k+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{k!}{\bf B}_{n+p,p}\left(\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,\delta^{2}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho,...,\delta^{n+1}_{\bm{\xi}}\rho\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{\tau}^{p+1}}{(p+1)!}\partial^{p}_{\rho}{\cal A}(\rho)\;.

(5.44)

Thus, the functional equation (
5.33
) for the gravitational perturbation
K_{\tau}
takes its final form as a Taylor series with respect to the total density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
. Specifically,

\displaystyle K_{\tau}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{\tau}^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\partial_{\rho}^{n}{\cal A}(\rho)=\varrho_{\tau}\left[{\cal A}(\rho)+\frac{\varrho_{\tau}}{2!}\partial_{\rho}{\cal A}(\rho)+\frac{\varrho_{\tau}^{2}}{3!}\partial_{\rho}^{2}{\cal A}(\rho)+...\right]\;,

(5.45)

where the function
{\cal A}={\cal A}(\rho)
is defined in Eq. (
5.32
) as the derivative of pressure.

Although calculating the gravitational field perturbation
K_{\tau}
in Eq. (
5.45
) might seem to require explicit knowledge of the equation of state
p=p(\rho)
, we can bypass this by using the hydrostatic equilibrium equation (
5.30
):

\displaystyle{\cal A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\;.

(5.46)

This indicates that the function
{\cal A}
can be determined if the unperturbed gravitational potential
U=U(r)
and the density
\rho=\rho(r)
are known.

In the unperturbed fluid configuration, density
\rho
and radial coordinate
r
are inverse functions, such that
\rho^{-1}(\rho(r))=r
. Consequently, partial derivatives of the function
{\cal A}
with respect to density can be expressed in terms of radial coordinate derivatives using the operator equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}=\frac{1}{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\;,

(5.47)

where
\rho^{\prime}=d\rho/dr
represents the radial derivative of density. This method enables direct calculation of
K_{\tau}
in terms of the total density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
, along with the radial derivatives of the unperturbed density
\rho
and the effective gravitational potential
U={\mathcal{U}}+W_{R}
.

5.5
Nonlinear Molodensky Equation for Gravitational Perturbations

The field equation (
5.28
) for the strongly perturbed gravitational potential
K_{\tau}
is determined by the total variation of density
\varrho_{\tau}
. Previously, we showed that applying the equation of hydrostatic equilibrium to perturbations allows the gravitational field perturbation
K_{\tau}
to be expressed as an infinite series (
5.45
) of the density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
. Substituting this series into equation (
5.28
) results in a non-linear differential equation for the density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
, which can be solved iteratively. More effectively, however, is to work with the gravitational field perturbation
K_{\tau}
.

To derive a differential equation for
K_{\tau}
, we need to invert Eq. (
5.45
) using the Lagrange method of inversion of power series
[
100
]
. First, we rewrite (
5.45
) as follows:

\displaystyle K_{\tau}=\sum_{{n}=1}^{\infty}\frac{h_{n}}{{n}!}\varrho_{\tau}^{n}\;,\qquad\qquad h_{n}=\partial_{\rho}^{{n}-1}{\cal A}\;.

(5.48)

Applying the Lagrange method to invert Eq. (
5.48
), we express
\varrho_{\tau}
as a power series of the gravitational potential perturbation
K_{\tau}
:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{g_{n}}{{n}!}\left(\frac{K_{\tau}}{h_{1}}\right)^{n}=\frac{K_{\tau}}{{\cal A}}+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{g_{n}}{{n}!}\left(\frac{K_{\tau}}{{\cal A}}\right)^{n}\;,

(5.49)

where
g_{1}=1
and the other coefficients

\displaystyle g_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{{n}-1}(-1)^{k}({n})_{k}{\bf B}_{{n}-1,k}\left(c_{1},c_{2},...,c_{{n}-k}\right)\;,\qquad\qquad({n}\geq 2)

(5.50)

are given in terms of the incomplete Bell polynomials
{\bf B}_{{n}-1,k}\left(c_{1},...,c_{{n}-k}\right)
with arguments

\displaystyle c_{k}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k+1}\frac{h_{k+1}}{h_{1}}=\frac{1}{k+1}\frac{\partial_{\rho}^{k}{\cal A}}{{\cal A}}\;,

(5.51)

and

\displaystyle({n})_{k}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{n}({n}+1)...({n}+k-1)=\frac{\Gamma({n}+k)}{\Gamma({n})}\;,

(5.52)

is the Pochhammer symbol, also known as the
rising
factorial
[
101
]
, and
\Gamma(n)
is the gamma function
[
92
]
.

The coefficients
g_{n}
depend solely on the unperturbed (spherically-symmetric) fluid density
\rho=\rho(r)
and its radial derivatives, which are determined by solving the equations in Section
2
that define the body’s reference configuration. The first few coefficients
g_{n}

(n\geq 2)
are given by:

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial_{\rho}A}{{\cal A}}\;,

(5.53)

\displaystyle g_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3(\partial_{\rho}{\cal A})^{2}-{\cal A}\partial_{\rho}^{2}{\cal A}}{{\cal A}^{2}}\;,

(5.54)

\displaystyle g_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{15(\partial_{\rho}{\cal A})^{3}-10{\cal A}\partial_{\rho}{\cal A}\partial_{\rho}^{2}{\cal A}+{\cal A}^{2}\partial_{\rho}^{3}{\cal A}}{{\cal A}^{3}}\;,

(5.55)

and so forth.

The partial derivatives of the function
{\cal A}={\cal A}(\rho)
with respect to the density
\rho
are computed using formula (
5.47
), for example:

\displaystyle{\cal{\cal A}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\;,

(5.56)

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}{\cal{\cal A}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{\rho^{\prime}U^{\prime\prime}-U^{\prime}\rho^{\prime\prime}}{\rho^{\prime 3}}\;,

(5.57)

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\rho}{\cal A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dr}\left[\frac{1}{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}\right)\right]=\frac{3\rho^{\prime\prime 2}U^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime 2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}-\rho^{\prime}\left(3\rho^{\prime\prime}U^{\prime\prime}+U^{\prime}\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}\right)}{\rho^{\prime 5}}\;,

(5.58)

and so on.

For the polytropic equation of state,
p\sim\rho^{1+1/n}
(where
n
is the polytropic index), the function
{\cal A}
, its partial derivatives, and the coefficients
g_{n}
in Eq. (
5.49
) can be directly computed from the thermodynamic equation (
5.32
). This case is discussed in Appendix
F
.

For small deformations of the fluid body, the density perturbation varies linearly with the perturbation in the gravitational field, as described by Eq. (
5.31
). However, as the deformation increases, the density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
enters a nonlinear regime, becoming increasingly influenced by higher-order gravitational field perturbations, denoted by
K_{\tau}
. This relationship is captured by an infinite power series expansion, given in Eq. (
5.49
).

By substituting Eq. (
5.49
) into Eq. (
5.28
), the explicit dependence on the volume density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
is eliminated, yielding a fully nonlinear equation governing the gravitational field perturbation within the interior of the body:

\displaystyle\Delta K_{\tau}+4\pi G\frac{K_{\tau}}{{\cal A}}+4\pi G\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{g_{n}}{{n}!}\left(\frac{K_{\tau}}{{\cal A}}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,\qquad\qquad\qquad(r<a)\;.

(5.59)

In this formulation, the surface density
\nu(x)
is omitted, as the analysis is restricted to the region strictly within the body’s boundary, where
r<a
.

In the vacuum region outside the body, the general equation (
5.28
) reduces to the Laplace equation:

\displaystyle\Delta K_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,\qquad\qquad\qquad(r>a)\;.

(5.60)

This linear form is consistent with the superposition principle in Newtonian gravity, which states that the total gravitational potential in a vacuum is the sum of the potentials due to individual sources.

Equation (
5.59
) generalizes the linearized Molodensky equation (
3.40
) to account for finite, non-linear perturbations in both density and the gravitational field. Notably, it reveals a compelling phenomenon: the gravitational field perturbation
K_{\tau}
exhibits self-interaction through non-linear terms. At first glance, this may appear to contradict the linear nature of Newtonian gravity, which is governed by the superposition principle. However, this principle strictly applies only in vacuum, where the gravitational potential satisfies the Laplace equation (
5.60
). Within matter, the gravitational field obeys the non-linear equation (
5.59
).

When the system is subjected to a sufficiently strong external perturbation
W_{Q}
– such as that induced by rapid rotation – it generates a significant density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
, which is non-linearly coupled to
K_{\tau}
via Eq. (
5.49
). As a result, the fluid’s response to gravitational perturbations becomes inherently non-linear. This behavior is analogous to non-linear electrodynamics, where strong electromagnetic fields interacting with matter lead to non-linear corrections to Maxwell’s equations
[
102
]
.

The non-linear terms in Eq. (
5.59
) may be empirically tested by analyzing the gravitational field of rapidly rotating bodies such as Jupiter, whose gravitational inhomogeneities are now measured with exceptional precision
[
70
]
. These investigations are closely tied to the study of rotational deformation, Love numbers, and the multipole structure of the external gravitational field – topics that are explored in the subsequent sections.

6
Nonlinear Analysis of Rotational Deformations in Fluid Bodies

This section develops an exact theoretical framework for determining the shape of rotating fluid bodies, grounded in the mathematical structure of the Lie group of diffeomorphisms and the exponential mapping of gravitational and hydrostatic perturbations. Within this formalism, the classical hydrostatic equilibrium equations are reformulated as functional equations for the fluid density and gravitational field. This approach establishes a direct and intrinsic link between the geometry of equipotential surfaces and the underlying gravitational perturbations.

By substituting the solutions of these functional equations into the Poisson equations governing gravitational perturbations, one derives a fundamental nonlinear partial differential equation for the height function generalizing the Molodensky equation (
3.49
). This equation enables, in principle, the exact determination of the deformation of level surfaces and the body’s shape as functions of angular velocity, internal density distribution, and the equation of state – without relying on perturbative or approximation methods.

This exact theory represents a substantial advancement over traditional approximation-based models. It provides mathematically rigorous insights into the relationship between the body’s internal structure – quantified by Love numbers – and the multipole moments of its external gravitational field. Furthermore, it supports high-precision predictions across a broad spectrum of physical conditions and offers a robust framework for assessing the accuracy of approximate computational models derived from observational data, by enabling the explicit computation of residual terms.

6.1
Functional Equation Approach to the Geometry of Level Surfaces

6.1.1
Functional Equation for Level Surfaces in Rotating Fluid Bodies

The base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
of the fluid reference configuration initially features spherical level surfaces parameterized by the radial coordinate
r\in[0,a]
. Applying an external quadrupole potential
W_{Q}
induces rotational perturbations, deforming the manifold into a new configuration
{\mathfrak{M}}\mapsto\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
with non-spherical level surfaces. This deformation is described by the diffeomorphism (
5.2
), mapping each point
x^{i}\in{\mathfrak{M}}
to
x^{i}+X^{i}_{\tau}\in\mathfrak{N}_{\tau}
. The finite displacement vector
X^{i}_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\in{\mathfrak{G}}
arises from the transformation generated by an infinitesimal element
\zeta^{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
of the Lie algebra via the exponential map (
5.3
).

This section derives an exact functional relationship between
X^{i}_{\tau}
and the gravitational perturbation
K_{\tau}
, assuming the latter is known from the solution of the nonlinear Molodensky equation (
5.59
). The resulting equation captures the correspondence between the deformation of the level surfaces and the magnitude of the gravitational perturbation.

Calculations are performed in the radial gauge, where both the fluid diffeomorphism generator
{\bm{\xi}}=\xi{\bm{n}}
and the level surface generator
{\bm{\zeta}}=\zeta{\bm{n}}
possess only radial components, with
\xi=\xi({\bm{x}})
and
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
. This gauge significantly simplifies the derivation of functional equations linking Eulerian variations in matter and the gravitational field to the geometric deformation of level surfaces.

We begin by establishing the relationship between the fluid and level surface diffeomorphism generators,
{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{\zeta}}
. To facilitate this, we adopt the unperturbed density
\rho=\rho(r)
as a proxy for the radial coordinate
r
. This choice is motivated by the fact that, in hydrostatic equilibrium, the level surfaces of the body correspond to isodensity shells, naturally parameterized by the fluid density. Recasting the radial coordinate in terms of
\rho
amounts to transitioning from spherical coordinates
(r,\theta,\varphi)
to isopycnal coordinates
(\rho,\theta,\varphi)
, a common framework in fluid dynamics
[
103
,
104
]
.

We introduce the notation for the effective gravitational potential
U(r(\rho)):=\mathsf{U}(\rho)
and express Eq. (
5.46
) in terms of
\mathsf{U}
:

\displaystyle{\cal A}(\rho)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{U^{\prime}}{\rho^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial{\mathsf{U}}}{\partial\rho}\;.

(6.1)

Substituting this expression for
{\cal A}(\rho)
into Eq. (
5.45
) yields:

\displaystyle K_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{\tau}^{n}}{n!}\frac{\partial^{n}{\mathsf{U}}}{\partial\rho^{n}}\;,

(6.2)

which represents a Taylor expansion of the gravitational perturbation
K_{\tau}=K_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
about the point
(\rho,\theta,\varphi)
on a level surface of constant density
\rho
, with respect to the finite Eulerian density variation
\varrho_{\tau}=\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
.
This expansion is generated by the following functional expression:

\displaystyle K_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathsf{U}}(\rho+\varrho_{\tau})-{\mathsf{U}}(\rho)\;.

(6.3)

According to the equivariance theorem established in Appendix
A
, the function
\mathsf{U}(\rho+\varrho_{\tau})
can be expressed via the exponential map:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{U}}(\rho+\varrho_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau\chi\partial_{\rho}\right){\mathsf{U}}(\rho)\;,

(6.4)

where the generator
\chi=\chi({\bm{x}})
is related to the variation
\varrho_{\tau}=\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
through:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\exp\left(\tau\chi\partial_{\rho}\right)-1\right]\rho\;.

(6.5)

The generator
\chi=\chi({\bm{x}})
can now be identified by noting that the density variation
\varrho_{\tau}
is also expressed via the exponential operator (
4.14
), involving the fluid diffeomorphism generator
\delta_{\bm{\xi}}
. Equating this with the exponential form in Eq. (
6.5
) yields the operator identity:
\chi\partial_{\rho}=\delta_{\bm{\xi}}
, valid in the radial gauge. Applying this identity to any smooth function of
\rho
, such as the pressure
p=p(\rho)
or the effective gravitational potential
\mathsf{U}=\mathsf{U}(\rho)
, gives the explicit form of the generator:
\chi=\delta_{\bm{\xi}}\rho=\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
.

We now return from the isopycnal density variable
\rho=\rho(r)
to the spherical radial coordinate
r=r(\rho)
. To describe the radial displacement of level surfaces, we introduce the generator of infinitesimal radial translations,
\zeta^{i}=\zeta n^{i}
, where
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
is defined via the correspondence
\chi\partial_{\rho}=-\zeta\partial_{r}
. Using the identity
\partial_{\rho}=(1/\rho^{\prime})\partial_{r}
, this yields
\zeta=-\chi/\rho^{\prime}
, where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
r
. Recalling that
\chi=\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho
, we obtain the explicit form of the generator of the infinitesimal radial translation of the level surface:

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pounds_{\bm{\xi}}\rho}{\rho^{\prime}}=\xi+\frac{\rho}{\rho^{\prime}}\left(\xi^{\prime}+\frac{2\xi}{r}\right)\;.

(6.6)

This expression coincides with the generator
\zeta
appearing in Eq. (
3.45
) in the linearized perturbation regime discussed in Section
3.7
. It shows that, in general, for a compressible fluid, the generator
{\bm{\zeta}}
of radial level surface displacements differs from the fluid diffeomorphism generator
{\bm{\xi}}
responsible for density variations. This discrepancy arises due to fluid compressibility, which introduces a non-zero divergence
\partial_{i}\xi^{i}=\xi^{\prime}+2\xi/r
of the vector field
{\bm{\xi}}
. In the incompressible fluid limit, where
\partial_{i}\xi^{i}=0
, the two generators are equal:
{\bm{\xi}}={\bm{\zeta}}
.

Our analysis shows that, under large deformations, the radial displacement
X_{\tau}
of a level surface – initially spherical with radius
r
– is governed by an exponential map generated by
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
. In hydrostatic equilibrium, where the level surfaces of density and gravitational potential coincide, this same exponential map describes variations in both fields:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\exp\left(-\tau L_{\bm{\zeta}}\right)-1\right]\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.7)

\displaystyle K_{\tau}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[\exp\left(-\tau L_{\bm{\zeta}}\right)-1\bigr]U({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.8)

where the linear operator
L_{\bm{\zeta}}=\zeta^{i}\partial_{i}=\zeta\partial_{r}
was introduced in Eq. (
5.3
).

Equations (
6.7
) and (
6.8
) represent pullback diffeomorphisms. It is more convenient to work with their pushforward counterparts, expressed as:

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau L_{\bm{\zeta}}\right)\left[\rho({\bm{x}})+\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.9)

\displaystyle\exp\left(\tau L_{\bm{\zeta}}\right)\left[U({\bm{x}})+K_{\tau}({\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.10)

These functional equations describe finite deformations of level surfaces through the exponential map. However, it is more practical to express them in terms of the finite radial displacement of a level surface, represented by the height function
X_{\tau}=X_{\tau}({\bm{x}})
. This function is related to the infinitesimal generator of radial translations,
\zeta=\zeta({\bm{x}})
, via the exponential map defined in Eq. (
5.4
). By invoking the equivariance property of Lie group actions, as detailed in Appendix
A
, Eqs. (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) can be reformulated in terms of the height function, yielding the following functional equations:

\displaystyle\rho({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}_{\tau})+\varrho_{\tau}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.11)

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}_{\tau})+K_{\tau}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}_{\tau})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.12)

These equations determine the height function
X_{\tau}
in terms of either the density perturbation
\varrho_{\tau}
or the gravitational field perturbation
K_{\tau}
within the body. They form the foundation for deriving the differential equation that governs the shape of the deformed level surfaces.

Functional equations (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) describe a homotopic, continuous transformation of the density and gravitational potential, parameterized by
\tau\in[0,1]
. These equations assert that the values of the density and gravitational potential at a given level surface and point
{\bm{x}}
remain invariant as the surface undergoes a radial displacement to the point
{\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}_{\tau}
, driven by the external perturbation
\tau W_{Q}({\bm{x}})
. A similar conservation principle applies to the fluid pressure. Equation (
6.12
) generalizes the fundamental relation (
1.7
) that defines level surfaces in the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory
[
2
]
.

From this point forward, we consider finite perturbations of the fluid body, characterized by the exponential mappings
\phi_{\tau}(\bm{\xi})=\exp(\tau\delta_{\bm{\xi}})
and
\phi_{\tau}(\bm{\zeta})=\exp(\tau\delta_{\bm{\zeta}})
, evaluated at the homotopy parameter
\tau=1
. For notational simplicity, we omit the subscript
\tau=1
and adopt the following conventions:
\varrho\equiv\varrho_{1}({\bm{x}})
,
K\equiv K_{1}({\bm{x}})
, and
\bm{X}\equiv\bm{X}_{1}({\bm{x}})
. The perturbed fluid density and gravitational potential at
\tau=1
are denoted by
\mu\equiv\rho_{1}({\bm{x}})
and
\Phi\equiv{\mathcal{U}}_{1}({\bm{x}})
, respectively, in accordance with the notation introduced in Eqs. (
1.1
)–(
1.5
). The volume of the fluid body deformed by rotation is denoted by
{\cal D}\equiv{\mathcal{V}}_{1}
.

6.1.2
Solving Functional Equations for the Height Function with the Shift Operator

We now turn to solving the functional equations (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) for the height function
X
. In both cases, the solution can be constructed using a Neumann series
[
105
]
, generated by the translation operator defined as
\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{\bm{X}}:=1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
, where
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
is the
shift
operator. This operator acts on any analytic function
F({\bm{x}})
via the rule:
\hat{\mathsf{S}}_{{\bm{X}}}F({\bm{x}})=F({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}})
. In this work, we adopt the shift operator
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
as the primary tool for expressing translations. All calculations are performed in the
radial gauge
, where the displacement vector is purely radial:
{\bm{X}}=X({\bm{x}}){\bm{n}}
.

The unperturbed reference configuration of the fluid is described by the functions
\rho=\rho(r)
,
p=p(r)
, and
U=U(r)
, which are assumed to be known either analytically or through numerical computation to any desired degree of accuracy. These functions depend solely on the radial coordinate
r
and are fully determined by the solutions to the hydrostatic equilibrium equations presented in Section
2
.

The total gravitational potential
V
, defined as the sum of the unperturbed potential
U
and the perturbation
K
, represents the complete gravitational field (cf. Eq. (
5.26
)):

\displaystyle V({\bm{x}})=U({\bm{x}})+K({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.13)

where the perturbation term
K=(\Phi-{\mathcal{U}})+W_{Q}
encompasses both the deviation
\Phi-{\mathcal{U}}
arising from the rotating fluid and the rotational quadrupole contribution
W_{Q}
(refer to Eq. (
5.27
) for
\tau=1
).
The functional equation (
6.12
), which governs the deformation of level surfaces for
\tau=1
, can thus be reformulated as:

\displaystyle V({\bm{y}}):=\Phi({\bm{y}})+W({\bm{y}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.14)

where
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}
, and
{\bm{X}}={\bm{X}}({\bm{x}})
denotes the displacement vector of the level surface. This equation may be directly compared with the condition (
1.7
) from the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory
[
2
]
, which defines the level surfaces of the total potential. The comparison reveals that the Lie group approach identifies
(4\pi G/3)r^{2}A_{0}(r)\equiv U(r)
, where
U(r)
is the effective gravitational potential of the reference fluid configuration, assumed to be known. This identification enables a significantly more efficient methodology for determining the shape of equipotential surfaces and the figure of a rotating fluid body. By employing the shift operator technique, this approach offers substantial advantages over the classical Zharkov-Trubitsyn framework
[
2
]
. The details of this method are presented in the following sections.

To support the application of the shift operator method, it is beneficial to express the perturbed gravitational potential
K
in terms of the total potential
V
:

\displaystyle K({\bm{x}})=V({\bm{x}})-V\left({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}\right)\;,

(6.15)

where
{\bm{X}}={\bm{X}}({\bm{x}})
denotes the displacement vector of the level surface. As demonstrated in the following section, this formulation serves as the foundation for determining the displacement vector
{\bm{X}}
, which characterizes the geometry of the level surfaces within a rotating fluid body. Moreover, it enables the determination of the body’s external gravitational field and boundary shape, given a prescribed density distribution and equation of state for the reference configuration.

To solve Eq. (
6.15
), we expand
V\left({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}\right)
in a Taylor series with respect to the displacement vector
{\bm{X}}
, assuming the series converges throughout the interior of the fluid body. This yields:

\displaystyle K({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}V({\bm{x}})=-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\bigl[U({\bm{x}})+K({\bm{x}})\bigr]\;,

(6.16)

where
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
is the shift operator defined via the infinite Taylor series as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}X^{i_{1}}X^{i_{2}}...X^{i_{n}}\partial_{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{n}}=X^{i}\partial_{i}+\frac{1}{2!}X^{i}X^{j}\partial_{ij}+\frac{1}{3!}X^{i}X^{j}X^{k}\partial_{ijk}+...\;,

(6.17)

using multi-index notation and Einstein summation over repeated spatial indices.

Equation (
6.16
) can be solved iteratively for
K({\bm{x}})
, assuming convergence. This leads to a nested expression:

\displaystyle K({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\left[U({\bm{x}})-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\left[U({\bm{x}})-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\Bigl[U({\bm{x}})-...\Bigr]\right]\right]\;.

(6.18)

This is equivalent to expressing
K({\bm{x}})
as a Neumann series
[
105
]
in powers of the shift operator
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.19)

The same solution for
K({\bm{x}})
can also be derived using the symbolic operator method. Rewriting Eq. (
6.16
) gives:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)K({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.20)

The formal operator solution of this equation is:

\displaystyle K({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}U({\bm{x}})}{1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}}\;.

(6.21)

Expanding the denominator in a power series confirms the equivalence of Eqs. (
6.19
) and (
6.21
).

It is important to note that the displacement
X^{i}=X^{i}({\bm{x}})
is a spatially varying vector field. Thus, repeated applications of
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
in Eq. (
6.19
) for
n=2,3,4,...
, involve differentiating both the potential
U({\bm{x}})
and the components of
X^{i}({\bm{x}})
, as defined in the Taylor expansion (
6.17
). For instance, the quadratic approximation of Eq. (
6.21
) yields:

\displaystyle K({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-X^{i}\partial_{i}U({\bm{x}})+\frac{1}{2}X^{i}X^{j}\partial_{ij}U({\bm{x}})+X^{i}\partial_{i}X^{j}\partial_{j}U({\bm{x}})+\ldots\;,

(6.22)

The level surfaces of the gravitational potential coincide with surfaces of constant pressure and density
[
13
,
2
,
14
]
. As a result, the perturbed density
\rho+\varrho_{\tau}
at
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}
must equal the unperturbed density
\rho
at the original position
{\bm{x}}
, in accordance with Eq. (
6.11
). For the case
\tau=1
, this condition can be equivalently expressed as:

\displaystyle\sigma({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}})+\varrho({\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.23)

where
\sigma
denotes the reference configuration density, as defined in Eq. (
2.22
). The substitution
\rho\to\sigma
is justified here, since
\rho
and
\sigma
differ only by a constant offset that cancels out in Eq. (
6.23
), thereby preserving consistency with Eq. (
6.11
).

Equation (
6.23
) can be reformulated using the shift operator technique, yielding:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\varrho({\bm{x}})=-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.24)

This indicates that the density perturbation
\varrho({\bm{x}})
satisfies an equation structurally analogous to Eq. (
6.20
) for the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
, involving the same displacement vector
{\bm{X}}
. Accordingly, its solution takes the form of a Neumann series similar to Eq. (
6.19
):

\displaystyle\varrho({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.25)

Equations (
6.19
) and (
6.25
) express the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
and the density perturbation
\varrho({\bm{x}})
, respectively, as power series in the shift operator
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
acting on the unperturbed potential
U({\bm{x}})=U(r)
and density
\rho({\bm{x}})=\rho(r)
. The displacement vector
{\bm{X}}={\bm{X}}({\bm{x}})
represents the finite radial deformation of the level surfaces in the reference configuration due to the rotational quadrupole perturbation
W_{Q}
.

As in the linearized theory, the height function
X=X({\bm{x}})
can be determined by two methods. The first method generalizes Bruns’ theorem (Eq. (
3.48
)) and involves solving Eq. (
6.20
), which relates
K({\bm{x}})
and
X({\bm{x}})
, in the form of a power series. This approach requires the explicit form of the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
, which is obtained by solving the nonlinear Molodensky equation (
5.59
).

The second method for determining the height function
X
generalizes the Molodensky equation (
3.49
) to the nonlinear regime, accounting for finite deformations of the level surfaces. This approach involves a detailed manipulation of the shift operator
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
to derive a fundamental differential equation for
X({\bm{x}})
that eliminates the explicit dependence on the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
. A comprehensive discussion of both methods is presented in the following two sections.

6.2
Developing Power Series Solution for the Height Function

In this section, we assume that the perturbation field
K=K({\bm{x}})
is known as a solution to the Molodensky equation (
5.59
). To determine the height function
X=X({\bm{x}})
of the perturbed level surface above a reference sphere of radius
r
, we apply the power series method to Eq. (
6.20
), utilizing the expansion of the shift operator
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
given in Eq. (
6.17
). Since the displacement vector
X^{i}=Xn^{i}
is purely radial, Eq. (
6.20
) simplifies to a scalar relation involving only the height function
X
and the radial derivatives of the gravitational potential
U
and its perturbation
K
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha_{n}}{n!}X^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-K\;,

(6.26)

where the coefficients are defined as
\alpha_{n}=U^{(n)}+K^{(n)}
. Here,
U=U(r)
is the known gravitational potential of the unperturbed configuration, and
K=K(\bm{x})
is the perturbation field obtained from Eq. (
5.59
). The terms
U^{(n)}:=\partial_{r}^{n}U(r)
and
K^{(n)}:=\partial_{r}^{n}K({\bm{x}})
denote the
n
-th order radial derivatives. The objective is to invert Eq. (
6.26
) to express the height function
X
explicitly in terms of the known functions
U
and
K
.

To invert Eq. (
6.26
), we apply the Lagrange inversion theorem
[
100
]
, following the approach outlined in Section
5.5
. This yields a power series representation for
X
:

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{\beta_{n}}{n!}\left(\frac{K}{\alpha_{1}}\right)^{n}\;,

(6.27)

where
\beta_{1}=1
, and for
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{{n}-1}(-1)^{k}({n})_{k}{\bf B}_{{n}-1,k}\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},...,\gamma_{n-k}\right)\;,\

(6.28)

with
{\bf B}_{n-1,k}
denoting the incomplete Bell polynomials – see Eq. (
A.5
). The arguments
\gamma_{k}
are defined as

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k+1}\frac{\alpha_{k+1}}{\alpha_{1}}=\frac{1}{k+1}\frac{U^{(k+1)}+K^{(k+1)}}{U^{\prime}+K^{\prime}}\;,

(6.29)

with the prime denoting differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate
r
.

Since the perturbation magnitude satisfies
K\sim\mathsf{m}
, the displacement vector
X
can be expressed as an infinite power series in the small parameter
\mathsf{m}
. This expansion involves the unperturbed gravitational potential
U
, its perturbation
K
, and their radial derivatives. Such a formulation extends the classical linearized Bruns’ theorem (cf. Eq. (
3.48
)) to higher-order perturbation theory, providing a systematic framework for analyzing nonlinear deformations of level surfaces in rotating fluid bodies.

In practical applications, only a finite number of terms from the series expansion in Eq. (
6.27
) are required for computation. Higher-order derivatives of the unperturbed potential
U
can be analytically reduced to lower-order terms using the field equation (
2.23
), as demonstrated in Eqs. (
2.28
)-(
2.30
).

The angular dependence of the solution
X=X(r,\theta,\varphi)
arises through the perturbation
K=K(r,\theta,\varphi)
, which is assumed to be known from the solution of Eq. (
5.59
).

When both
U
and
K
are finite-order polynomials in the radial coordinate
r
, the number of arguments in the Bell polynomial appearing in Eq. (
6.28
) is bounded by the polynomial degree. Consequently, only a finite number of coefficients
\beta_{n}
contribute to the expansion in Eq. (
6.27
), reducing the solution for
X
to a finite series. However, since
K
also appears in the denominator of the coefficients
\gamma_{k}
, its expansion in powers of
K\sim\mathsf{m}
introduces an infinite series. Thus, even with finitely many
\beta_{n}
, the full solution (
6.27
) remains an infinite series in the perturbation parameter
\mathsf{m}
.

A detailed application of this power series method for computing the height function
X
in case of a polytropic model with index
n=1
is presented in Section
9.2
.

6.3
Differential Equations for Fluid Body Deformations

6.3.1
Master Equation for the Deformation Gradient

Traditionally, the structure of level surfaces and the shape of a rotating fluid body – such as a planet or star – are analyzed using perturbation theory, with a small parameter
\mathsf{m}
, defined in Eq. (
2.14
). Within this framework, the perturbed gravitational potential
V
is expanded in a Legendre polynomial series, and the height function is expressed as
X=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}X_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)
, where each spectral harmonic scales as
X_{l}\sim\mathsf{m}^{l/2}
. At each order
l
, the corresponding equations for
X_{l}
are derived using various perturbative techniques, as outlined in the introduction of this manuscript. Regardless of the specific scheme employed, the computational complexity of formulating and solving these equations increases substantially with higher-order harmonics.

The most advanced developments in this field originate from the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory of figures
[
2
]
, which has been extended up to seventh order (
l=14
, corresponding to
\mathsf{m}^{7}
)
[
9
]
. However, the feasibility of further extending perturbative methods remains uncertain. Deriving higher-order equations for
X_{l}
leads to increasingly complex, nonlinear integro-differential equations with a rapidly growing number of terms, necessitating extensive symbolic computation. Moreover, the numerical evaluation of higher-order contributions is prone to instability, as small inaccuracies in lower-order terms can propagate and significantly distort the final results. These challenges underscore the need for carefully designed algorithms and robust numerical strategies
[
106
]
.

The challenges associated with computing higher-order perturbations can be effectively addressed by adopting a non-perturbative approach to deriving the differential equation for the height function
X
, thereby bypassing the traditional Legendre polynomial decomposition of the gravitational potential
V
. The method presented in this section avoids reliance on successive approximations and instead exploits the mathematical structure of the shift operator
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}
, analytic summation of infinite series, and tools from linear matrix algebra. This framework enables the derivation of a general, exact master differential equation for
X
. Once this equation is obtained, the spectral harmonics
X_{l}
can be extracted by expanding the master equation in Legendre polynomials using Wigner’s addition theorem for spherical harmonics – a straightforward algebraic procedure
[
63
]
.

The derivation begins with the formulation of a master equation for the
deformation gradient matrix

A_{ij}:=\partial_{i}X^{j}
, where the displacement vector is given by
X^{i}=Xn^{i}
representing a purely radial field. The starting point is Eq. (
6.16
), which, using the definition of the total potential
V
from Eq. (
6.13
), can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)V({\bm{x}})=U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.30)

Differentiating Eq. (
6.30
) and applying the Leibniz rule to the product
\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}V({\bm{x}})
yields:

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{i}\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)V({\bm{x}})+\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{i}V({\bm{x}})=\partial_{i}U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.31)

The derivative of the shift operator, based on its definition in Eq. (
6.17
), is given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}X^{p}\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{p}\;.

(6.32)

Substituting this into Eq. (
6.31
) leads to the matrix equation:

\displaystyle M_{ij}({\bm{X}})\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{j}V({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}U({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.33)

where the matrix

\displaystyle M_{ij}({\bm{X}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y^{j}}{\partial x^{i}}=\delta_{ij}+A_{ij}\;,

(6.34)

and
A_{ij}:=\partial_{i}X^{j}
is the deformation gradient
[
78
]
. Notably,
A_{ij}
is generally non-symmetric (
A_{ij}\neq A_{ji}
), and therefore
M_{ij}
is also non-symmetric – even in the case of an ideal fluid.

The inverse matrix
M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})
is defined by the standard relations:

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ik}({\bm{X}})M_{kj}({\bm{X}})=\delta_{ij}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\qquad M_{ik}({\bm{X}})M^{-1}_{kj}({\bm{X}})=\delta_{ij}\;,

(6.35)

with summation implied over repeated indices from 1 to 3. Substituting the definition of
M_{ij}
from Eq. (
6.34
) into these identities yields the explicit form:

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial y^{i}}=\delta_{ij}+B_{ij}({\bm{X}})\;,

(6.36)

where
B_{ij}
is given by the Neumann series expansion in powers of the deformation gradient:

\displaystyle B_{ij}({\bm{X}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}({\bf A}^{n})_{ij}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}A_{ip_{1}}A_{p_{1}p_{2}}...A_{p_{n-1}j}\;.

(6.37)

As before, repeated indices imply summation over the range 1 to 3.

Using the inverse matrix (
6.36
), Eq. (
6.33
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{i}V({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})\partial_{j}U({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.38)

This equation mirrors the structure of Eq. (
6.30
), which becomes evident by substituting
V({\bm{x}})\rightarrow\partial_{i}V({\bm{x}})
and
U({\bm{x}})\rightarrow M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})\partial_{j}U({\bm{x}})
. Following the same reasoning that led from (
6.30
) to (
6.38
), we obtain the corresponding equation for the second partial derivative:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{ij}V({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ip}({\bm{X}})\partial_{p}\left[M^{-1}_{jq}({\bm{X}})\partial_{q}U({\bm{x}})\right]\;.

(6.39)

The left-hand side of Eq. (
6.39
) is symmetric in the indices
i
and
j
. Therefore, the compatibility condition requires that the antisymmetric part of the right-hand side vanishes identically:

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{[ip}({\bm{X}})\partial_{p}\left[M^{-1}_{j]q}({\bm{X}})\partial_{q}U({\bm{x}})\right]\equiv 0\;.

(6.40)

Although this identity may not be immediately evident, it can be rigorously proven (see Appendix
C
).

Contracting the free indices in Eq. (
6.39
) yields:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\Delta V({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ip}({\bm{X}})\partial_{p}\left[M^{-1}_{iq}({\bm{X}})\partial_{q}U({\bm{x}})\right]\;.

(6.41)

This expression can be simplified using the definition of
V
from Eq. (
6.13
). The Laplacian of
V
is obtained via the Poisson equations for
U
and
K
(Eqs. (
2.1
) and (
5.28
)), omitting the surface density term
\nu\equiv\nu_{1}
since we are working within the domain
r<a
:

\displaystyle\Delta V({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\left[\sigma({\bm{x}})+\varrho({\bm{x}})\right]\;,

(6.42)

where
\varrho
is the density perturbation defined in Eq. (
6.25
). Applying Eq. (
6.24
), which can be equivalently written as:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\left[\sigma({\bm{x}})+\varrho({\bm{x}})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma({\bm{x}})\;,

(6.43)

we find that the left-hand side of Eq. (
6.41
) simplifies to:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\Delta V({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.44)

Substituting this result into Eq. (
6.41
) yields the
master equation for the deformation gradient
:

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}M^{-1}_{ip}({\bm{X}})\partial_{p}\left[M^{-1}_{jq}({\bm{X}})\partial_{q}U({\bm{x}})\right]=-4\pi G\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.45)

This equation represents a central result in the theory of figures of rotating fluid bodies. Although exact, it is highly nonlinear due to the dependence of the inverse matrix
M^{-1}_{ij}
on the deformation gradient
A_{ij}
, which itself is expressed as an infinite Neumann series – see Eqs. (
6.36
) and (
6.37
).

At first glance, Eq. (
6.45
) may resemble a Poisson equation for the effective gravitational potential
U=U({\bm{x}})
in the reference configuration, but expressed in the deformed coordinates
y^{i}=x^{i}+X^{i}({\bm{x}})
. Indeed, by using the definitions of the matrix
M_{ij}
and its inverse, Eq. (
6.45
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}\frac{\partial^{2}U({\bm{x}})}{\partial y^{i}\partial y^{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.46)

This form might be naively interpreted as a straightforward coordinate transformation of the Poisson equation (
2.23
) from the original coordinates
{\bm{x}}
to the deformed coordinates
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{X}}
. However, this interpretation is incorrect. When Eq. (
2.23
) is properly expressed in terms of the deformed coordinates
{\bm{y}}
, it takes the form:

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}M_{ip}({\bm{X}})\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{p}}\left[M_{jq}({\bm{X}})\frac{\partial U({\bm{x}})}{\partial y^{q}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4\pi G\sigma({\bm{x}})\;.

(6.47)

This equation is not equivalent to either Eq. (
6.46
) or Eq. (
6.45
). The original Poisson equation (
2.23
) is used to determine the reference potential
U=U({\bm{x}})
as a function of the radial coordinate
r=|{\bm{x}}|
, as shown in Eq. (
2.24
). In contrast, Eq. (
