6.46
) (or Eq. (
6.45
)) is employed to determine the height function
{\bm{X}}={\bm{y}}-{\bm{x}}
, under the assumption that the unperturbed fluid density
\sigma({\bm{x}})
and the potential
U({\bm{x}})
are already known from the solution describing the unperturbed fluid configuration.

Equation (
6.45
) is preferable to Eq. (
6.46
) for further theoretical development. The primary advantage lies in its matrix formulation, which features the inverse matrix
M^{-1}_{ij}
expressed as an infinite Neumann series. Remarkably, this series can be summed analytically, reducing Eq. (
6.45
) to a single second-order partial differential equation for the height function
X
. This leads to a desirable nonlinear generalization of the classical Clairaut equation.

This result is novel: earlier perturbative methods were unable to manage the infinite series of nonlinear terms that arise when attempting a closed-form expansion of the perturbed gravitational potential
\Phi
. In contrast, our method – based on the summable Lie-Neumann series – offers a more transparent and tractable framework for studying the stratification of level surfaces in rotating fluid bodies. Unlike traditional techniques, it avoids expanding the gravitational potential in terms of Legendre polynomials, thereby sidestepping the divergence issues that commonly afflict such expansions.

6.3.2
Master Equation for the Height Function

The master equation (
6.45
) governing the deformation gradient is formulated in terms of the inverse matrix
M^{-1}_{ij}
, which is represented as a Neumann series comprising an infinite sum of terms involving the deformation gradient matrix
A_{ij}
, itself dependent on the derivative of the radial displacement vector
X^{i}
. At first glance, this formulation may appear to offer no immediate simplification in deriving the differential equation for the height function. However, this is not the case. Notably, the Neumann series in Eq. (
6.45
) can be summed analytically, allowing the original matrix-based formulation to be recast as a scalar partial differential equation involving only the height function
X
.

This transformation is of considerable significance: it reduces a complex, nonlinear matrix equation to a more tractable scalar form, while fully preserving the nonlinearity inherent in the original formulation. The key steps of this transformation are outlined below:

1.

The inverse matrix
M^{-1}_{ij}
is expressed in closed form by leveraging the analytic structure of the deformation gradient.

2.

This closed-form expression is substituted into the master equation (
6.45
), reformulating it entirely in terms of the height function
X=X({\bm{x}})
and its derivatives.

3.

The resulting expression is contracted and simplified, yielding a second-order partial differential equation for the scalar field
X=X({\bm{x}})
.

Taking the partial derivative on the left-hand side of the master equation (
6.45
) and dividing both sides by the radial derivative of the unperturbed gravitational potential,
U^{\prime}
, yields the following expression:

\displaystyle n^{q}M^{-1}_{ip}({\bm{X}})\partial_{p}M^{-1}_{iq}({\bm{X}})+M^{-1}_{ip}({\bm{X}})M^{-1}_{iq}({\bm{X}})\left[\frac{{\cal P}^{pq}}{r}-n^{p}n^{q}\left(\frac{4\pi G\sigma}{U^{\prime}}+\frac{2}{r}\right)\right]=-\frac{4\pi G\sigma}{U^{\prime}}\;,

(6.48)

where repeated indices imply summation over the range 1 to 3, and the tensor
{\cal P}^{pq}:=\delta^{pq}-n^{p}n^{q}
denotes the projection operator onto the plane orthogonal to the radial unit vector
{\bm{n}}=(n^{p})
.
Introducing the auxiliary vector
N^{i}:=M^{-1}_{ip}n^{p}
, the equation simplifies to:

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ip}\partial_{p}N^{i}+\frac{3{\upbeta}}{r}\left(N^{2}-1\right)-\frac{2}{r}N^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(6.49)

where
N^{2}:=\delta_{ij}N^{i}N^{j}
, and the substitution
4\pi G\sigma/U^{\prime}\to-3\upbeta/r
follows from Eq. (
2.37
).

To further simplify the expression, we evaluate the divergence term as follows:

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ip}\partial_{p}N^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ip}\delta^{pq}\partial_{q}N^{i}=M^{-1}_{ip}\left({\cal P}^{pq}+n^{p}n^{q}\right)\partial_{q}N^{i}=M^{-1}_{ip}{\cal P}^{pq}\partial_{q}N^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}N^{2}\;.

(6.50)

Applying Eq. (
D.7
), this expression can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ip}\partial_{p}N^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{R}\left[\partial_{i}N^{i}-\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{R^{\prime}}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}N^{2}\;,

(6.51)

where we introduce the auxiliary variable
R\equiv r+X
, with
R^{\prime}=1+X^{\prime}
, and the prime denotes partial differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate
r
. Substituting Eq. (
6.51
) into Eq. (
6.49
) yields the following scalar partial differential equation for the height function
X
:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}N^{i}+\frac{R^{\prime\prime}}{R^{\prime 2}}+\frac{R}{r}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}N^{2}-\frac{2N^{2}}{r}+3\frac{N^{2}-1}{r}{\upbeta}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(6.52)

In terms of the variable
R\equiv r+X
, the vector
N^{i}
, as defined explicitly in Eq. (
D.17
), can be expressed using the radial displacement
X
and its derivative
X^{\prime}=\partial_{r}X
, as follows:

\displaystyle N^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n^{i}}{R^{\prime}}-\frac{r{\cal P}^{ij}{R_{j}}}{R^{\prime}R}=\frac{n^{i}}{R^{\prime}}+\frac{rn^{i}}{R}-\frac{r{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime}R}\;,

(6.53)

\displaystyle N^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R^{\prime 2}}+\frac{r^{2}{\cal P}^{ij}{R_{i}}{R_{j}}}{R^{\prime 2}R^{2}}=\frac{1}{R^{\prime 2}}-\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime 2}R^{2}}\;,

(6.54)

where
R_{i}\equiv\partial_{i}R=\partial R/\partial x^{i}
denotes the partial derivative of
R
. The divergence of
N^{i}
is given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}N^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{rR^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{R}-\frac{rR^{\prime}}{R^{2}}-\frac{R^{\prime\prime}}{R^{\prime 2}}+\frac{rn^{p}{R_{i}}\partial_{ip}R}{R^{\prime 2}R}+\frac{R+rR^{\prime}}{R^{\prime 2}R^{2}}{R_{i}}{R_{i}}-\frac{r\Delta R}{R^{\prime}R}\;,

(6.55)

where
\Delta=\delta^{ij}\partial^{2}/\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}
is the Laplace operator.
The radial derivative of
N^{2}
, as derived from Eq. (
6.54
), is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}N^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}\left(\frac{1}{R^{\prime 2}}-\frac{r^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime 2}R^{2}}\right)

(6.56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{R^{\prime\prime}}{R^{\prime 3}}-\frac{r}{R^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}R^{\prime}}{R^{3}}+\frac{r{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime 2}R^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}n^{p}\partial_{ip}R{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime 2}R^{2}}-\frac{r^{2}R^{\prime\prime}{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime 3}R^{2}}-\frac{r^{2}{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime}R^{3}}\;.

By adding Eqs. (
6.55
) and (
6.56
), we arrive at the following result:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}N^{i}+\frac{R^{\prime\prime}}{R^{\prime 2}}+\frac{R}{r}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}N^{2}-\frac{2N^{2}}{r}\right)

(6.57)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\frac{r}{R^{\prime}R}\left[-\Delta{R}+\frac{2R^{\prime}}{r}+\frac{2R}{r^{2}}-\frac{R^{\prime\prime}R^{2}}{r^{2}R^{\prime 2}}-\frac{2R^{2}}{r^{3}R^{\prime}}+\frac{2n^{p}{R_{i}}\partial_{ip}R}{R^{\prime}}-\frac{R^{\prime\prime}{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime 2}}\right]\;,

which simplifies to:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}N^{i}+\frac{R^{\prime\prime}}{R^{\prime 2}}+\frac{R}{r}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}N^{2}-\frac{2N^{2}}{r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{R^{\prime}R}\left[-\Delta{R}+\frac{2R^{\prime}}{r}+\partial_{r}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{r^{2}R^{\prime}}+\frac{{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime}}\right)\right]\;.

(6.58)

Substituting this result into Eq. (
6.52
), and incorporating the relation from Eq. (
6.54
), we obtain the final expression:

\displaystyle\Delta{R}-\frac{2R^{\prime}}{r}-\partial_{r}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{r^{2}R^{\prime}}+\frac{{R_{i}}{R_{i}}}{R^{\prime}}\right)+\frac{3{\upbeta}}{r}\left[\frac{R^{2}}{r^{2}}R^{\prime}-\frac{1}{R^{\prime}}\left(\frac{R^{2}}{r^{2}}+{\cal P}^{ij}{R_{i}}{R_{j}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(6.59)

This nonlinear second-order partial differential equation governs the total radial displacement
R=r+X
of the level surface from its unperturbed spherical configuration with radius
r
. It encapsulates the influence of the quadrupole rotational perturbation
W_{Q}
on the equilibrium shape of a rotating fluid body.

6.3.3
Master Equation for the Shape Function

The height function
X=X({\bm{x}})
describes the radial displacement of a level surface above a reference sphere of radius
r
. It plays a fundamental role in analyzing and computing perturbations in the density, pressure, and gravitational field of a rotating fluid body. Moreover, it is essential for determining the multipole moments of the external gravitational field of planets and stars, thereby linking local deformations to global gravitational signatures.

For global analyses of the shape of a rotating astronomical body, it is often more practical to introduce a dimensionless measure of deformation – the shape function
f=f({\bm{x}})
– defined as the normalized radial displacement:

\displaystyle f({\bm{x}})\equiv\frac{X({\bm{x}})}{r}\;.

(6.60)

The shape function offers a concise, scale-invariant characterization of the global deviation of the perturbed level surface from a perfect sphere of radius
r
, making it particularly useful for comparative studies across different planetary bodies or rotational regimes.

The master equation for the shape function
f
is derived from Eq. (
6.59
) through a sequence of variable transformations. As a first step, we introduce the logarithmic variable:

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)=\log\left(1+f\right)\;.

(6.61)

Rewriting Eq. (
6.59
) in terms of
Z
yields the following nonlinear partial differential equation:

\displaystyle\Delta Z-\frac{2}{r}Z^{\prime}-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{r{\partial_{i}Z}{\partial_{i}Z}}{1+rZ^{\prime}}\right)-3{\upbeta}\left[\frac{1-e^{2Z}}{r^{2}}-\frac{1+e^{2Z}}{r}Z^{\prime}+\frac{{\partial_{i}Z}{\partial_{i}Z}}{1+rZ^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(6.62)

where
\partial_{i}Z\equiv\partial Z/\partial x^{i}
.
This equation is transcendental and highly nonlinear. Once a solution for
Z
is obtained, the shape function
f
can be recovered by inverting Eq. (
6.61
). Alternatively, one may formulate and solve a differential equation directly in terms of
f
.

To proceed, we invert Eq. (
6.61
) to express the shape function
f
in terms of variable
Z
:

\displaystyle e^{Z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+f\;.

(6.63)

Differentiating both sides and introducing the notations
\partial_{i}f\equiv\partial f/\partial x^{i}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle e^{Z}\partial_{i}Z=\partial_{i}f\;\quad,\qquad\quad e^{Z}Z^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\;\quad,\qquad\quad e^{Z}\Delta Z=\Delta f-\frac{\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+f}\;,

(6.64)

and

\displaystyle e^{Z}\frac{{\partial_{i}Z}{\partial_{i}Z}}{1+rZ^{\prime}}=\frac{\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+(rf)^{\prime}}\;\quad,\qquad\qquad e^{Z}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r\frac{{\partial_{i}Z}{\partial_{i}Z}}{1+rZ^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{r\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+(rf)^{\prime}}\right]-\frac{\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+f}\frac{rf^{\prime}}{1+(rf)^{\prime}}\;.

(6.65)

Multiplying Eq. (
6.62
) by
e^{Z}
and applying the relations (
6.63
)–(
6.65
), we arrive at:

\displaystyle\Delta f-\frac{2f^{\prime}}{r}+\frac{6{\upbeta}}{r}\left(f^{\prime}+\frac{f}{r}\right)-r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left[\frac{\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+X^{\prime}}\right]-\frac{2\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+X^{\prime}}+3{\upbeta}\left[\frac{3f^{2}+f^{3}}{r^{2}}+\frac{(2f+f^{2})f^{\prime}}{r}-\frac{\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f}{1+X^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(6.66)

This equation contains both linear and nonlinear terms. By isolating the linear terms on the left-hand side and moving the nonlinear terms to the right-hand side, we obtain the master equation for the shape function
f=f({\bm{x}})
in the form:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{D}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{S(f)}{r}\;,

(6.67)

where the second-order differential (Clairaut) operator is defined as:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{D}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Delta-\frac{2}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}\right)\;.

(6.68)

The nonlinear source term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
6.67
) is defined as:

\displaystyle S(f)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle A^{\prime}-\upbeta B^{\prime}+\frac{3\upbeta}{r}(A-B)\;.

(6.69)

where we introduce the shorthand notations:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{Y}{1+X^{\prime}}\qquad\quad,\quad\qquad B\equiv 3f^{2}+f^{3}\;,

(6.70)

with
X^{\prime}=(rf)^{\prime}=rf^{\prime}+f
and
Y\equiv r^{2}\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f
. Here, the prime denotes partial differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate
r
.

The master equation (
6.67
) for the shape function
f=f({\bm{x}})
is a new result not previously obtained in the literature. It is exact, depends on all three spatial coordinates, and is nonlinear. Remarkably, in the case of constant density, where the parameter
{\upbeta}=1
, Eq. (
6.67
) admits several exact solutions. Among these are the classical Maclaurin spheroid and the Jacobi triaxial ellipsoid, both of which are discussed in detail in Section
9
. The explicit formulation of the master equation for the shape function provides a rigorous foundation for establishing the asymptotic nature of the perturbation series previously employed by various researchers in the study of rotating fluid bodies in both geophysical and astrophysical contexts
[
107
]
.

Unfortunately, Eq. (
6.67
) cannot be solved analytically in the general case, necessitating the use of approximation methods. The following section outlines an approach for constructing approximate solutions to Eq. (
6.67
) under the assumption of axisymmetric level surfaces, where the shape function
f({\bm{x}})=f(r,\theta)
depends only on the spherical coordinates
r
and
\theta
. The approximations are developed using the method of separation of variables, based on the spectral decomposition of the shape function into zonal spherical harmonics, which is a standard technique in planetary and stellar modeling
[
2
,
14
]
.

A key advantage of our approach is that it naturally leads to ordinary differential equations governing the spectral radial harmonics of the shape function. This represents a significant improvement over the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory, in which deriving such equations from the underlying integro-differential framework is both complex and cumbersome
[
33
,
34
,
8
]
. Moreover, the traditional method does not provide a general analytic form for these equations at arbitrary orders of approximation. In contrast, our formulation offers a systematic and transparent pathway for obtaining such results, enabling more efficient and scalable analyses of rotating fluid bodies.

7
Differential Equation Framework for Radial Spectral Harmonics

In this section, we employ the Legendre polynomial series method to systematically reduce the nonlinear partial differential equation (
6.67
) for the shape function
f({\bm{x}})=f(r,\theta)
to a set of ordinary differential equations. These equations govern the radial dependence of the Legendre coefficients – referred to as radial harmonics – which encapsulate the angular structure of the solution through a spectral expansion in terms of Legendre polynomials.

We assume that the dimensionless parameter
\mathsf{m}
, introduced in Eq. (
2.14
), is small (
\mathsf{m}\ll 1
), and perform a perturbative expansion of the master equation (
6.67
) in powers of
\mathsf{m}
. This expansion linearizes the problem order by order, yielding a hierarchy of inhomogeneous linear ordinary differential equations for the radial harmonics. At each order, the source terms on the right-hand side are explicitly determined by the solutions obtained at lower orders. This structure enables an iterative solution procedure, where the system is solved sequentially, starting from the lowest-order approximation.

Our approach to deriving the system of approximate equations offers a significant improvement in efficiency over traditional methods, which typically involve expanding the gravitational potential into spherical harmonics. Such methods lead to a system of coupled integro-differential equations that are often complex and computationally demanding
[
2
,
51
,
54
]
. In contrast, the present work introduces a direct procedure for obtaining differential equations for the radial harmonics of the shape function from the master equation (
6.67
), thereby eliminating the need to handle integro-differential formulations altogether.

7.1
Spectral Decomposition of the Shape Function

In the present paper, we assume that the uniformly rotating body is axially symmetric. This implies that the height function
X
, which characterizes the perturbation of the level surface of a uniformly rotating fluid body, depends only on two coordinates:
X=X(r,\theta)
, where
r
and
\theta
denote the radial and polar angular variables, respectively. Under this assumption, the shape function
f
also depends solely on
r
and
\theta
, i.e.,
f\equiv f(r,\theta)
. It can be expanded in a series of Legendre polynomials (zonal harmonics) as follows:

\displaystyle f(r,\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}(r)P_{k}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.1)

where
P_{k}(\cos\theta)
are the Legendre polynomials of degree
k
, and
f_{k}(r)
are the corresponding radial harmonics. We assume that the series (
7.1
) is convergent and that each term satisfies the scaling
f_{k}\sim{\mathsf{m}}^{k/2}
for
k\geq 1
. The monopole term
f_{0}
is of higher order, scaling as
f_{0}\sim{\mathsf{m}}^{2}
, as will be discussed in detail below.

It is also useful to express the spectral decomposition of the height function
X(r,\theta)=rf(r,\theta)
and its radial derivative, both of which appear in the master equation (
6.67
). The height function can be expanded as:

\displaystyle X(r,\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}X_{k}(r)P_{k}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.2)

and its radial derivative as:

\displaystyle X^{\prime}(r,\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}X^{\prime}_{k}(r)P_{k}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.3)

where the radial harmonics of the height function are given by
X_{k}(r)=rf_{k}(r)
, and their radial derivatives are:

\displaystyle X^{\prime}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle rf^{\prime}_{k}+f_{k}\;.

(7.4)

It is important to emphasize that the expansions in Eqs. (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) include the monopole harmonic
f_{0}
, corresponding to the index
k=0
. This harmonic is purely radial and, as demonstrated below, arises from the nonlinear coupling of higher-order harmonics
f_{k}
with
k\geq 2
in the nonlinear master equation (
6.67
). The monopole term
f_{0}
can be interpreted as an infinitesimal gauge transformation of the radial coordinate
r
. Its presence reflects the inherent freedom in parametrizing the level surfaces, which stems from the arbitrariness in choosing the reference unit for measuring the radial coordinate
[
2
]
. This gauge freedom may manifest differently depending on the computational framework adopted to solve the system of equations governing the radial harmonics of the shape function via successive approximations.

For example, within the Zharkov-Trubitsyn framework
[
2
]
, the governing system consists of a set of equations of the form
A_{2n}=0
, where each functional
A_{2n}=A_{2n}\left(\rho,f_{0},f_{2},...\right)
, for all
n\geq 1
depends on the density profile
\rho=\rho(r)
, the shape functions
f_{2n}=f_{2n}(r)
with
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, and their integrals. These equations are supplemented by the hydrodynamic equilibrium equation:

\displaystyle\rho\frac{dU}{dr}-\frac{dp}{dr}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4\pi G\rho}{3}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}A_{0}-\frac{3U}{4\pi G}\right)\;,

(7.5)

where the right-hand side represents the radial force arising from rotational perturbations, with the potential
U=U(r)
defined in Eq. (
2.24
). The functional
A_{0}=A_{0}\left(\rho,f_{0},f_{2},...\right)
is constructed from the same density profile and shape functions as the
A_{2n}
functionals, and contributes to the description of the body’s internal structure and equilibrium shape.

A count of the unknown functions and equations reveals that the system contains one more unknown function in the set
\{\rho,f_{0},f_{2},f_{4},\ldots\}
than the number of equations. This implies the presence of one degree of freedom, which originates from the arbitrariness in defining the reference unit for radial distance. The overdeterminacy of the system can be resolved in various ways, depending on the computational scheme employed.

Zharkov and Trubitsyn
[
2
]
treated the monopole (purely radial) perturbation of the gravitational field and the level surfaces separately from the higher-order harmonics. In their formulation, the choice of the radial coordinate is fixed by requiring that the monopole term
f_{0}
ensures volume conservation: the volume enclosed by the perturbed level surface, described by the function
R(r,\theta)=r+X(r,\theta)
, remains equal to that of the unperturbed sphere of radius
r
. This condition imposes the identity:

\displaystyle\frac{4\pi r^{3}}{3}=\int_{0}^{R(r,\theta)}\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\xi^{2}\sin\theta d\xi d\theta d\varphi\;,

(7.6)

which guarantees volume conservation under the perturbation. The integral on the right-hand side can be evaluated using the Wigner decomposition of products of Legendre polynomials, as detailed in Appendix
E
. Equation (
7.6
) can then be solved iteratively for
f_{0}
to any desired order of accuracy. The first few terms of the expansion are

\displaystyle f_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{5}f_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{105}f_{2}^{3}-\frac{1}{9}f_{4}^{2}-\frac{2}{35}f_{2}^{2}f_{4}-\ldots\;,

(7.7)

indicating that, within the Zharkov-Trubitsyn framework, the monopole term
f_{0}
emerges at second order in the small parameter (i.e.,
\sim\mathsf{m}^{2}
) as a predetermined function of products of the higher-order shape harmonics
f_{2n}
with
n\geq 1
. Fixing
f_{0}
in this way closes the system of equations for the remaining set of unknown functions
\{\rho,f_{2},f_{4},\ldots\}
, thereby rendering it solvable. This approach eliminates the need to solve an equation for
f_{0}
but it incorporates the full hydrodynamic equilibrium equation (
7.5
) into the computational scheme, which necessitates recalculating the density profile
\rho
along with the shape functions
f_{2},f_{4},\ldots
at each successive order of perturbation theory.

In contrast to the Zharkov-Trubitsyn approach, we do not impose the constraint given by Eq. (
7.7
) on the monopole harmonic
f_{0}
, as doing so would decouple its determination from the general computational framework adopted in this manuscript. Instead, the theory presented here treats the monopole harmonic
f_{0}
on equal footing with all other harmonics
f_{2n}
for all
n\geq 1
. The radial gauge freedom is fixed by postulating that the left-hand side of the hydrodynamic equilibrium equation (
7.5
), which represents the equilibrium condition for the reference fluid configuration as defined in Eq. (
2.41
), vanishes. This convention causes the right-hand side of Eq. (
7.5
) to vanish as well, thereby yielding a differential equation for determining the radial function
f_{0}
. As a result, the system of equations for the shape functions
f_{0},f_{2},f_{4},\ldots
becomes closed and solvable.

In our approach, the monopole term
f_{0}
remains of second order in the small parameter
\mathsf{m}
, but its relationship to the higher-order harmonics is not prescribed a priori, unlike in the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory. Rather, it emerges naturally from the differential equation governing
f_{0}
, together with the boundary conditions imposed on the gravitational field perturbations at the surface of the body. Within our computational scheme, the density profile
\rho
is determined by solving the unperturbed hydrodynamic equilibrium equation (
2.41
), and it remains fixed across all orders of perturbation theory. Consequently, the shape functions of lower order do not require recalculation when higher-order effects are considered. This renders our computational approach more efficient than the Zharkov-Trubitsyn formalism, in which both the density profile and all shape functions must be recalculated as the perturbative order increases.

7.2
Spectral Decomposition for the Shape Function Products

The equations from the previous section suffice for the spectral decomposition of the left-hand side of the master equation (
6.67
), as it contains only linear terms. However, the spectral decomposition of the right-hand side is more involved due to the presence of non-linear terms.

We begin by decomposing the function
Y=Y(r,\theta)=r^{2}\partial_{i}f\partial_{i}f
into spherical harmonics. The gradient
\partial_{i}f
of the shape function
f=f(r,\theta)
is given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}n^{i}+\dot{f}\partial_{i}\theta\;,

(7.8)

where the derivatives are defined as
f^{\prime}\equiv\partial f/\partial r
and
\dot{f}\equiv\partial f/\partial\theta
. Using Eq. (
D.1
), we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\cos\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\cal P}^{i3}}{r}\;,

(7.9)

where
{\cal P}^{ij}=\delta^{ij}-n^{i}n^{j}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\partial_{i}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}n^{i}-\frac{\dot{f}\,{\cal P}^{i3}}{r\sin\theta}\;,

(7.10)

which leads to:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(rf^{\prime}\right)^{2}+{\dot{f}}^{2}\;.

(7.11)

By computing the derivatives from
f(r,\theta)
given in Eq. (
7.1
) and substituting into Eq. (
7.11
), we obtain:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left[r^{2}f^{\prime}_{n}(r)f^{\prime}_{m}(r)P_{n}(\cos\theta)P_{m}(\cos\theta)+f_{n}(r)f_{m}(r)\sin^{2}\theta\frac{dP_{n}(\cos\theta)}{d(\cos\theta)}\frac{dP_{m}(\cos\theta)}{d(\cos\theta)}\right]\;.

(7.12)

The products of Legendre polynomials and their derivatives are decomposed using Eqs. (
E.15
) and (
E.19
), yielding the spectral decomposition:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}Y_{k}(r)P_{k}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.13)

where the coefficients
Y_{k}(r)
are:

\displaystyle Y_{k}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-k|}^{n+k}\left\{r^{2}f^{\prime}_{n}f^{\prime}_{m}+\frac{1}{2}f_{n}f_{m}\left[n(n+1)+m(m+1)-k(k+1)\right]\right\}T^{nmk}\;.

(7.14)

Here, the symbol
T^{nmk}
denotes the Wigner matrix, as defined in Appendix
E
by Eqs. (
E.1
)–(
E.1
).

The function
A=A(r,\theta)
, defined in Eq. (
6.70
), admits a spectral decomposition in terms of Legendre harmonics:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}A_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.15)

where the harmonic amplitudes
A_{l}
are obtained by expanding the denominator of
A
into a Taylor series:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}X^{\prime k}\;,

(7.16)

and substituting the Legendre expansions for each function in Eq. (
7.16
), followed by reducing the resulting products using the Wigner decomposition (
E.17
). Inserting expansions (
7.3
) and (
7.14
) into the right-hand side of Eq. (
7.16
) yields:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{0}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}...\sum_{n_{k}=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}Y_{n_{0}}(r)X^{\prime}_{n_{1}}(r)...X^{\prime}_{n_{k}}(r)P_{n_{0}}(\cos\theta)P_{n_{1}}(\cos\theta)...P_{n_{k}}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.17)

which corresponds to the left-hand side of Eq. (
7.15
). Multiplying Eq. (
7.17
) by
P_{l}(\cos\theta)
, integrating over
\theta
, and applying the orthogonality relation (
E.20
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptA_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2l+1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{0}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}...\sum_{n_{k}=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}Y_{n_{0}}(r)X^{\prime}_{n_{1}}(r)...X^{\prime}_{n_{k}}(r)

(7.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\int\limits_{0}^{\pi}P_{n_{0}}(\cos\theta)P_{n_{1}}(\cos\theta)...P_{n_{k}}(\cos\theta)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\sin\theta d\theta\;.

The integral on the right-hand side of Eq. (
7.18
) is evaluated using Wigner’s formula (
E.22
), resulting in the expansion coefficients
A_{l}
:

\displaystyle A_{l}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{0}=0}^{\infty}\ldots\sum_{n_{k-1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{k}=|l-a_{k-1}|}^{l+a_{k-1}}\sum_{a_{1}=|n_{1}-n_{0}|}^{n_{1}+n_{0}}...\sum_{a_{k-1}=|n_{k-1}-a_{k-2}|}^{n_{k-1}+a_{k-2}}(-1)^{k}Q^{n_{0}...n_{k-1}n_{k}l}_{a_{1}...a_{k-1}}Y_{(n_{0}}X^{\prime}_{n_{1}}...X^{\prime}_{n_{k})}\;.

(7.19)

Here,
a_{0}=n_{0}
, and the parentheses around indices
(n_{0},n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
denote full symmetrization. The coefficients

\displaystyle Q^{n_{0}...n_{k}l}_{a_{1}...a_{k-1}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\;,\qquad\;k=0\;,\\
T^{n_{0}n_{1}l}&\;,\qquad\;k=1\;,\\
T^{n_{0}n_{1}a_{1}}T^{a_{1}n_{2}a_{2}}...T^{a_{k-2}n_{k-1}a_{k-1}}T^{a_{k-1}n_{k}l}&\;,\qquad\;k\geq 2\;,\end{array}

represent products of
k
Wigner matrices.

The spectral decomposition of the function
B=B(r,\theta)
is given by:

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}B_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.24)

where the expansion coefficients
B_{l}(r)
depend on the radial coordinate
r
and are defined as:

\displaystyle B_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3(f^{2})_{l}+(f^{3})_{l}\;,

(7.25)

with the spectral components
(f^{2})_{l}
and
(f^{3})_{l}
expressed as:

\displaystyle(f^{2})_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}f_{n}(r)f_{m}(r)T^{nml}\;,

(7.26)

\displaystyle(f^{3})_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=|l-a|}^{l+a}\sum_{a=|m-n|}^{m+n}f_{n}(r)f_{m}(r)f_{k}(r)T^{nma}T^{akl}\;.

(7.27)

7.3
Differential Equations for Radial Harmonics of the Shape Function

The equations from the previous section are employed to derive ordinary differential equations (ODEs) for the radial spectral harmonics
f_{l}
of the shape function. The spectral decomposition of the Laplacian of
f
is given by:

\displaystyle\Delta f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left[f_{l}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2f_{l}^{\prime}}{r}-\frac{l(l+1)f_{l}}{r^{2}}\right]P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;.

(7.28)

Using this expression, together with the spectral decomposition of the non-linear terms in the master equation (
6.67
), we obtain the ODE governing each harmonic
f_{l}
:

\displaystyle f_{l}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(f_{l}^{\prime}+\frac{f_{l}}{r}\right)-\frac{l(l+1)f_{l}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{S_{l}}{r}\;,

(7.29)

where

\displaystyle S_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{l}^{\prime}-\upbeta B_{l}^{\prime}+\frac{3\upbeta}{r}\left(A_{l}-B_{l}\right)\;.

(7.30)

The spectral harmonics
A_{l}
and
B_{l}
of the non-linear terms are given in Eqs. (
7.19
) and (
7.25
), respectively. Due to the non-linear nature of these terms, Eq. (
7.29
) is solved iteratively. Analyzing the first few iterations provides insight into the solution structure and facilitates comparison with results obtained by other methods. In this context, we focus on the first and second iterations. Higher-order iterations will be addressed in future work.

7.3.1
First Iteration: The Clairaut Equation

In the first (linear) iteration, all non-linear terms are neglected, reducing Eq. (
7.29
) to the classical Clairaut equation:

\displaystyle f_{l}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(f_{l}^{\prime}+\frac{f_{l}}{r}\right)-\frac{l(l+1)f_{l}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(7.31)

which describes axisymmetric rotational perturbations. This equation is traditionally derived by expanding the kernel of the gravitational potential in Legendre polynomials and transforming the resulting integro-differential equation for the radial harmonics into the differential Clairaut form
[
4
,
7
,
2
,
13
,
14
]
.

Milne
[
108
]
and Chandrasekhar
[
42
]
derived the Clairaut equation by expanding the density and gravitational potential in powers of the small rotational parameter
\mathsf{m}
. Hubbard, Slattery, and Devito
[
66
]
obtained the same result by decomposing the perturbed density
\mu
of a rotating body into Legendre polynomials. More recently, Chao and Shih
[
109
]
demonstrated an alternative derivation using the gravitational multipole formalism.

In contrast, we have derived the Clairaut equation (
7.31
) by applying a perturbative approach grounded in Lie group theory.

7.3.2
Second Iteration: Darwin – de Sitter Theory

In the second iteration, the spectral components of the non-linear term
S_{l}
on the right-hand side of Eq. (
7.29
) are given by
A_{l}=Y_{l}
and
B_{l}=3(f^{2})_{l}
, as defined in Eqs. (
7.14
) and (
7.26
), respectively. To evaluate
S_{l}
, we also require the radial derivatives
A_{l}^{\prime}
and
B_{l}^{\prime}
.

The derivative
A_{l}^{\prime}
is obtained by differentiating Eq. (
7.14
) and substituting the second derivative
f_{l}^{\prime\prime}
using the Clairaut equation (
7.31
), yielding:

\displaystyle A_{l}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}\left[2r\left(1-6\upbeta\right)f_{n}^{\prime}f_{m}^{\prime}+n(n+1)f_{n}f_{m}^{\prime}+m(m+1)f_{m}f_{n}^{\prime}\right]T^{nml}

(7.32)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}\left[n(n+1)+m(m+1)-l(l+1)-12\upbeta\right]f_{(n}^{\phantom{{}^{\prime}}}f_{m)}^{\prime}T^{nml}

Differentiating (
7.26
) gives:

\displaystyle B_{l}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 6\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}f_{(n}f_{m)}^{\prime}T^{nml}=3\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}\left(f_{n}f_{m}^{\prime}+f_{m}f_{n}^{\prime}\right)T^{nml}\;.

(7.33)

Substituting these expressions into Eq. (
7.30
), the non-linear source term
S_{l}
in the quadratic approximation becomes:

\displaystyle S_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}\left[r\left(2-9\upbeta\right)f_{n}^{\prime}f_{m}^{\prime}+n(n+1)f_{n}f_{m}^{\prime}+m(m+1)f_{m}f_{n}^{\prime}\right]T^{nml}

(7.34)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}\left[n(n+1)+m(m+1)-l(l+1)-18\upbeta\right]f_{(n}^{\phantom{{}^{\prime}}}f_{m)}^{\prime}T^{nml}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{3\upbeta}{2r}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}\left[n(n+1)+m(m+1)-l(l+1)-6\right]f_{n}f_{m}T^{nml}\;.

Equations (
7.34
) include all terms of quadratic order in the radial spectral harmonics
f_{l}
of the shape function. However, since each harmonic scales as
f_{0}\sim\mathsf{m}^{2}
for
l=0
and
f_{l}\sim\mathsf{m}^{l/2}
for
l\geq 2
, their magnitudes decrease rapidly with increasing
l
. Consequently, in the second-order approximation with respect to the rotation parameter
\mathsf{m}
, only the lowest harmonics – specifically
f_{0}
,
f_{2}
, and
f_{4}
– contribute significantly to the solution.

In this approximation, the non-linear terms contributing to the source function
S_{l}
involve only products of the harmonic
f_{2}\sim\mathsf{m}
and its derivatives. The harmonic
f_{0}\sim\mathsf{m}^{2}
is of second order, as noted in the final paragraph of Section
7.1
, and its contribution to non-linear terms can be neglected. A direct evaluation of
S_{l}
for
l=0,2,4
using Eq. (
7.34
) with
n=m=2
yields the following system:

\displaystyle f_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(f_{0}^{\prime}+\frac{f_{0}}{r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\left(2-9\upbeta\right)f_{2}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{6}{5r}\left(4-3\upbeta\right)f_{2}f_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{9\upbeta}{5r^{2}}f_{2}^{2}\;,

(7.35)

\displaystyle f_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(f_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{f_{2}}{r}\right)-\frac{6f_{2}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{7}\left(2-9\upbeta\right)f_{2}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{36}{7r}\left(1-\upbeta\right)f_{2}f_{2}^{\prime}\;,

(7.36)

\displaystyle f_{4}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{6\upbeta}{r}\left(f_{4}^{\prime}+\frac{f_{4}}{r}\right)-\frac{20f_{4}}{r^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{18}{35}\left(2-9\upbeta\right)f_{2}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{36}{35r}\left(2-9\upbeta\right)f_{2}f_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{54\upbeta}{5r^{2}}f_{2}^{2}\;,

(7.37)

where the function
\upbeta
is defined by (c.f. Eq. (
2.38
)):

\displaystyle\upbeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\upalpha+\frac{2{\mathsf{m}}}{3}\frac{\bar{\rho}(a)}{\bar{\rho}(r)}\left(\upalpha-1\right)\;.

(7.38)

Equations (
7.36
) and (
7.37
), commonly referred to as the Darwin-de Sitter and Darwin equations, respectively, represent important generalizations of Airy’s classical work on second-order corrections to the gravitational potential of rotating celestial bodies
[
28
,
5
,
2
]
. These equations have also been independently derived by Kopal
[
7
]
and Lanzano
[
8
]
, further underscoring their foundational role in the theory of planetary figures.

As noted by Chambat et al.
[
110
]
, there is a typographical error in Kopal’s differential equation for the harmonic component
f_{4}
. Specifically, the coefficient of the term proportional to
f_{2}f^{\prime}_{2}
is incorrectly given as
1-9\upbeta
in Kopal’s formulation, whereas the correct expression should be
2-9\upbeta
. This discrepancy, if uncorrected, may lead to inaccuracies in subsequent theoretical or numerical analyses.

The derivation of the Darwin-de Sitter equations presented in this section offers a novel perspective. Unlike traditional approaches that rely on gravitational potential expansions or multipole formalism, this formulation emerges naturally from a perturbative framework grounded in Lie group theory. By systematically incorporating non-linear corrections up to second order in the rotation parameter
\mathsf{m}
, the resulting equations not only recover classical results but also provide deeper insight into the structure and coupling of spectral harmonics in rotating celestial bodies.

7.4
Spectral Analysis of Gravitational Perturbations in the Body Interior

The gravitational field of a rotating fluid body lacks spherical symmetry and exhibits a complex spatial structure. This structure can be analyzed by expanding the gravitational field in spherical harmonics, both outside and inside the body. While the external expansion is well established – its coefficients known as multipole moments – this section focuses on the internal spectral expansion, where the harmonics are functions of the radial coordinate
r
and are referred to as radial spectral harmonics. The objective is to derive differential equations governing these harmonics.

We consider an axially symmetric perturbation of the gravitational field, described by the function
K
introduced in Eq. (
6.15
). Inside the body,
K
is expanded in Legendre polynomials:

\displaystyle K=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}K_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.39)

where
K_{l}(r)
are the radial harmonics. The finite density perturbation
\varrho\equiv\varrho_{1}
, defined in Eq. (
4.14
), is a nonlinear function of
K
, as given by Eq. (
5.49
) for
\tau=1
. It is similarly expanded:

\displaystyle\varrho=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\varrho_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.40)

where
\varrho_{l}(r)
denotes the
l
-th radial harmonic of the density perturbation.

Substituting Eq. (
7.39
) into Eq. (
5.49
) yields the spectral expansion:

\displaystyle\varrho=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{g_{j}}{{\cal A}^{j}j!}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}...\sum_{n_{j}=0}^{\infty}K_{n_{1}}(r)...K_{n_{j}}(r)P_{n_{1}}(\cos\theta)...P_{n_{j}}(\cos\theta)\;,

(7.41)

where
g_{j}=g_{j}(r)
are defined in Eqs. (
5.50
)–(
5.51
), and
{\cal A}
is given in Eq. (
5.56
). Applying the Wigner decomposition (
E.17
) to the product of Legendre polynomials, we obtain:

\displaystyle\varrho_{l}(r)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{g_{j}}{j!}\frac{k_{jl}}{{\cal A}^{j}}\;,

(7.42)

where the
k_{jl}\equiv\left(K^{j}\right)_{l}
is the
l
-th spectral harmonic of
K^{j}
:

\displaystyle k_{jl}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{\infty}...\sum_{n_{j}=|a_{j-1}-l|}^{a_{j-1}+l}\sum_{a_{2}=|n_{2}-n_{1}|}^{n_{2}+n_{1}}...\sum_{a_{j-1}=|n_{j-1}-a_{j-2}|}^{n_{j-1}+a_{j-1}}T^{n_{1}n_{2}a_{2}}...T^{a_{j-1}n_{j}l}K_{n_{1}}(r)...K_{n_{j}}(r)\;.

(7.43)

The first few harmonics are:

\displaystyle k_{1l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{l}(r)\;,

(7.44)

\displaystyle k_{2l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}K_{n}(r)K_{m}(r)\;,

(7.45)

\displaystyle k_{3l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{p=|n-m|}^{n+m}\sum_{q=|l-p|}^{l+p}T^{nmp}T^{pql}K_{n}(r)K_{m}(r)K_{q}(r)\;.

(7.46)

Substituting expansions (
7.39
) and (
7.40
) into the field equation (
5.59
) for
\tau=1
, we derive the second-order ODE for
K_{l}(r)
:

\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}_{l}+\frac{2}{r}K^{\prime}_{l}-\frac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}K_{l}+4\pi G\frac{K_{l}}{A}+4\pi G\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{g_{j}}{j!}\frac{k_{jl}}{A^{j}}=0\;.

(7.47)

This nonlinear equation can be solved iteratively. For the first three harmonics, we obtain:

\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}_{0}+\frac{2K^{\prime}_{0}}{r}+4\pi G\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}K_{0}+\frac{2\pi G}{5}\left(\sigma^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2\sigma^{\prime}}{r}+4\pi G\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}\right)\left(\frac{K_{2}}{U^{\prime}}\right)^{2}=0\;,

(7.48)

\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}_{2}+\frac{2K^{\prime}_{2}}{r}-\frac{6K_{2}}{r^{2}}+4\pi G\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}K_{2}+\frac{4\pi G}{7}\left(\sigma^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2\sigma^{\prime}}{r}+4\pi G\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}\right)\left(\frac{K_{2}}{U^{\prime}}\right)^{2}=0\;,

(7.49)

\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime}_{4}+\frac{2K^{\prime}_{4}}{r}-\frac{20K_{4}}{r^{2}}+4\pi G\frac{\sigma^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}K_{4}+\frac{36\pi G}{35}\left(\sigma^{\prime\prime}+\frac{2\sigma^{\prime}}{r}+4\pi G\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{U^{\prime}}\right)\left(\frac{K_{2}}{U^{\prime}}\right)^{2}=0\;.

(7.50)

These equations are fully consistent with Eqs. (
7.35
)–(
7.37
), which govern the spectral harmonics
f_{0}
,
f_{2}
, and
f_{4}
of the shape function
f
. Their equivalence follows from Eq. (
6.19
), which relates
K
to
f
. For the first three harmonics:

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-U^{\prime}X_{0}+\frac{1}{5}U^{\prime}X_{2}X^{\prime}_{2}+\frac{1}{10}U^{\prime\prime}X_{2}^{2}\;

(7.51)

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-U^{\prime}X_{2}+\frac{2}{7}U^{\prime}X_{2}X^{\prime}_{2}+\frac{1}{7}U^{\prime\prime}X_{2}^{2}\;

(7.52)

\displaystyle K_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-U^{\prime}X_{4}+\frac{18}{35}U^{\prime}X_{2}X^{\prime}_{2}+\frac{9}{35}U^{\prime\prime}X_{2}^{2}\;,

(7.53)

where
X_{l}=rf_{l}
are the radial spectral harmonics of the height function
X
. These expressions are truncated at second order in
\mathsf{m}
. Substituting Eqs. (
7.51
)–(
7.53
) into Eqs. (
7.48
)–(
7.50
), and using Eqs. (
2.28
)–(
2.30
) to eliminate higher derivatives of
U
, we recover Eqs. (
7.35
)–(
7.37
).

This procedure confirms the equivalence between the gravitational harmonics
K_{l}
governed by Eq. (
7.47
) and the shape function harmonics
f_{l}
governed by Eq. (
7.29
), for any order
l\geq 6
.

7.5
Spectral Analysis of External Gravitational Perturbations Using Multipole Moments

Outside the body, the density and its derivatives vanish. Consequently, Eq. (
5.28
) reduces to the Laplace equation for
K^{+}({\bm{x}})
, which appears in the matching conditions (
8.16
) and (
8.17
):

\displaystyle\Delta K^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(7.54)

This equation is solved in the external domain, which is the vacuum region complementary to the spherical volume
{\mathcal{V}}
occupied by the fluid body. Inside
{\mathcal{V}}
, the internal solution
K^{-}
satisfies the Poisson equation (
5.59
). The external solution
K^{+}({\bm{x}})
is sought in the form of a series expansion in Legendre polynomials:

\displaystyle K^{+}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}K^{+}_{l}(r)P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;.

(7.55)

Substituting this expansion into Eq. (
7.54
) yields the following ordinary differential equation for each spectral harmonic
K^{+}_{l}(r)
:

\displaystyle K^{\prime\prime+}_{l}+\frac{2}{r}K^{\prime+}_{l}-\frac{l(l+1)}{r^{2}}K_{l}^{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(7.56)

The general solution to this equation is a linear combination of two radial power-law terms:

\displaystyle K_{l}^{+}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{l}\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{l+1}+\mathfrak{B}_{l}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{l}\;,

(7.57)

where
\mathfrak{A}_{l}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{l}
are constants specific to each harmonic degree
l
. Differentiating Eq. (
7.57
) with respect to
r
gives:

\displaystyle K^{\prime+}_{l}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(l+1)\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{l}}{a}\left(\frac{a}{r}\right)^{l+2}+l\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{l}}{a}\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{l-1}\;.

(7.58)

The coefficients
\mathfrak{A}_{l}
characterize the perturbation of the spherically symmetric gravitational field of the fluid body and are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{G{\cal M}}{a}{\cal I}_{l}\;,

(7.59)

where
\mathcal{I}_{l}
are the gravitational coefficients of the zonal harmonics induced by the external rotational perturbation
[
2
]
, normalized to the unperturbed radius
a
of the body. In satellite dynamics, the multipole moments
J_{l}
, normalized to the equatorial radius
R_{\rm e}
, are typically used. The equatorial radius is obtained from Eq. (
7.2
) evaluated at
\theta=\pi/2
, and expressed in terms of the shape function
f(a)=X/a
as:

\displaystyle\frac{R_{\rm e}}{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}(2k-1)!!}{2^{k}k!}\frac{X_{2k}(a)}{a}=1+f_{0}(a)-\frac{1}{2}f_{2}(a)+\frac{3}{8}f_{4}(a)+\ldots\;.

(7.60)

The relationship between the multipole moments
\mathcal{I}_{l}
and
J_{l}
is given by:

\displaystyle{\cal I}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{R_{\rm e}}{a}\right)^{l}J_{l}\;.

(7.61)

The coefficients
\mathfrak{A}_{l}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{l}
are related through the classical definition of the Love numbers
k_{l}
, widely used in geodesy and geophysics
[
111
,
78
,
2
]
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{l}\mathfrak{B}_{l}\;.

(7.62)

The Love numbers
k_{l}
quantify the integrated response of the body’s material to external rotational perturbations. The coefficients
\mathfrak{B}_{l}
in Eq. (
7.57
) represent the amplitudes of the spectral harmonics of the external perturbing potential
W_{Q}
, defined in Eq. (
2.16
), and are given by:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\frac{G{\cal M}}{a}\delta_{2l}\;,

(7.63)

where
\delta_{2l}
is the Kronecker delta. Thus, only the quadrupole term
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
is non-zero under purely rotational perturbations. Accordingly, the relation (
7.62
) between
\mathfrak{A}_{l}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{l}
holds exactly for
l=2
in the linear approximation with respect to the perturbation parameter
\mathsf{m}
, implying
\mathfrak{A}_{l}=0
for
l>2
at this order. However, at higher orders in
\mathsf{m}
, the coefficients
\mathfrak{A}_{l}\sim\mathsf{m}^{2}
become non-zero and are determined by boundary conditions imposed on the radial harmonics of the height function. This will be elaborated in the subsequent section.

By extending Eq. (
7.62
) to higher-order harmonics, a generalized correspondence between the gravitational multipole moments
J_{l}
and the Love numbers
k_{l}
is established:

\displaystyle{\cal I}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}k_{l}\;.

(7.64)

It is important to note that the fluid Love number
k_{l}
is twice the apsidal motion constant
k^{\rm aps}_{l}
, as originally introduced by Kopal
[
112
]
and further developed by Brooker and Olle
[
113
]
, i.e.,
k_{l}=2k^{\rm aps}_{l}
. In astrophysical literature
[
19
,
21
,
20
,
114
]
, the notation for the apsidal motion constant is often conflated with that of the Love number
k_{l}
used in geodesy
[
78
]
, geophysics
[
2
]
, and planetary science
[
115
,
116
]
. This overlap in terminology can lead to confusion, particularly due to the factor-of-two difference. In this work, we adopt the classical definition of the Love number
k_{l}
as originally formulated by Love
[
111
]
and consistently used in geophysical and planetary science contexts.

8
Boundary Conditions and Love Numbers

The determination of solutions to the differential equations governing the height function, denoted by
X({\bm{x}})=X(r,\theta)
and the gravitational field perturbation,
K({\bm{x}})=K(r,\theta)
, relies critically on the application of appropriate boundary conditions. These functions are not independent; rather, they are coupled through a transformation relationship as indicated by Eq. (
6.19
). This coupling implies that the behavior of one function at the boundaries directly influences the behavior of the other. Consequently, the boundary conditions imposed on
X({\bm{x}})
and
K({\bm{x}})
must be consistent with their interdependence, ensuring that the physical and mathematical constraints of the rotating fluid body are satisfied simultaneously. These boundary conditions typically reflect physical requirements such as regularity at the origin, continuity across interfaces, or decay at infinity, and they play a crucial role in selecting a physically meaningful solution from the general solution space of the differential equations.

8.1
Boundary Conditions for Gravitational Perturbations

The gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
is assumed to be finite and continuous throughout the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The boundary condition at the center of the fluid body is derived from the transformation relation in Eq. (
6.19
), which links
K({\bm{x}})
to the height function
X({\bm{x}})
– a measure of the fluid surface deformation. For physical consistency,
X({\bm{x}})
must remain finite and continuous across the domain, and we specifically impose the condition
X(0)=0
at the center.

This condition is physically motivated. A nonzero value of
X(0)
would imply a displacement of the central fluid parcel in response to the external perturbing force
{\bm{F}}=-\rho{\bm{\nabla}}W_{Q}
, where
W_{Q}
is the rotational perturbation potential defined in Eq. (
2.11
). However, since
{\bm{\nabla}}W_{Q}=0
at the origin, the force vanishes at the center, indicating that the central fluid element experiences no net acceleration. Therefore, any displacement of the fluid parcel at this point would contradict the absence of force. As a result, the condition
X(0)=0
, together with Eq. (
6.19
), necessitates that the gravitational perturbation also vanishes at the center:
K(0)=0
.

Additional boundary conditions for
K({\bm{x}})
are determined by analyzing its behavior at the outer boundary of the fluid body. The physical boundary is axisymmetric and described by the function
R=r+X(r,\theta)
, where
X(r,\theta)
is the unknown height function. This complicates the direct imposition of boundary conditions on
K({\bm{x}})
at the deformed surface. To address this, we employ the pullback transformation of the integral representation of gravitational potential, as discussed in Section
5.2
. This transformation allows us to treat the boundary of the rotating fluid as a spherical surface of radius
a
, enclosing a volume
\mathcal{V}
with fluid density
\sigma({\bm{x}})+\varrho
, and a surface-layer density
\nu({\bm{x}})
defined on the spherical boundary
\partial\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{S}^{2}
of the reference configuration.

Let
K^{-}({\bm{x}})=K^{-}(r,\theta)
denote the interior solution valid for
r<a
, and
K^{+}({\bm{x}})=K^{+}(r,\theta)
the exterior solution valid for
r>a
. The first boundary condition enforces continuity of the gravitational perturbation across the boundary:

\displaystyle K^{+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K^{-}({\bm{a}})\;.

(8.1)

The second boundary condition concerns the radial derivative of
K({\bm{x}})
. It is obtained by integrating the field equation (
5.28
) over a thin cylindrical shell centered on the boundary and taking the limit as the shell thickness tends to zero. This analysis is performed in the radial gauge
{\bm{X}}=X({\bm{x}})\mathbf{n}
, as defined in Section
4.3
. The resulting jump condition, which accounts for a surface layer with a Dirac delta-function contribution, is:

\displaystyle K^{\prime+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K^{\prime-}({\bm{a}})-4\pi G\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}({\bm{a}})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\Bigl[\rho(a)+\varrho({\bm{a}})\Bigr]\;.

(8.2)

Here,
\rho(a)
is the unperturbed fluid density at the boundary radius
r=a
, and
\varrho(\mathbf{a})
denotes the surface-layer density. The latter is defined in terms of the limiting behavior of the height function as:

\displaystyle\varrho({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{{\bm{x}}\to{\bm{a}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{k}\sigma(r)\;.

(8.3)

These conditions fully characterize the behavior of the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
at the boundary.

Equation (
8.2
) presents the most general boundary condition for the radial derivative of the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
, explicitly accounting for a nonzero surface density
\rho(a)
. In many astrophysical contexts, however, the surface density is negligible relative to the mean interior density
\bar{\rho}(a)
and can be omitted. This simplification is particularly valid for fluid bodies described by a polytropic equation of state with index
0\leq n<5
, as given in Eq. (
2.42
) and discussed in
[
14
]
.

Nevertheless, even when
\rho(a)=0
, higher-order derivatives of the density may remain finite at the surface. A notable example is the unit-index polytrope, analyzed in Section
9.2
, where such terms contribute significantly. Therefore, the second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
8.2
) must be carefully evaluated and retained when these higher-order derivative effects are non-negligible.

8.2
Boundary Conditions for the Height Function

The behavior of the height function
X({\bm{x}})
at the center of the body is utilized to impose a constraint on the gravitational field perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
at the origin,
{\bm{x}}=0
. Conversely, the matching conditions (
8.1
) and (
8.2
), applied to the gravitational perturbation
K({\bm{x}})
and its normal derivative
K^{\prime}({\bm{x}})
on the spherical boundary of the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, are employed to derive the corresponding boundary conditions for the height function
X({\bm{x}})
and its radial derivative
X^{\prime}({\bm{x}})
at the surface
r=a
.

Within the volume
{\mathcal{V}}
occupied by the base manifold
{\mathfrak{M}}
, the interior gravitational perturbation
K^{-}({\bm{x}})
is governed by equation (
6.19
). Substituting the continuity condition (
8.1
) into this expression yields an explicit formula that determines the behavior of the height function
X({\bm{x}})
and its radial derivatives on the boundary surface of the body.

\displaystyle K^{+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}U(a)\;,

(8.4)

where
{\bm{X}}=X{\bm{n}}
,
X=X({\bm{a}})
, and
U(a)
denotes the unperturbed effective gravitational potential on the reference surface
r=a
, as given in (
2.31
).

The second boundary condition, which involves the height function
X({\bm{x}})
and its radial derivatives, is derived from equation (
6.38
). By incorporating the expression for the potential
V
from equation (
6.13
), this condition can be written in the following form:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{i}K^{-}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})\partial_{j}U({\bm{x}})-\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)\partial_{i}U({\bm{x}})\;.

(8.5)

Multiplying both sides of this equation on the left by the unit normal vector
n^{i}
, and noting that in the radial gauge the vector commutes with the shift operator – i.e.,
n^{i}\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}=\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}n^{i}
– we obtain:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)K^{\prime-}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{i}M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})\partial_{j}U({\bm{x}})-\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)U^{\prime}({\bm{x}})\;,

(8.6)

where the prime denotes differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate.

The first term on the right-hand side of (
8.6
) is evaluated using equation (
D.12
):

\displaystyle n^{i}M^{-1}_{ij}({\bm{X}})\partial_{j}U({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{U^{\prime}({\bm{x}})}{1+X^{\prime}({\bm{x}})}\;.

(8.7)

Substituting this into (
8.6
) yields:

\displaystyle\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)K^{\prime-}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{U^{\prime}({\bm{x}})}{1+X^{\prime}({\bm{x}})}-\left(1+\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)U^{\prime}({\bm{x}})\;.

(8.8)

The Neumann series solution of this equation is:

\displaystyle K^{\prime-}({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}\left(\frac{U^{\prime}({\bm{x}})}{1+X^{\prime}({\bm{x}})}\right)-U^{\prime}({\bm{x}})\;.

(8.9)

Expanding the fraction in a Taylor series with respect to
X^{\prime}
and applying the boundary condition (
8.2
) for the radial derivative of the gravitational perturbation, we derive the second boundary condition for the height function:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptK^{\prime+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}U^{\prime}(a)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}\biggl[(-X^{\prime})^{k}U^{\prime}(a)\biggr]

(8.10)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 4\pi G\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\left[\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{{\bm{X}}}\right)^{k}\rho(a)\right]\;,

where
{\bm{X}}=X{\bm{n}}
,
X=X({\bm{a}})
.

The boundary conditions (
8.4
) and (
8.10
) are exact and expressed as infinite series in powers of
X
,
X^{\prime}
, and higher-order radial derivatives. However, due to their complexity, they are not directly solvable and must be approached via successive approximations. In this work, we adopt a quadratic approximation, assuming the height function is small compared to the body’s radius,
X\simeq\mathsf{m}a\ll a
. Accordingly, we neglect terms of order
{\cal O}(\mathsf{m}X^{2})
and
{\cal O}(X^{3})
.

Expanding equations (
8.4
) and (
8.10
) in powers of
X
and retaining terms up to second order, we obtain the quadratic boundary conditions:

\displaystyle K^{+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-X+XX^{\prime}\right)U^{\prime}(a)+\frac{1}{2}X^{2}U^{\prime\prime}(a)\;,

(8.11)

\displaystyle K^{\prime+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-X^{\prime}+X^{\prime 2}+XX^{\prime\prime}\right)U^{\prime}(a)+\left(-X+2XX^{\prime}\right)U^{\prime\prime}(a)+\frac{1}{2}X^{2}U^{\prime\prime\prime}(a)

(8.12)

\displaystyle\;-4\pi G\left[\rho(a)X-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\prime}(a)X^{2}\right]\;,

where all functions and their derivatives are evaluated at
r=a
.

The radial derivatives of the gravitational potential
U
at the boundary surface
r=a
are computed using equations (
2.32
)–(
2.34
). By substituting the explicit expression for the Newtonian potential,
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(a)=-G{\cal M}/a^{2}
, these derivatives are evaluated as follows (cf. Eqs. (
2.32
)–(
2.34
)):

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)\;,

(8.13)

\displaystyle U^{\prime\prime}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2G{\cal M}}{a^{3}}\left(1+\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)-4\pi G\rho(a)\;,

(8.14)

\displaystyle U^{\prime\prime\prime}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{6G{\cal M}}{a^{4}}+\frac{8\pi G\rho(a)}{a}-4\pi G\rho^{\prime}(a)\;.

(8.15)

For simplicity, we assume that the surface density of the rotating fluid body vanishes,
\rho(a)=0
, a condition commonly satisfied in astrophysical contexts though not necessarily in geophysical applications. Under this assumption, all terms involving
\rho(a)
are eliminated. Substituting equations (
8.13
)–(
8.15
) into the boundary conditions (
8.11
) and (
8.12
), and noting that terms involving
\rho^{\prime}(a)
cancel, we obtain the simplified quadratic boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055ptK^{+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(X-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}X-XX^{\prime}+\frac{X^{2}}{a}\right)\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}\;,

(8.16)

\displaystyle K^{\prime+}({\bm{a}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(X^{\prime}-2\frac{X}{a}-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}X^{\prime}-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\frac{X}{a}-X^{\prime 2}-XX^{\prime\prime}+\frac{4XX^{\prime}}{a}-\frac{3X^{2}}{a^{2}}\right)\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}\;.

(8.17)

These boundary conditions are further developed in the subsequent sections, where they are applied to the radial harmonic components of the height and shape functions.

8.3
Boundary Conditions for Radial Harmonics of the Height Function

The boundary conditions for the radial spectral harmonics
X_{l}({\bm{x}})
of the height function
X({\bm{x}})
are obtained by decomposing both sides of equations (
8.16
) and (
8.17
) into series of Legendre polynomials and equating terms of identical spectral order.

The harmonic decomposition of the left-hand sides is straightforward and follows directly from equation (
7.55
). The right-hand sides are expanded using equation (
7.2
), along with the Wigner decomposition for products of the height function and its derivatives. Matching terms of the same spectral index yields:

\displaystyle K_{l}^{+}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(X_{l}-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}X_{l}\right)\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}-\frac{X_{n}X_{m}}{a}\right]\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}\;,

(8.18)

\displaystyle{K^{\prime}_{l}}^{+}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)X^{\prime}_{l}-\left(1+\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)\frac{2X_{l}}{a}\right]\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}

(8.19)

\displaystyle-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X_{(n}^{\prime\prime}X_{m)}+X^{\prime}_{n}X^{\prime}_{m}-\frac{4X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}}{a}+\frac{3X_{n}X_{m}}{a^{2}}\right]\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}\;,

where all quantities are evaluated at the boundary
r=a
, and parentheses around indices denote full symmetrization.

The second radial derivative
X^{\prime\prime}_{n}
appearing in (
8.19
) can be simplified using the Clairaut equation for the height function evaluated at the boundary, where
\upbeta=0
due to the assumption
\rho(a)=0
. This yields:

\displaystyle X_{n}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2X_{n}^{\prime}}{a}+\frac{(n+2)(n-1)X_{n}}{a^{2}}\;.

(8.20)

Substituting (
8.20
) into (
8.19
) gives the final form of the boundary condition:

\displaystyle{K^{\prime}_{l}}^{+}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)X^{\prime}_{l}-\left(1+\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)\frac{2X_{l}}{a}\right]\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}

(8.21)

\displaystyle-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X^{\prime}_{n}X^{\prime}_{m}-\frac{2X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}}{a}+\left(n^{2}+n+1\right)\frac{X_{n}X_{m}}{a^{2}}\right]\frac{G{\cal M}}{a^{2}}\;.

Next, we substitute the expressions for
K_{l}^{+}(a)
and
{K^{\prime}_{l}}^{+}(a)
from equations (
7.57
) and (
7.58
) into the left-hand sides of (
8.18
) and (
8.21
), which contain the unknown coefficients
\mathfrak{A}_{l}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{l}
, and the boundary values of
X_{l}(a)
and
X^{\prime}_{l}(a)
.

Using the definitions of the multipole moments
\mathfrak{A}_{l}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{l}
from equations (
7.62
) and (
7.63
), and canceling like terms, the boundary conditions (
8.18
) and (
8.21
) reduce to:

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)X_{l}-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}-\frac{X_{n}X_{m}}{a}\right]=-\frac{\mathsf{m}a}{3}\biggl(k_{l}+\delta_{2l}\biggr)\;,

(8.22)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)X^{\prime}_{l}-\left(1+\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)\frac{2X_{l}}{a}-

(8.23)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X^{\prime}_{n}X^{\prime}_{m}-\frac{2X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}}{a}+(n^{2}+n+1)\frac{X_{n}X_{m}}{a^{2}}\right]=\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\biggl[(l+1)k_{l}-l\delta_{2l}\biggr]\;.

Solving this system yields the boundary condition for the radial harmonics of the height function:

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)X^{\prime}_{l}+\left[l-1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}(l+2)\right]\frac{X_{l}}{a}-

(8.24)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X^{\prime}_{n}X^{\prime}_{m}+(l-1)\frac{X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}}{a}+(n^{2}+n-l)\frac{X_{n}X_{m}}{a^{2}}\right]=-\frac{2l+1}{3}\mathsf{m}\delta_{2l}\;.

An alternative solution to the system (
8.22
)–(
8.23
) expresses the Love number
k_{l}
in terms of the radial harmonics of the height function and their derivatives:

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)X^{\prime}_{l}-\left[l+2-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}(l-1)\right]\frac{X_{l}}{a}-

(8.25)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[X^{\prime}_{n}X^{\prime}_{m}-(l+2)\frac{X_{(n}X^{\prime}_{m)}}{a}+(n^{2}+n+l+1)\frac{X_{n}X_{m}}{a^{2}}\right]=\frac{2l+1}{3}\mathsf{m}k_{l}\;.

A detailed discussion of the Love numbers and their physical interpretation is presented in Section
8.5
.

8.4
Boundary Conditions for Radial Harmonics of the Shape Function

It is instructive to derive the boundary conditions imposed directly on the radial harmonics
f_{l}=X_{l}/r
of the shape function
f=X/r
, which satisfies the nonlinear differential equation (
7.29
). We focus on the first three harmonics
f_{l}(r)
for
l=0,2,4
, within the frameworks of the Clairaut and Darwin-de Sitter approximations, as discussed in Sections
7.3.1
and
7.3.2
.

These boundary conditions follow from the condition (
8.24
) for the height function harmonics
X_{l}
, by substituting
X_{l}\rightarrow af_{l}
and
X^{\prime}_{l}\rightarrow af^{\prime}_{l}+f_{l}
at the body’s surface. This yields:

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)af^{\prime}_{l}+\left[l-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}(l+3)\right]f_{l}-

(8.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[a^{2}f^{\prime}_{n}f^{\prime}_{m}+a(l+1)f_{(n}f^{\prime}_{m)}+n(n+1)f_{n}f_{m}\right]=-\frac{2l+1}{3}\mathsf{m}\delta_{2l}\;.

The computation of the Wigner matrix elements
T^{nml}
for the specific values of the index
l=0,2,4
yields the boundary conditions governing the radial harmonics of the shape function in a more explicit form. These conditions are expressed as follows:

\displaystyle af^{\prime}_{0}-\frac{1}{5}\left(a^{2}{f^{\prime}_{2}}^{2}+af_{2}f^{\prime}_{2}+6f_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(8.27)

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)af^{\prime}_{2}+\left(2-\frac{10\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)f_{2}-\frac{2}{7}\left(a^{2}{f^{\prime}_{2}}^{2}+3af_{2}f^{\prime}_{2}+6f_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{5\mathsf{m}}{3}\;,

(8.28)

\displaystyle af^{\prime}_{4}+4f_{4}-\frac{18}{35}\left(a^{2}{f^{\prime}_{2}}^{2}+5af_{2}f^{\prime}_{2}+6f_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(8.29)

In deriving these expressions, all terms of order
{\cal O}(\mathsf{m}^{3})
and higher have been neglected, under the assumption that parameter
\mathsf{m}
is sufficiently small for such higher-order contributions to be insignificant.

The boundary conditions given in equations (
8.28
) and (
8.29
) are consistent with those originally derived by Kopal
[
7
]
and later confirmed by Lanzano
[
8
]
. In contrast, Nakiboglu
[
117
]
derived equation (
8.28
) with an error in the numerical coefficient preceding the term proportional to
f_{2}f^{\prime}_{2}
, using a value of
1/2
instead of the correct value of
3
. This error was subsequently propagated in his later work
[
118
]
, and it inadvertently influenced the study by Chambat et al.
[
110
]
, who performed numerical simulations that appeared to validate Nakiboglu’s incorrect coefficient.

Our independent analytical derivation of the boundary condition (
8.26
) fully aligns with the results obtained by Kopal
[
7
]
, Lanzano
[
8
]
, and also with the boundary conditions established by Zharkov and Trubitsyn
[
2
]
. This agreement provides strong evidence that the boundary conditions proposed by Nakiboglu
[
117
]
are erroneous and should not be used in geophysical modeling or interpretation.

The boundary condition (
8.26
) can be further simplified by applying iterative approach. In the first iteration, we neglect all terms which are products of the
f_{l}
, and obtain:

\displaystyle af^{\prime}_{l}+lf_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2l+1}{3}\mathsf{m}\delta_{2l}\;.

(8.30)

This is the boundary condition for the Clairaut equation (
7.31
). This linear condition implies that only the quadrupole harmonic
f_{2}
is excited by the external rotational potential
W_{Q}
(see (
2.16
)), while all others vanish (
f_{l}=0
for
l\neq 2
). Thus, the Clairaut approximation describes a rotating, self-gravitating fluid body as an oblate spheroid, flattened along the axis of rotation.

In the second iteration, we substitute the Clairaut boundary condition (
8.30
) into the quadratic terms on the right-hand side of (
8.26
) to eliminate the radial derivatives
f^{\prime}_{l}
. This yields the boundary conditions for the harmonics
f_{l}
that appear in the Darwin-de Sitter equations (
7.35
)–(
7.37
). We restrict our analysis to the harmonics with
l=0,2,4
, as these are the only ones contributing at order
\mathsf{m}^{2}
in the Darwin-de Sitter approximation. Higher-order harmonics, which arise at
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{m}^{3})
and beyond, lie outside the scope of this approximation. Equation (
8.26
) for these harmonics is equivalent to three conditions:

\displaystyle af_{0}^{\prime}-\mathsf{m}f_{2}-\frac{5}{9}\mathsf{m}^{2}-\frac{8f_{2}^{2}}{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(8.31)

\displaystyle af_{2}^{\prime}+2f_{2}-\frac{52}{21}\mathsf{m}f_{2}+\frac{20}{63}{{\mathsf{q}}^{2}}-\frac{8f_{2}^{2}}{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{5}{3}{{\mathsf{m}}}\;,

(8.32)

\displaystyle af_{4}^{\prime}+4f_{4}+\frac{6}{7}\mathsf{m}f_{2}-\frac{10}{7}\mathsf{m}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(8.33)

The boundary condition (
8.31
) for the monopole harmonic
f_{0}
shows that it becomes nonzero at second order in
\mathsf{m}
. This arises from nonlinear coupling with the quadrupole harmonic
f_{2}
, resulting in an additional, purely radial distortion of the fluid body. This effect supplements the radial distortion caused by the potential
W_{R}
(see Eq. (
2.15
)) and confirms the earlier estimate of the magnitude of
f_{0}\sim\mathsf{m}^{2}
discussed in the final paragraph of Section
7.1
.

Equation (
8.32
) provides a second-order correction to the boundary condition for the quadrupole harmonic
f_{2}
of the shape function. This result is consistent with the boundary condition derived in the Zharkov-Trubitsyn theory, specifically Eq. 33.8 in their monograph
[
2
]
, which expresses the condition in terms of the variables
\hat{e}
and
\hat{\eta}
:

\displaystyle\hat{e}\hat{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}\mathsf{m}-2\hat{e}+\frac{10}{21}\mathsf{m}^{2}+\frac{4}{7}\hat{e}^{2}-\frac{6}{7}\mathsf{m}\hat{e}\;,

(8.34)

which are related to the harmonics
f_{2}
and
f_{4}
of the shape function
f
(Eqs 30.4 and 33.7 from Zharkov-Trubitsyn’s monograph
[
2
]
):

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}f_{2}-\frac{69}{56}f_{2}^{2}-\frac{4}{7}k\;,

(8.35)

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{35}{32}f_{4}-\frac{27}{32}f_{2}^{2}\;,

(8.36)

\displaystyle\hat{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e-\frac{5}{42}e^{2}+\frac{4}{7}k\;,

(8.37)

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\hat{e}}{d\ln r}\;.

(8.38)

Substituting these into the expression for
\hat{e}\hat{\eta}
and retaining terms up to second order in
\mathsf{m}
reproduces Eq. (
8.32
), thereby confirming the equivalence between the two formulations.

The fourth harmonic
f_{4}
of the shape function remains unexcited in the linear Clairaut regime but is induced at second order in the Darwin-de Sitter approximation due to nonlinear self-coupling of the quadrupole harmonic
f_{2}
. As shown, the boundary condition (
8.33
) precisely matches Eq. 33.4 in Zharkov and Trubitsyn’s monograph
[
2
]
, where the function
k
– defined in Eq. (
8.36
) – quantifies the second-order deviation of the boundary surface from the Clairaut ellipsoid.

It follows that higher-order harmonics (
l>4
) will also be excited through similar nonlinear interactions involving
f_{2}
. However, their contributions arise at order
\mathsf{m}^{3}
and beyond, and thus lie outside the scope of the Darwin-de Sitter approximation considered here.

8.5
The Love Numbers and Multipole Moments of Rotating Fluid Bodies

8.5.1
Love Numbers

In case of the fluid body, there exist two dimensionless types of Love numbers
[
111
]
for each spectral harmonic
l\geq 0
, denoted correspondingly as
k_{l}
and
h_{l}
.
The Love numebr
k_{l}
was introduced in Eq. (
7.62
). The second Love number is defined by the following equation:

\displaystyle h_{l}:=-\frac{3}{\mathsf{m}}f_{l}\;,

(8.39)

where the shape function
f_{l}
refers to the surface of the fluid body at
r=a
. The Love numbers
k_{l}
characterize the integral response of the body’s gravitational potential to a perturbing potential
W_{Q}
, while the Love numbers
h_{l}
characterize the susceptibility of its shape to the perturbing potential.

The Love numbers
k_{l}
and
h_{l}
are interconnected. The relashionship between the two Love numbers can be obtained in the most simple way from Eq. (
8.22
) which depends on the height’s function harmonic
X_{l}
and its first derivative
X^{\prime}_{l}
. Replacing in this equation
X_{l}\rightarrow af_{l}
and
X^{\prime}_{l}\rightarrow af^{\prime}_{l}+f_{l}
, and using Eq. (
8.30
) for replacing
f^{\prime}_{l}
, yields:

\displaystyle k_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\mathsf{m}}{3}\right)h_{l}-\delta_{2l}+\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=|n-l|}^{n+l}T^{nml}\left[\delta_{2(n}h_{m)}+nh_{m}\delta_{2n}+mh_{n}\delta_{2m}-\frac{1}{2}(n+m)h_{n}h_{m}\right]\;,

(8.40)

which clearly demonstrates that the two types of Love numbers are intimately related, and not independent. The shape Love number
h_{l}
is more fundamental as it is found by solving equation for the shape function
f_{l}
. As soon as
h_{l}
is known, we can use Eq. (
8.40
) to calculate the Love number
k_{l}
.

For the first three non-vanishing harmonics Eq. (
8.40
) yields

\displaystyle k_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{0}-\;\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}\;\left(2h_{0}-h_{2}+\frac{2}{5}h_{2}^{2}\right)\;,

(8.41)

\displaystyle k_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{2}-\frac{4\mathsf{m}}{21}\left(h_{2}-\frac{5}{2}h_{4}+h_{2}^{2}\right)-1\;,

(8.42)

\displaystyle k_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{4}+\frac{6\mathsf{m}}{7}\left(h_{2}-\frac{3}{11}h_{4}-\frac{2}{5}h_{2}^{2}\right)\;.

(8.43)

The classical theory of Love numbers is typically limited to Clairaut’s approximation and does not account for the higher-order terms in the second approximation of the Darwin-de Sitter theory. These nonlinear terms are crucial for a better understanding of the interior structure of rapidly rotating major planets like Jupiter and Saturn.

8.5.2
Multipole Moments

The multipole moments of a rotating body are derived from the general expression for the gravitational potential, given by Eq. (
5.22
), evaluated at the parameter value
\tau=1
:

\displaystyle\Phi({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\int_{\cal V}\frac{\mu({\bm{x}}^{\prime})d^{3}x^{\prime}}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|}+G\oint_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}d^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}}_{a})\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}({\bm{a}})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\left[\frac{a^{2}\mu({\bm{a}})}{|{\bm{x}}-{\bm{a}}|}\right]\;,

(8.44)

where the perturbed mass density
\mu({\bm{x}})
is expressed as

\displaystyle\mu({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(r)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(-\hat{\mathsf{T}}_{\bm{X}}\right)^{n}\rho(r)\;,

(8.45)

representing a linear combination of the unperturbed density
\rho(r)
and the perturbation
\varrho({\bm{x}})
defined in Eq. (
6.25
).

To extract the multipole moments, we expand the gravitational potential in Eq. (
8.44
) in terms of Legendre polynomials at a field point
{\bm{x}}
located at a distance
r>a
:

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{G{\cal M}_{l}}{r^{l+1}}P_{l}(\cos\theta)\;,

(8.46)

where the coefficients
\mathcal{M}_{l}
are, by definition, the gravitational multipole moments:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{V}}\mu({\bm{x}})r^{l}P_{l}(\cos\theta)d^{3}x+\oint_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}d^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}}_{a})P_{l}\left(\cos\theta_{a}\right)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{n+1}({\bm{a}})}{(n+1)!}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial a^{n}}\biggl[\mu({\bm{a}})a^{l+2}\biggr]\;.

(8.47)

These multipole moments are related to the zonal harmonic coefficients
\mathcal{I}_{l}
and
J_{l}
, introduced in Eqs. (
7.59
) and (
7.61
), via the relations:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\cal M}a^{l}{\cal I}_{l}=-{\cal M}R^{l}_{\rm e}J_{l}\;.

(8.48)

Furthermore, the Love numbers
k_{l}
are connected to the multipole moments through the expression (see Eq. (
7.64
)):

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}{\cal M}a^{l}k_{l}\;.

(8.49)

Thus, empirical determination of the multipole moments
\mathcal{M}_{l}
provides direct insight into the Love numbers, which characterize the body’s elastic response to external gravitational forces. In particular, by combining the results presented in Eqs. (
8.42
) and (
8.49
), we obtain a generalized form of the classical Clairaut’s theorem:

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-f_{2}-\frac{\mathsf{m}}{3}-\frac{\mathsf{m}}{7}f_{2}-\frac{11}{7}f^{2}_{2}\;,

(8.50)

which expresses the second zonal harmonic
J_{2}
in terms of the body’s geometric flattening
f_{2}
and its angular rotation rate
\sim\mathsf{m}
. This relation extends Clairaut’s original formulation by incorporating higher-order corrections and rotational effects.

The dependence of the multipole moments in Eq. (
8.47
) on the height function
X({\bm{x}})
is nontrivial. To facilitate further analysis, we adopt the quadratic approximation employed in the preceding section. Under this approximation, the perturbed density becomes:

\displaystyle\mu({\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho+\left(-X+XX^{\prime}\right)\rho^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}X^{2}\rho^{\prime\prime}+\ldots\;.

(8.51)

Substituting this into Eq. (
8.47
) and retaining terms up to second order yields:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{V}}\left[\rho+\left(-X+XX^{\prime}\right)\rho^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}X^{2}\rho^{\prime\prime}\right]r^{l+2}P_{l}(\cos\theta)drd^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}})

(8.52)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\oint_{{\mathbb{S}}^{2}}d^{2}\Omega({\bm{n}}_{a})P_{l}\left(\cos\theta_{a}\right)Xa^{l+1}\biggl[a\rho+\left(1+\frac{l}{2}\right)\rho X-\frac{1}{2}aX\rho^{\prime}\biggr]\;.

By integrating the volume term by parts, surface contributions cancel, simplifying the expression to:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{l}

\displaystyle=
